# Libya — English Grade 9 (Preparatory 3): Teacher Book

**READ COMPLETE 2026-10-25** — read in full from page
renders (`teacher9-pages/`, all 162 pages at 100 dpi); the
PDF's text layer is the repeated Arabic rights footer only.
**Page rule: book p. = PDF p. − 1** (the Introduction opens
on book p.6 = PDF p.7; the back cover is book p.161).

## Identity

"21st Century English for Libya — Preparatory 3 Teacher's
Book", State of Libya, Ministry of Education, Curricula and
Educational Research Centre. **Published by Garnet
Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading
RG1 4QS, UK — "First edition, copyright © 2019 Garnet
Publishing Ltd"** (this companion carries the same 2019
year as its Course Book; the Grade 8 Teacher Book was a
year earlier, © 2018). No individual authors named. Back
cover: components = **Course Book, Workbook, Teacher's
Book, Audio**; "For more resources, visit:
www.englishforlibya.com"; code **11000901**. Cover: a
black-and-white photograph of a domed Libyan building at
night. The Contents (book p.4) lists only Introduction
(p.6) and the eight units (U1 p.16 · U2 p.38 · U3 p.58 ·
U4 p.76 · U5 p.88 · U6 p.108 · U7 p.130 · U8 p.150) — the
**Book Map** spreads (book pp.4–5) and the closing
**Phonetic Chart** (book p.160) are not listed, the same
unlisted-back-matter habit as the Course Book's Phonetic
Symbols page.

## Shape of the notes

Each unit opens with its four strands (Listening and
speaking · Reading and writing · Structure and language ·
Vocabulary), its 21st Century Skills, and a **Key words**
page with phonetic transcriptions. Then one note-block per
lesson: an Objectives / Language / Vocabulary / 21st
Century Skills / Reference box (Course Book and Workbook
pages, track numbers), a timed Warm up, and a timed walk
through every Course Book exercise and every Workbook
exercise, with **the full audio scripts printed inline**
and **every answer key**. Lessons are 45 minutes. The
audio runs to **Track 53**, matching the Course Book's
Transcripts exactly — no extra or missing tracks.

## The Introduction (book pp.6–14)

The same architecture as Grade 8's, reworded for
Preparatory 3 and worth filing where it speaks for this
year:

- **Overview**: the course "integrates a general English
  syllabus with an element of 21st century skills, such as
  study skills, collaboration, critical thinking and
  problem-solving"; designed for students "starting to
  become young adults"; topics draw on their knowledge of
  the world and personal interests; emphasis on English as
  an international language and students' long-term as well
  as short-term goals.
- **Approach**: communicative, with the overt analysis of
  structure carried over from Preparatory 2 for the same
  three stated reasons (cognitive ability at this age;
  language needs constant recycling; examination
  constraints mean students must demonstrate both knowledge
  of the language and ability to use it). "Within this
  approach to learning, mistakes should be seen as part of
  the developmental process." The Round up lesson is the
  teacher's reminder for class-based summaries of progress.
- **Self-study strand** kept: students work through
  activities to arrive at their own answers and conclusions,
  then discuss and correct them with a partner, group or
  whole class "as part of the learning process."
- **Components**: Course Book · Workbook · **Test booklet**
  — named again here, with its full task list (listening to
  a conversation and completing notes · reading and
  identifying topic sentences · reordering a conversation ·
  matching sentence halves · completing and correcting
  sentences · matching words to pictures and definitions ·
  completing an application form · writing an email) — and
  **again NOT in the grade-09 library record**; the four
  components on the back cover do not include it either.
  Plus the **Website** (www.englishforlibya.com, downloadable
  audio MP3 files and learning resources).
- **Tests**: students are assessed on listening, reading and
  writing skills, "not their ability to understand written
  instructions"; the teacher keeps a written record of each
  student's performance, usefully broken down into sections
  (reading, listening, speaking, vocabulary, writing); "The
  actual allocation of marks is less important than the
  consistency of marking"; praise students whose results
  improve over time, "not just those who consistently
  achieve well."
- **Organization**: eight units; Units 1–3 and 5–7 present
  new language on a topic basis; Units 4 and 8 (Fun with
  English) recycle the previous three units.
- **Features**: Functional grammar and language tips in
  shaded boxes · Round up pages at the end of Units 1, 2,
  3, 5, 6 and 7 · Homework activities — the Workbook
  "contains a number of projects and extended writing
  activities" the teacher may set as independent study ·
  Vocabulary notebooks (word maps and pictures instead of
  Arabic translations; students find their own method) ·
  Using a dictionary (monolingual dictionaries; phonemic
  script taught as a pronunciation aid; "All the symbols
  that represent vowel sounds in English are presented by
  the end of Preparatory 3") · an Icon key (Pairwork ·
  Group work · Project work · Grammar tips · 21st Century
  Skills · Suggested timing).
- **§6 Advice to teachers of teenagers**: characteristics
  (i) clearer personal goals — tap into the positive
  aspects and shape them "for their long-term benefit and
  the benefit of society"; (ii) firmer opinions — teachers
  need to understand and acknowledge them while introducing
  alternative ways of thinking; (iii) greater interest in
  the outside world; (iv) importance of peer pressure —
  identify influential students "so that their leadership
  qualities can be used for the good of the class";
  educational characteristics (v) understanding of abstract
  concepts, (vi) critical thinking, (vii) a more reflective
  attitude to information; then lesson planning (plan blocks
  of lessons, e.g. a week's classes; timings are guidelines;
  plan ahead for classroom language, board use, layout and
  realia), recycling language (the six revisable strands:
  social language and question tags — *Really? That's nice.
  Was it?* · asking for and giving opinions · describing
  location and giving directions · adjectives to describe
  experiences and places — *What was it like?* · a range of
  tenses · talking about timetables), organizing pairwork
  and group work (draw an imaginary 'line' down the room or
  give each student a letter A or B; mixed-ability groups;
  extra work or a game ready for early finishers), and
  **§6.5 Continuous assessment** — written tests per unit
  plus assessment inside normal lessons; "It is important
  to assess a student's effort, not just the results that
  he/she achieves"; written work collected in Workbooks can
  be graded, "but this should be carried out sensitively" —
  and **§6.6 Homework**: correction of independent work
  "should promote positive reinforcement at the same time
  as giving constructive criticism"; highlight mistakes and
  let students work out how to correct them; "Self-correction
  is a valuable skill that needs to be developed if the
  students are to improve the quality of their written work."
- **§7 Teaching in the Preparatory levels**: the four
  skills with their sub-skills. Listening types: words and
  phrases · conversations · descriptions · narratives or
  interviews · discussions; "The main aim of the tasks is
  to develop students' listening ability, not to test it."
  Speaking competencies for Preparatory 3 include
  understanding formal/informal differences, making and
  responding to suggestions, interrupting politely, showing
  interest, and "giving simple explanations". Writing:
  **each presentation unit contains six writing tasks —
  short tasks (50–70 words) and one longer end-of-unit task
  (80–120 words)**; text types produced at this level:
  informal messages and postcards · letters/emails · factual
  texts · descriptions · stories and extracts from stories ·
  instructions · reports · questionnaires; students "should
  also be encouraged to refer to the Checklist for Written
  Work on page 100 of the Course Book"; stages and sub-skills
  of writing listed in full (brainstorming · selecting and
  rejecting ideas · arranging for impact · sequencing ·
  layout · proofreading · editing · writing legibly).

## What the keys settle

- **The magazine has eleven sections** (U1 L1 key) —
  Cartoons, Interviews, Jokes, Letters, Poetry, Problem
  page, Puzzles, School news, Sports reports, Stories,
  Town events. The Course Book filing's "twelve" is
  corrected there. The girls' team (Track 1, printed in
  full): Maysam does the problem page, Sarah the interview,
  Nadia the story, Rasha the TV review, **Muna the School
  news and Sally — "fantastic at English" — the Sports
  news** (match key: Maysam c · Sarah d · Nadia f · Rasha b ·
  Muna e · Sally a).
- **The boys' magazine** (Track 2, in full): Mazin becomes
  editor ("he's really good at telling people what to do"),
  Layth and Usama reporters, Tariq photographer ("You have
  a camera, don't you?"); they decide NOT to have all the
  girls' sections — "There are too many" — and Mazin wants
  some articles about things outside the school.
- **The ice-creams were five** (U3 L8 key): "Five (one for
  himself and one each for Bashir, Paul, Hans and Rod)" —
  corrected in the Course Book filing.
- **Track 8's boy is Jameel here too** — the Teacher Book's
  script agrees with the Course Book's transcript; only the
  Course Book's lesson pictures say Jamal.
- **The Eggs: whose clothes Bob borrowed** (U2 L8 notes):
  "He borrowed Paul's trousers, Bashir's shirt and Aziz's
  sweater" — the story itself names only countries. And the
  recall key: "They are two boys going to the World Youth
  Camp. They met on the plane in Tripoli. They are in
  Australia (at the World Youth Camp). He lost his suitcase."
- **London 2012** (U3 L5 keys): the five texts are *Women
  at the 2012 Olympic Games · The Jamaican team · The gold
  medals for tennis · The Mexican men's football team ·
  The gold medal for men's long jump*; fastest answers:
  Mexico · American · Serena Williams · The 100 metres ·
  Great Britain; model questions: "Which country won four
  gold, five silver and three bronze medals? (Jamaica.) ·
  When did Mike Powell set his long jump record? (1991.) ·
  Who did Mexico beat in the final of the men's football?
  (Brazil.) · What is Usain Bolt's world record? (100
  metres in 9.63 seconds.) · Who won the men's tennis
  singles? (Andy Murray.)" The years/times/weights reading
  lists are keyed in full (years: eighteen ninety-six ·
  nineteen thirty-six · nineteen forty-eight · nineteen
  eighty-eight · two thousand · two thousand and two · two
  thousand and four · twenty ten/two thousand and ten).
- **Samir's article** (U3 L6 key): what he wrote that was
  NOT in his notes — "He started doing judo two years ago.
  He couldn't run fast and was often ill. He was lazy, but
  now he trains every day. Now he gets good marks all the
  time. He used to be afraid of competitions and exams."
  Topic sentence: "I started doing judo two years ago and
  it has changed my personality and my life." Find-the-word
  key: 1 personality · 2 confidence · 3 train. **Huda's
  changes** (Workbook key): "Huda used to live in Zawiya,
  but now she lives in Misurata. She used to be shy, but
  now she is confident. Huda used to eat lots of ice-cream,
  but now she eats fruit. She used to watch TV a lot, but
  now she does sport." The Mary-and-Jane story built orally
  from the Speaking tip is keyed in full (Jane ran the
  100 metres in 12.5 seconds against Mary's 13.2; Mary
  jumped 1.45 metres against Jane's 1.4; the girls save a
  drowning boy; "Well done, Jane! You were fantastic.").
- **Jalal's Story** (U2 L5 keys): full name **Jalal
  Abdullah**; the story "is about when Jalal's brother
  Ibrahim was unwell"; he did NOT want Ibrahim to play by
  himself, said it again and again, did not eat the crisps,
  Ibrahim had to go to hospital. "You are a pain" is glossed:
  "You give me a pain because you are a nuisance." The
  correction marks are keyed twice (Course Book box: sp. =
  spelling · gr. = grammar · p. = punctuation · n.p. = new
  paragraph; Workbook: join · gr. · p. · sp. · n.p.).
- **The two doctor visits in full** (Track 10, Workbook
  only): *Part 1 — Noora's earache*: drops in the ear, "Two
  drops at night… Use the drops twice a day until your ear
  doesn't hurt any more"; "don't go swimming until it's
  better". *Part 2 — Rasha's headache*: her eyes look red;
  she doesn't like wearing glasses; "you should wear them
  all the time, Rasha. If you don't, you'll have headaches
  all day, every day – and night"; twelve pills in the box,
  "Take two with water, three times a day – today and
  tomorrow"; "Have you got your glasses with you?… Put them
  on now, please, and don't take them off until you go to
  bed." The paragraph completions are keyed (Noora: (an)
  earache · put some drops · twice a few days · not go
  swimming; Rasha: headache · hurt · wear glasses · doesn't
  like wearing them · should wear her glasses all the time ·
  headaches · painkillers/pills · three times a day).
- **The restaurant order in full** (Track 7): Reema — baked
  potato with a little butter, mixed salad, fruit salad and
  a little ice-cream, orange juice ("That sounds lovely");
  Waleed — a large burger and chips, a large piece of
  chocolate cake and a lot of ice-cream, a large cola;
  Father — grilled fish and rice. Meal key: Reema's =
  baked potato with butter, mixed salad, fruit salad,
  ice-cream, orange juice; Waleed's = large burger, chips,
  chocolate cake, ice-cream, cola.
- **Mr Fawzi** (U1 L7 key): "Mr Fawzi thinks the town would
  be improved by having some more parks. He thinks the roads
  and bridges should also be improved." Workbook key for
  the interview: he works at a high school; dislikes doing
  paperwork and sitting at home in the evenings; is learning
  French; question 4's answer is c; item 5 is a programme
  about health.
- **The timetable completed** (U3 L3, Workbook key gives
  the whole grid): Mon 5–7 Tennis (B), Karate (B) · Mon
  W&G 7–9 Tennis (T), Volleyball (B) · Tue 5–7 Basketball
  (B), Judo (B) · Tue M&B 7–9 Gymnastics (B), Volleyball (B)
  · Wed 5–7 Horse-riding (B), Athletics (B) · Wed W&G 7–9
  Gymnastics (B) · Thu 5–7 Athletics (B), Karate (B) · Thu
  M&B 7–9 Swimming (T), Gymnastics (A) · Fri 5–7 Volleyball
  (A) · Fri W&G 7–9 Swimming (T), Gymnastics (A) · Sat
  10–12 Horse-riding (A) · Sat M&B 3–5 Volleyball (A),
  Basketball (T). Track 19's three calls keyed: Wednesday
  7–9 Gymnastics (B) · Thursday 7–9 Swimming (T) · Saturday
  10–12 Horse-riding (A). Track 16's message: separate
  classes for women and girls and for men and boys;
  beginners, advanced and team practice; "leave your name
  and address and we'll send you a leaflet". Track 15's
  advertisement names the centre's number: **952841**.
- **The sports weekend** (Track 26, Workbook only): only
  six classes are scheduled — karate beginners Saturday
  10–12 · karate advanced Sunday 2–4 · tennis advanced
  only, Sunday 5–7 · athletics beginners only, Saturday
  2–4 · gymnastics beginners Saturday 5–7 · gymnastics
  advanced Sunday 10–12 (match key 1 a · 2 e · 6 f · 7 b ·
  11 c · 12 d, the rest blank).
- **Wendy's problem** (Track 27, Workbook only, in full):
  Wendy can't sleep and has a headache every morning; pills
  give her a stomach ache; the teacher finds the cause —
  "a glass of lemonade and a packet of crisps just before
  I go to bed. At about ten o'clock" — and advises milk or
  water instead, no tea, coffee or lemonade, no crisps;
  and since she lives about one kilometre away and her
  father drives her, "You should walk to school. And walk
  home. You have to take some exercise. If you do, I think
  you'll find that your headaches will go away."
- **The football score** (Track 24): "At the end of an
  exciting game at the new National Stadium, the score was
  **Libya 3, Algeria 3**" — a draw, the word taught from it.
  Mr and Mrs Andrews' orders (Track 25) keyed: his — fried
  chicken, rice and salad, chocolate cake, apple juice;
  hers — grilled fish, chips and salad, fruit salad,
  mineral water.
- **Firefighter Mazin's day told in full** (Track 37):
  "When I arrived at the fire station yesterday morning, I
  checked my equipment as usual. We had our first call at
  ten o'clock. We were told there was a fire in a
  supermarket in the middle of town. We drove through the
  streets as fast as possible. When we got there, the fire
  was burning strongly. The heat was terrible and some
  people were trapped inside. We had to get them out
  quickly. We got out our hoses and pointed them at the
  building. Twenty minutes later, the fire was out and we
  were able to go into the building. We saved all the
  people, but some had to go to hospital. At two o'clock,
  we were called to an accident on the ring road. The
  police helped us get there very fast. One car was on fire
  and another was upside down on the road. Fortunately, the
  driver of the burning car was standing by the side of the
  road. However, the driver of the other car was trapped.
  We put the fire out quickly and got the second driver
  out. He was badly injured. At the end of the day, I was
  tired and upset about the accident. But it's all in a
  day's work for a firefighter. We do our best." The notes
  add: "Explain that this paragraph has been added to make
  it a better story" — the feelings ending is the course's
  own addition. Sentence-order key: 1 g · 2 d · 3 a · 4 f ·
  5 e · 6 b · 7 c · 8 j · 9 k · 10 m · 11 i · 12 h · 13 n ·
  14 l.
- **The two jobs compared** (U6 L2 key): the doctor works
  in a hospital, takes care of sick people and makes them
  better, usually works 8 hours (but sometimes 12), studied
  at university for 6 years; the office manager works in
  an office, answers phone calls, sends and receives
  emails, works 6 hours (but sometimes more), studied at
  college for 4 years. The interview that checks them
  (Track 36) is with a **nurse at the Albaraa Hospital** —
  a third job, existing only in the audio: uniform, ten
  hours a day, studied four years, "about forty or fifty
  people every day", does not want to be a doctor.
- **The games' rules in full** (U6 L4): Game A — Student 2
  asks five questions only; a correct guess within five
  wins a point. Game B — Student 1 says five things; after
  each, Student 2 guesses; a guess after five sentences or
  fewer wins a point. Game C — Student 1 says five
  sentences about the job, all together, then Student 2
  guesses once. Track 38 plays two: Miss Hadeel's class
  plays Game A (the job is a policewoman — "Wrong. I'm a
  policewoman"), Mr Kareem's class plays Game C (a pilot,
  guessed only on the last clue, "I work at the airport");
  jobs named across both games: doctor, policewoman,
  farmer, bus driver, pilot, ambulance driver, taxi driver.
- **Six ambitions** (Track 39, Workbook only, in full):
  1. work in my father's restaurant for a year, then
  college, "Perhaps one day I'll have my own restaurant";
  2. work on an aeroplane — "I will show people to their
  seats and give them food and drink" (best subjects
  English and Arabic, learning French); 3. fly planes —
  watches them at the airport in the evenings; 4. teach
  history — training college, "I'm very patient"; 5. help
  sick animals — university first; 6. an office job —
  "writing letters, answering the phone, talking to
  visitors – all those things" (good at Arabic and IT).
- **Firas and Mona** (U6 L6 keys): Firas goes to the zoo,
  Mona visits a hospital; "Firas is going to be a zoologist.
  Mona is going to be a doctor." Firas's text: favourite
  subjects history, geography and art; hobby taking
  photographs of birds and animals; "He is very patient."
  Mona's: biology, chemistry, English and physics; helping
  people; "She is very kind and friendly"; and "The patients
  who have no visitors."
- **The bring-and-buy sale** (Tracks 42–44): Sarah's
  friends decide to hold the sale in the playground on
  Monday at four o'clock, after asking the headmistress;
  offers keyed — Suhad a camera · Wisal some books · Widad
  a doll; the model email (Workbook, from **Nesreen** to
  Fatima) is a teacher-only text: "Yesterday we had a
  bring-and-buy sale at school. It was to raise money for
  local homeless people. It was great – there were over 100
  people there! I brought some cakes and my friend Aya
  brought some plants. I bought some new books to read and
  a really lovely jacket. I think the sale made about 200
  dinar! Are you going to have one at your school?"
- **Animals in Danger** (U7 L3 keys): only two reasons are
  given on the whole page — "They are losing their homes
  (golden toad)" and "People are hunting and killing them
  (all the other animals)"; the snow leopard is hunted for
  its fur; the three room objects came from a snow leopard
  (the coat), an ibex (the head on the wall) and a tiger
  (the rug); habitat answers: snow leopard — high, cold
  places with snow · tiger — the forest · ibex — the
  mountain forest ("called a 'mountain goat'") · blue
  whale — the sea · golden toad — wetlands that can be
  drained; the one habitat none of them lives in is the
  desert. True/false key: blue whales are now protected
  (the "hunted now" sentence is false); they hunted tigers
  "for their skins", not their fur as the false sentence
  claims.
- **Please keep the rivers clean!** (U7 L4 keys): paragraphs
  match b · d · a · c; definitions key: 1 volunteer ·
  2 pollute · 3 litter · 4 habitats; discussion answers
  include "It destroys wildlife habitats, damages the
  rivers and damages health" and "Stop littering, and
  force the government to stop sewage and waste from
  factories, etc."
- **A Warmer World** (U7 L5 keys): the diagram "shows how
  the world becomes warmer. Heat from the Sun stays in the
  Earth's atmosphere."; results list: ice caps melt · sea
  level rises · some land disappears/is underwater · weather
  may change · hotter summers and wetter winters possible ·
  less rain in hot countries · some farmland might become
  desert; interview answers keyed ("They produce gases
  which trap heat in the atmosphere" · fuels: "Coal, gas
  and oil"); the model conclusion: "To stop the world
  getting warmer, we can stop burning fuels like coal, gas
  and oil. We can use wind power instead. This should be
  done because we have to stop our farmland becoming
  desert. I think it will be done because we have no
  choice. Governments have to do something. Otherwise our
  world will be destroyed."
- **The Carter cat told in full** (Track 46): "The Carter
  family bought a new TV. They put the empty box outside to
  be collected. Later, the Carters' cat wanted to come
  inside because it was raining. Nobody opened the window
  for the cat, so it went into the box. When some men came
  to collect the rubbish next morning, they put the box
  into their truck. They drove the truck to the rubbish
  tip and emptied it. A man who was working at the rubbish
  tip heard a sound: 'Miaow! Miaow!' 'Puss! Where are you,
  puss?' he called. He found the box, opened it, and the
  cat jumped out. It raced away. Like all cats, it was able
  to find its way home. It reached the house and sat
  outside. 'Miaow! Miaow!' Somebody opened the door and the
  cat ran inside." Sentence order (Track 45): 1f · 2c ·
  3i · 4a · 5k · 6e · 7g · 8b · 9d · 10j · 11h. The notes
  underline that the pictures end at Picture 8 but "the
  story itself doesn't end there".
- **Waste conversations** (Track 47): Picture 1 — Father
  and **Tamara** ("That's so wasteful, Tamara. People
  around the world are hungry. Think of them next time.");
  Picture 2 — James brushing his teeth with the tap
  running ("But we have plenty of water." — "Yes, we have
  now, but we may not always have plenty. Turn the tap
  off."); Picture 3 — Nasser and Kamal ("There are lights
  blazing everywhere!" — "No, I don't think so. I think
  it's terrible to waste electricity like this. You should
  switch most of them off. Waste not, want not,
  remember?"). Notes key: took too much food · think of
  hungry people · brushing teeth/plenty of water · turn
  tap off · lights look lovely · switch lights off.
- **The Moving Desert** (U7 L7 keys): causes — "Sand
  storms, low rainfall, keeping too many animals,
  over-farming, tree cutting"; word key: desert · vegetation
  · agriculture · irrigation · challenge · fertile ·
  government · region; discussion answers fix the geography:
  "if people don't do anything, the fertile areas **in the
  north – near Benghazi and Tripoli** might become desert";
  the National Plan's problem — "some people need to use
  their farmland all the time to have enough food. Or they
  need it for their animals. They don't have enough land to
  stop using it"; the other solutions: "We can stop cutting
  down trees. We can also try to use the same land for
  different things each season."
- **The Medals** (U7 L8 keys): who rescued whom — 1 Jim
  Cooper · 2 Bashir · 3 Rod Border; information sheet:
  characters "Bob, Bashir, Jim Cooper, Chuck, Aziz, **Dick
  Bird**, Paul, Hans, Rod Border" (the pilot joins the named
  cast); summary: "Jim Cooper breaks his leg on a mountain
  walk. Bob and Bashir help to rescue him in a helicopter.
  They both get medals, but Bob thinks he is being attacked
  by ants and jumps in the pool!"; new words: helicopter,
  stretcher. Track 49's beach-litter conversation (Yassir
  and Jamal) is printed in full in the Workbook notes —
  "We are all been asked to try to keep our country clean.
  Here's your chance" (as printed, "We are all been asked").
- **The improved phone call** (Track 32, Workbook only):
  Anas done right — "Hi. My name's Anas Abdullah. I need to
  book an appointment to see a doctor. I have a stomach
  ache"; spelling A-B-D-U-L-L-A-H; "around three days now";
  appointment at 4.30. The model job calls (U8 L2) are
  teacher-only possible conversations: Muhammad Maziq
  calls **Khaled Mahmudi** about the garden job — "It's 105
  West Street", Thursday at 4 p.m.; **Reem Hadid** calls
  **Hoda Abdallah** about helping with her children — "Our
  address is 12 North Street", Saturday morning.
- **Listening mistakes** (Track 50): Susan wrote down the
  wrong telephone number (432147 is the stranger's own
  number, not Liz's); Jalal came to the wrong address (24
  West Street — "Perhaps it's the wrong number. Or perhaps
  it's West Road – or Drive, or …"); Jane Smith missed her
  interview with Miss Brown because — the script — she
  wrote the day down wrong: "It was yesterday. You didn't
  come for it."
- **The fire report** (Track 51, Workbook only, in full):
  a house in Castle Street ruined by fire; **Mrs Sarah
  Brown** and her two children **Ann, five, and Kate,
  three**, asleep upstairs; Mr Brown works at night;
  neighbour **Mr Sam White** smelled smoke, ran towards
  the house and phoned the firefighters on his mobile;
  Mrs Brown wouldn't jump — "Not without the girls!" — the
  girls were found **in the bathroom**, "not in their
  bedroom… That probably saved their lives." Headline key:
  1 a.
- **Going on holiday** (Track 53): the memory game played
  to Person F, who forgets *book* — "so we have to start
  again"; the chain runs shoes · shoes and three shirts ·
  + a camera · + a book · + a bar of chocolate.
- **A high-flying job** (U8 L3 keys): paragraph topics —
  1 "Muhammad has always wanted to be a pilot" · 2
  "Muhammad at school" · 3 "Muhammad at college"; topic
  sentences keyed ("Muhammad did very well at school" ·
  "Muhammad will never forget his time at the college or
  the people he met there"); word meanings: *especially* —
  more than any other · *language of the air* — the language
  used by pilots (and air traffic controllers) throughout
  the world; the English language · *extra* — more work
  than Muhammad had to do for his course · *aviation
  college* — a college where you can learn to be a pilot ·
  *solo* — alone · *pilot's certificate* — a piece of paper
  saying you can fly a plane. The Workbook's fish article
  keyed: paragraph 1 when his hobby started, paragraph 2
  his favourite fish; a glass bowl with stones, plants,
  water and goldfish; the great white shark lives in
  Australia (answers 7: 1 b · 2 a).
- **Model paragraphs kept**: U4 L4's town — "My town has
  changed a lot in the last five years. It used to be very
  quiet, but now more people have moved here, and more
  shops have opened. This has been good for me because
  there are more people my age. I used to have only one
  friend in my neighbourhood, but now I have more friends.
  There is a new basketball court and a cinema. I used to
  be bored, but now I enjoy life more." · U8 L4's best day —
  "The best day of my life was my birthday last year. My
  parents gave me a beautiful new bike. It was a complete
  surprise! I took it to the seafront with my best friend
  Tareq. We had races and we rode very fast. It was so
  exciting! Then I went to my favourite restaurant with my
  parents, my sister, my grandparents and all my friends.
  They gave me more presents and we had a delicious meal. I
  felt so happy. It was a very special day." The U8 L4
  sentence key keeps the model correction: "'You've done
  well in maths, English and Arabic,' my mother said."
  Joining key: "Ameen was very sad when/because his
  grandfather died · Hanan enjoys her job because she meets
  a lot of different people · Marwa is very good at
  English, but she isn't good at maths."
- **Unit 5 Lesson 1 discussion** names the modern channels
  in the teacher's own answer list: "text messages,
  **WhatsApp/other app-based messaging services**, email,
  the telephone, letters by post, radio, television" — a
  2019 Libyan school book naming WhatsApp, as printed.
  Workbook model answer for the same lesson kept: "In the
  past, it was difficult to get and to send messages and
  information. Now it is easy… You can just send a message
  on WhatsApp."
- **Parts of speech keyed** (U5 L3): the abbreviations are
  expanded in the notes — n. noun · v. verb · adj. adjective
  · adv. adverb · prep. preposition · conj. conjunction ·
  pron. pronoun — with the worked sentence "The black horse
  ran fast, so it won the race easily" labelled word by
  word, and the honest note: "There is no example of a
  preposition in the sentence in question 2… its usual
  grammatical name is article" (of *the*). *Bank* key:
  a 3 (noun) · b 6 (verb) · c 2 (noun) · d 5 (verb). A
  chesterfield is "a piece of furniture". *Comb* sentences:
  "Your hair is untidy. You should comb it." · "Can you
  lend me a comb, please? I've lost mine."
- **From Cairo to Tripoli** (U5 L4 keys): the article takes
  **one day** from reporter to reader; passive forms by
  picture — is written (1) · is sent (2) · is read (3) ·
  is edited (4) · (is) inserted (4) · is sent (5) · are
  printed (5) · are sent (6) · are sold (7) · is read (8);
  active forms — takes (2) · arrives (3) · likes (4) ·
  open (7). The fish-canning model text: "First, the fish
  are caught and put into boxes. Next, the boxes are
  brought to land. Then the fish are taken to a fish
  factory. At the factory, the fish are washed. Then they
  are cooked. After that, they are put into cans. Finally,
  the cans are sent to the supermarket." Verb table keyed:
  catch/caught/caught · bring/brought/brought · sell/sold/
  sold · take/took/taken · send/sent/sent · make/made/made ·
  write/wrote/written · wash/washed/washed · cook/cooked/
  cooked · put/put/put.
- **Great Inventions** (U5 L5 keys): the extracts come from
  "a reference site"; Baird — "John Baird invented
  television. He was Scottish. He invented it in 1926. He
  is dead."; Marconi — "Guglielmo Marconi invented the
  radio. He was Italian."; true/false key settles two more:
  Alexander Bell invented the telephone (the extract's
  claim otherwise is false), and Marconi "sent a radio
  message from America to England" (the reverse claim is
  false). Passive transformations keyed: "The wheel was
  invented by Mesopotamians around 3500 BCE · The Chinese
  made the earliest compasses · The World Wide Web was
  invented by Sir Tim Berners-Lee in 1989."
- **Telephones compared** (U5 L6 key): landline advantages —
  "You can phone people all over the world · Often more
  reliable than a mobile phone"; disadvantage — "Can only
  be used in one place". Mobile advantages — "Does not
  need a landline · Can be used almost anywhere ·
  Smartphones can be used to take photographs, play music,
  access the internet"; disadvantage — "Often less reliable
  than a landline phone". Layla's phone message keyed:
  message, did, buy, was wearing, likes, very, wants, buy,
  one. Texts true/false key: 1 F · 2 T · 3 DS · 4 T · 5 T ·
  6 F.
- **Two Jobs' neighbours**: U6 L1's match key (1 D · 2 B ·
  3 G · 4 A · 5 C · 6 F · 7 E) and the drill facts — a pilot
  flies a plane and wears a uniform; a shop assistant works
  for a shopkeeper; an engineer works outside or in an
  office. U6 L5's timetable answers key the England school
  week (Wednesday · Thursday · Monday · Tuesday · Friday ·
  Monday) and the Workbook comparison: "We have English,
  maths, history, science, geography, sport, RE, IT, art
  and music in common · We do not study French or home
  economics · We study Arabic and Libyan literature · We
  have six lessons a day, not seven · The main difference
  is that we start school earlier and finish earlier."
  Subject-link key (Workbook): Islam — RE · weather and
  population — geography · past events — history · numbers
  and shapes — maths · cooking — home economics · coding
  and programming — IT.

## Differences and slips in this book, as printed

1. **Jane's mistake is told two ways.** The U8 L1 notes'
   "Possible answers" say "Jane forgot the time of the
   interview"; Track 50's script and the printed Answers
   both say she **wrote down the wrong date** — the
   interview was yesterday. Each as printed.
2. **"homelgo"** — the U2 L4 notes print the model advice
   as "You should go **homelgo** to bed" (book p.47), the
   two alternatives *home* / *go to bed* run together.
3. **Track 49's grammar slip kept as printed**: "We are
   all **been** asked to try to keep our country clean"
   (Yassir's line, book p.148).
4. **The Phonetic Chart (book p.160) prints the /e/
   keyword correctly as "men"** — where the Course Book's
   Phonetic Symbols keyword column reads "mgn" as printed
   there. The chart otherwise matches the Course Book's
   set: vowels tree · sit · book · too / men · america ·
   word · sort / cat · but · part · not; diphthongs here ·
   day / tour · boy · go / wear · my · how; consonants
   plane · bed · tomato · do · chicken · jam · kite · gate /
   five · violin · think · father · six · zoo · fish ·
   casual / milk · no · sing · hello · lion · rocket ·
   window · yes.
5. Unlike Grade 8, there is **no Rashid/Waleed-style name
   split**: this Teacher Book's scripts agree with the
   Course Book's transcripts throughout (checked track by
   track against the Course Book filing — Tracks 1–53 all
   present, same speakers, same words, including the
   Course Book's own slips).

# Libya — English Grade 9 (Preparatory 3): Work Book

**READ COMPLETE 2026-10-25** — read in full from page
renders (`work9-pages/`, all 94 pages at 100 dpi).
**Page rule: book p. = PDF p. − 1** (Unit 1 opens on book
p.4 = PDF p.5).

## Identity

"21st Century English for Libya — Preparatory 3 Workbook",
State of Libya, Ministry of Education, CERC. Garnet
Publishing Ltd, Reading RG1 4QS, UK — **"First edition,
copyright © 2019 Garnet Publishing Ltd."** The imprint
thanks **Donna Bailey** for her contribution; illustrators
**Red Giraffe, Janette Hill, Doug Nash**. Cover: the same
domed Libyan building as the Teacher Book's cover, here
tinted red; school year 2019–2020 (1440–1441 AH). Back
cover code **10030904**; the back cover prints the four
components (Course Book, Workbook, Teacher's Book, Audio)
with the same descriptions as the Teacher Book's back
cover, plus an Arabic line addressed to the pupil:
«عزيزي التلميذ محافظتك على الكتاب المدرسي قيمة حضارية»
("Dear pupil, keeping your school book is a civilized
value"). Contents: U1 A School Magazine (p.4) · U2 Health
Matters (p.17) · U3 Olympic Sports and Games (p.32) · U4
Fun with English 1 (p.44) · U5 Communication and Sharing
Information (p.51) · U6 Jobs and Professions (p.63) · U7
Saving Our World (p.77) · U8 Fun with English 2 (p.88).
**No back matter of its own** — no wordlists, no tapescript
(both live in the Teacher Book's notes instead).

## Shape

The black-and-white writing twin: every Course Book lesson
gets its Workbook exercises in the Course Book's order,
cross-referenced by page ("on page 24 of your Course Book"),
many carrying a **C21 SKILLS** badge. The work is the
written half of every key already filed from the Teacher
Book — sentence building, cloze, ordering, matching,
punctuation repair, and the year's writing tasks done in
stages (notes → draft → correct → final copy), with the
five-part story information sheet (Title · Characters ·
What is it about? · Why I liked/didn't like the story ·
New words) repeated for all five Bob and Bashir episodes.
Pupil-facing tip boxes, kept word for word:

- **Writing tip: Punctuation – speech marks** (U1 L5):
  "When we report what people asked or said, we have to use
  speech marks. Examples: *Mike said, 'I'm a good
  photographer.' · 'I'm a good photographer,' Mike said.*
  Look at the commas in both sentences. Look at this
  example. Why is there no comma? *'Is Rajab good at
  writing stories?' I asked.*"
- **Writing tip: Paragraphs** (U1 L5): "This is a very short
  story, so it needs one paragraph. In paragraphs, sentences
  follow each other. They do **not** begin on new lines.
  When you finish, check your punctuation."
- **Vocabulary tip: Expressing frequency** (U2 L1): every
  day · every Thursday · often · not very often · once a
  week · twice a week · sometimes.
- **Vocabulary tip: Expressing quantity – a lot of** (U2
  L2): "We can say: *a lot of biscuits, a lot of fruit.*"
  The exercise above it makes the pupil write the rule:
  too many / a few go before count nouns; too much / a
  little before non-count nouns.
- **Writing tip: Linking ideas in a sentence** (U2 L7):
  "We use *but*, *and* and *when* to link ideas in a
  sentence. *Mr Blue has the flu, but he is feeling better
  today.* (contrasting information) · *Mr Blue has the flu,
  and is seeing the doctor tomorrow.* (additional
  information) · *Mr Blue was at the doctors when I saw
  him.* (when something happened)"
- **Writing tip: Topic sentences** (U3 L6): "When you write
  a paragraph describing or explaining something, the first
  sentence often tells you what the paragraph is about.
  This is called the **topic sentence**."
- **Speaking tip: Saying numbers** (U3 L4): "There are 60
  seconds in one minute. There are 100 centimetres in a
  metre. We use a decimal point and say: **12.5 seconds:**
  *twelve point five seconds* · **1.45 metres:** *one point
  four five metres*."
- **Speaking tip: Saying dates – years** (U3 L7): "1981
  means the year one thousand, nine hundred and
  eighty-one. However, we say and write the year in these
  words: 1981 – *nineteen eighty-one* · 1896 – *eighteen
  ninety-six* · 2014 – *two thousand and fourteen*."
- **Reading tip: Understanding the meaning of new words**
  (U5 L3): "Sometimes you don't need a dictionary. You can
  work out the meanings of a word by looking for clues in
  the same sentence or in other sentences." The chesterfield
  exercise then walks the possible → impossible → correct
  method in three printed steps.
- **Remember!** boxes: U4 L4 — "You will need to use 'used
  to'." · U8 L4 — "Paint a picture. How did you feel?"

## Work-Book-only texts, kept

- **Aunt Sally's advice in note form** (U1 L4, printed as
  handwriting — the pupil turns the notes into her
  paragraphs): for the exam-stress letter — "work hard all
  semester – don't work too hard before exams – at exam
  time, try not to worry – before bed, read a story or
  listen to music, etc. – bed early"; for the
  homework-copying letter — "agree, not right – don't give
  h.w. – v. unhelpful – not helping your friend – doesn't
  do own work, won't learn anything – e.g., can't copy in
  exams – explain". Two more problems with her notes (U1
  L4 D): "I stammer when the teacher asks me a question. I
  feel very embarrassed." — notes: "count to 5 before u
  spk. – think abt. what to say – ask yr. teacher to help –
  she'll understand"; "I cannot remember new words in my
  French class." — notes: "write words in groups in yr.
  vocabulary bk. e.g. names of food, buildings, clothes –
  ask for help – learn 5 new words a day."
- **Sarah's letter to Muna** (U2 L6, in full): "Dear Muna,
  I was very sorry to hear about your accident. I hope your
  leg does not hurt too much and that you will be able to
  leave hospital soon. We have had an unusual week at
  school. First, we had a visit from a nurse called
  Fawzia. She gave us a talk about food. It was very
  interesting and I really enjoyed it. Now I can tell you
  which food is good for you and which is not so good. Then
  we had a visit from a netball coach. She was fantastic.
  We learned a lot from her. She showed us little things to
  make our game better. I will show them to you when you
  are back at school. All your friends in class miss you
  and send you their best wishes. We will come and see you
  on Friday if you are still in hospital. Love Sarah" —
  with the card-making instructions beside it (card about
  20 cm by 28 cm, fold in the middle, picture on the front,
  message inside).
- **Jalal's marked draft** (U2 L5 D, in full): paragraph 1
  of his story printed as handwriting with the teacher's
  marks on it — "I sometimes have to look after my little
  brother for an hour or so. [join] My parents go out.
  Ibrahim is ten. He always wants me to play with him. I
  [join] want him to play by himself. One day when I
  [gr. — 'played' corrected] a computer game, he said again
  and again, 'Jalal, I feel sick. I've got a [sp.] pane in
  my stomach.' [p.] [n.p.] 'You always say that, Ibrahim.
  There's nothing wrong with you,' I said. 'You haven't got
  a [sp.] pane. You are a [sp.] pane. Sit down and watch
  that video.'" — and a marginal question, "How did you
  feel?"
- **The Khalid paragraph** (U2 L8 C, to correct and join):
  "Khalid was watching TV. The phone rang. It was for his
  mother. I'll take it in the kitchen she said. Take care
  of your little brother. I will, he answered. Five minutes
  later Khalid looked for his brother. He wasn't there.
  Khalid ran into the garden. He saw his brother up a tree.
  Stay there, he shouted. Don't move. Just then, his
  brother fell. Khalid ran over. His brother was lying on
  the ground. He was crying. He wasn't hurt. He was just
  frightened." (The Teacher Book's model answer is filed
  in its section.)
- **The Waddan–Mizda final** (U3 L8 C, cloze): "Waddan
  played Mizda in the ___ of the Libyan Schools'
  competition. After 85 minutes, the ___ was Waddan 0,
  Mizda 0. Then the ball came to Hasan. He ___ it past the
  Mizda goalkeeper. 'Waddan 1, Mizda 0!' shouted Hasan.
  'We've ___ the ___! We've ___ Mizda.' Unfortunately,
  Mizda ___ two ___ very quickly after that and Waddan ___
  the game 1–2." (Box: lost, kicked, goals, final, beaten,
  cup, score, scored, won.)
- **The three notices reprinted in full** (U4 L2, book
  p.47 — the Work Book carries the texts itself, so its
  ten questions can be answered without the Course Book):
  *Watch your weight!* — "Find out if you weigh too much.
  Are you often out of breath? Do you eat more food than
  you need? If you answer 'Yes', you are overweight. **How
  to lose weight.** The best way is to eat the right food.
  **Eat less fat.** Eat no cakes, chocolate or biscuits.
  (Warning! These also contain sugar.) Drink less milk and
  eat less cheese. Cut down on chips and crisps. **Eat less
  sugar.** Don't have sugar in your tea or coffee. Cut down
  on jam and honey. Eat more fruit." · *Al-Mustafa Sports
  Centre* — "Take part in our Schools' Tennis Competition.
  Everybody who takes part will receive a medal. The
  competition starts on Monday 2nd March. Boys: 11 o'clock
  · Girls: 2 o'clock. Wonderful prizes for the winners!
  Each winner will receive a new computer for his or her
  school. Call or email now and ask for an application
  form. 04321 952841 / tennis@msc.com" (the number echoes
  the Ashby centre's 952841 from Track 15) · *Healthy
  teeth* — "Stop teeth decaying! Your teeth are important!
  Do not lose them! Brush your teeth at least once a day.
  Brush your teeth after every meal if you can. Spend
  three minutes cleaning your teeth carefully. Go to a
  dentist every six months. If you have toothache, go to a
  dentist. Stop eating sugar and food that contains sugar."
- **Nabeel and the computer** (U5 L8 D, in full): "Nabeel
  decided to buy a computer. He wanted to send emails and
  use social networking sites. He went to a shop and talked
  to the shopkeeper. He was very helpful. They spent an
  hour looking at different computers and, in the end,
  Nabeel bought one. He took it home and switched it on.
  Nothing happened. He tried five or six times, but the
  computer didn't work. Nabeel was not pleased. The next
  morning, Nabeel went back to the shop. The shopkeeper was
  not there, but his helper was. 'I bought this computer
  here yesterday,' said Nabeel, 'but it doesn't work.' The
  assistant tried it. Nothing happened. 'We'll have to send
  it away to be checked,' said the man. 'How long will that
  take?' asked Nabeel. 'About two weeks,' he answered.
  'That's no good,' said Nabeel. 'I paid a lot of money for
  a new computer and I want it now.' 'I'm sorry. There's
  nothing I can do,' said the assistant. 'Oh, yes there
  is,' said Nabeel. 'Either give me another new computer,
  or give me my money back.' He tried the new computer and
  everything worked. He took his new computer home."
- **The litter report in full** (U7 L1 C — headline choice
  A *Litter* · B *The big clean-up* · C *Lots of people go
  out picking up litter*; the Teacher Book's key picks B):
  "For the past three months, hundreds of people have
  worked in their spare time cleaning up litter. Everything
  was carefully planned, so people knew what they had to
  do. 'The planning was great,' said university student
  **Ahmed Ayoob**. 'It meant there weren't too many people
  in one place and too few people in another. We went to
  different streets in the town in groups and we also
  cleaned up a park. I found it hard to believe how much
  litter we found there.' Schools also took part in the
  clean-up. Schoolgirl **Jameela** said, 'We were sent to
  picnic places outside the city. They're lovely places, of
  course. Children can play and run about safely while the
  adults sit and talk. But they're not lovely when they're
  covered with old cans, bottles, plastic bags and food.
  And they're not safe. Why don't people think of others
  and take their rubbish home? It makes me very angry.'
  **Saeed Abdullah** has four children. 'The whole family
  took part,' he said, 'even little **Muna**. She's only
  four, but she was picking up litter just like the rest of
  us. I think education is important. We should teach our
  children from an early age to keep our country clean.'"
- **The marked career-plan draft** (U6 L7 C, handwriting
  with marks): "I am very kind and [sp.] frendly. My
  favourite subject at school is English. [join] I also
  like geography. At the weekend, I go walking [where?]. I
  like being outside. I like [join] [gr.] look at animals.
  When I [gr.] will finish school, I am going to be [gr.
  sp.] a busnessman. Before that, I am going to go to
  [sp.] colledge [to do what?]." The Teacher Book's
  corrected model is filed in its section.
- **My hobby** (U8 L3, in full, with two real photographs —
  a goldfish and a great white shark): "My hobby began
  when I was a boy and I have enjoyed collecting fish and
  learning about them since then. When I was ten, I made
  my own aquarium. My mother gave me a big glass bowl. I
  put some stones and plants in it, filled it with water
  and put my fish in. They were goldfish. When the first
  baby goldfish arrived, I was surprised to see that they
  were black. Then a friend told me that baby goldfish are
  always black. They change colour as they get older. My
  favourite fish is the great white shark. Most people
  think it is a killer. It has attacked 153 people swimming
  in the sea. But for me, it is a beautiful animal. A few
  years ago, I went all the way to Australia just to see it
  in the wild. After about an hour, one appeared next to
  our boat. It was about five metres long, almost as long
  as the boat. That was one of the best days of my life."
  (Questions keyed in the Teacher Book: he is not a
  fisherman; first aquarium a glass bowl; stones, plants,
  water and goldfish; the babies were black; favourite fish
  the great white shark; seen in the wild in Australia;
  aquarium = a glass home for fish, expensive = not cheap.)
- The firefighter's day is printed here as the ordering
  exercise itself (U6 L3): the two given sentences ("When I
  arrived at the fire station yesterday morning, I checked
  my equipment as usual. We had our first call at ten
  o'clock.") and the closing paragraph are set in type, and
  sentences a–n are the jumbled middle. The Track 39
  exercise (U6 L4 D) prints its three job choices per
  speaker — 1 a businessman · b reporter · **c cook** ·
  2 a flight attendant · b teacher · c waiter · 3 a
  astronaut · b flight attendant · **c pilot** · 4 a doctor
  · b coach · **c teacher** · 5 a nurse · **b vet** · c
  farmer · 6 **a secretary** · b businesswoman · c
  zoologist (the letters the Teacher Book keyed).

## Differences from the other two books, as printed

1. **Labib for Layth.** U1 L2 asks "Who does **Labib**
   suggest as the editor? Why?" and "What do **Labib** and
   Usama want to be?" — the boy the Course Book's
   conversation and Track 2 call **Layth** is **Labib**
   here. The Teacher Book's key answers the questions
   without naming him, so the split is this book's alone.
2. **Suad for Suhad.** U7 L2 C asks what the three girls
   will bring and prints the first name "**Suad**"; Track
   44's script in both companion books names her **Suhad**.
3. **Sawsan for Susan.** U5 L7 B tells pupils to "follow
   **Sawsan's** advice on page 64 of your Course Book";
   the Teacher Book's notes call the *Speak up!* blogger
   Susan.
4. This book's own rubric for the Carter cat (U7 L6 A)
   points at "the journey on page 88 of your Course Book"
   and Tracks 45–46 — matching the Course Book; and its
   sentence list quietly supplies the story's missing
   middle in single sentences (the cat "raced away", "Like
   all cats, it was able to find its way home"), the same
   completion the Teacher Book's Track 46 tells in full.
5. Otherwise the three books agree — checked exercise by
   exercise against the Teacher Book's keys (including the
   deliberately wrong spellings it sets out to teach:
   *sentre, biginers', afleetics, practice* for the verb,
   *teem*).

# Libya — English Grade 9 (Preparatory 3): FULLY READ 2026-10-25

**Libya Grade 9 — FULLY READ 2026-10-25** (Course Book +
Teacher Book + Work Book; the grade-09 record's three
books). The course names a fourth component, a **Test
booklet**, which is not in the library record. The grade's
grammar spine: tense revision with present perfect
*for/since* · gerunds after *like/enjoy/hate* · possessive
's against plural ' · defining relative clauses with
*who* · *should* advice · quantifiers · *want* + object +
*to*-infinitive · *used to* · decimals, years, weights and
times said aloud · the passive in present, past and
*can* forms · parts of speech named and abbreviated ·
*going to* for certain futures · *When* + present tense ·
*may/might* · the first conditional revised — all of it
carried by the five Bob and Bashir episodes at the World
Youth Camp and a full year of skills tips gathered in the
two Fun with English units. Next per the queue: **Libya
Grade 10** — record check first, then the re-cover check,
one book at a time.

# Libya — English Grade 10 (Secondary 1): Course Book

**File:** `10_English_for_Libya_Course_Book.pdf` — 11,509,487 B, 141 pp, real %PDF v1.7, from the grade-10 record's URL `https://liasinstitute.com/PDFs/10_English_for_Libya_Course_Book.pdf` (collected 2026-10-22, approval source Libya CERC). Text extraction `course10.txt` is 19,317 chars — essentially only the repeated Arabic CERC rights footer — so, per the Grade 9 lesson, the book was read visually from renders `course10-pages/` (141 PNGs at 100 dpi). **Page rule: book p. = PDF p.** (the printed folio matches the PDF page throughout).

**Re-cover check (the Grade 4 lesson):** PASSED as a genuine new book before reading — page counts of all three grade-10 books (141 / 158 / 100) differ from Grade 9's (130 / 162 / 94), and the cover is new: "21st CENTURY English for Libya — **Secondary 1** Course Book," a white Libyan old-town building under a blue sky. The line moves from Preparatory (Grades 7–9) to **Secondary** at Grade 10.

**Identity:** "English for Libya Secondary 1 Course Book," State of Libya, Ministry of Education, Curricula and Educational Research Centre. Published by Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK. Imprint (book p.3): "First edition, copyright © 2019 Garnet Publishing Ltd." No individual authors named (the imprint asserts "the right of the authors to be identified" without naming them). **Shape:** eight units — six teaching units of eight lessons each plus a Round up (U1 Language Learning · U2 Education · U3 Travel and Tourism · U5 Family and Friends · U6 Health and Fitness · U7 Science and Technology) and two four-lesson revision units (U4 Fun with English 1, U8 Fun with English 2) — the same shape as Grades 8 and 9. Back matter is re-arranged for the Secondary line: **Grammar and Functions Reference pp.96–105** (new inside the pupil book) · Checklist for Written Work p.106 · **Transcripts pp.107–125 (Tracks 1–65)** · Key Words pp.126–139 · Phonetic Symbols p.140. Contents (book p.4): U1 p.6 · U2 p.18 · U3 p.30 · U4 p.42 · U5 p.50 · U6 p.62 · U7 p.76 · U8 p.88 · Grammar and Functions p.96 · Checklist p.106 · Transcript p.107 · Key Words p.126 · Phonetic Symbols p.140. An Icon Key (p.4) names: pairwork, group work, project work, functional grammar, 21st Century Skills. The Book Map spread (book p.5) prints the four strands per unit (Listening and speaking · Reading and writing · Structure and language · Vocabulary), but its small type sits under the Garnet watermark in this scan and is largely illegible — strand detail below is taken from the unit dividers, lesson boxes and Round ups instead; nothing was guessed from the Book Map.

**The year's anchor pupil is Adam Al Sudani of Tripoli** (application form, U1 L5: surname Al Sudani, forenames Adam, P.O. Box 5674, 100 Hadhbah Road, Tripoli, Libya; phone +218 2XXXXXX; email adam@qanet.ly). Track 12 places him in **Year 10**.

## Unit 1 — Language Learning (divider book pp.6–7; lessons pp.8–16; Round up p.17)

Divider C21 skills: Communication Skills: Rephrasing information · Study Skills: Inferring meaning from context · Information Literacy: Reading diagrams · Information Literacy: Skimming a text · Communication Skills: Drawing conclusions · Life Skills: Critical thinking · Career Skills: Reading applications. Lessons: 1 How do you learn? p.8 · 2 Describing Trends p.10 · 3 Choosing a Summer Programme p.11 · 4 Getting Familiar p.12 · 5 Asking for Information p.13 · 6 A Tour of the Self-access Centre p.14 · 7 Arabic and English p.15 · 8 Official Languages p.16 · Round up p.17.

- **L1 How do you learn? (pp.8–9).** Six pictures, one per voice on Track 1: 1 an interpreter ("He says …", captioned *interpreter*) · 2 a boy at a CD player in a self-access centre (sign: "Open 9 a.m.–6 p.m. Mon–Sat") · 3 a boy reading a newspaper with a bilingual dictionary · 4 two boys talking in a café (menu on the table) · 5 three girls with index cards ("international" on the card) · 6 a girl handing her teacher a LEARNING DIARY. Pupils match six printed sayings to the pictures (p.9): A "Sometimes I write new words on cards and test my friends, and my friends test me." · B "I practise writing every day." · C "I usually look up new words in a dictionary and write them in a notebook." · D "I write what I have learned in my learning diary after every class." · E "I go to the self-access centre and practise saying the words with a CD." · F "I go to the café with my friends and we speak only English for two hours." Grammar box (p.9) — **Talking about facts and habits: Present simple**, examples tagged (habit)/(fact): "I walk to school every day." · "My sister uses a computer to do her homework." · "The capital of Libya is Tripoli." · "Bilingual dictionaries are very useful for learning languages." · "I don't speak Japanese." · "The self-access centre isn't open on Fridays." → Grammar and Functions p.96.
- **L2 Describing Trends (p.10).** Four topic sentences to match to four paragraphs, then each paragraph to a graph. The paragraphs, in full: **A** "Most experts think that there are around **6,800** [languages]. But the number of languages in the world **is going down quickly**. Linguists say there will only be 3,000 by 2100. This is because people prefer to learn the most common languages so they can communicate with others, read newspapers from other countries and use the internet." **B** "The UK is one of the most popular destinations. For example, the number of Indian students studying in the UK is **rising steeply**. In 1999 it was 3,000, but in 2004 it was 15,000." **C** "In 1980 3,285 students received a university degree in French and 4,331 received a degree in Spanish. In 2002 only 2,396 students received a degree in French, but 7,243 completed a degree in Spanish. Many people in the United States prefer to study Spanish because they think it is more useful." **D** "It went up from 5,000 students to 12,000 students between 2009 and 2013. Most people who study Arabic learn it at university, then go to an Arabic-speaking country to practise speaking and listening." Topic sentences: 1 "The number of American university students learning French **is falling slowly**." · 2 "Many students travel abroad to get a degree, learn a language or learn about a new country." · 3 "It is difficult to say exactly how many languages are spoken in the world today." · 4 "The number of people learning Arabic at university **is going up rapidly**." The trend verbs (falling slowly, going down quickly, rising steeply, going up rapidly) are the lesson's language.
- **L3 Choosing a Summer Programme (p.11).** A leaflet, "HOW TO CHOOSE A SUMMER PROGRAMME," in full: "The number of people taking part in summer study programmes is going up all the time. Choosing a programme can be confusing for both parents and children. That's why you need to think carefully before you make your choice. Here are some questions you should ask yourself. — What are your learning goals? Some summer programmes have classes in all subjects to help you prepare for school in the autumn. They can help you improve in all subjects. Others are especially for students who want to learn or practise a language. There are programmes that have classes all day, for people who need to make a lot of progress quickly. Other programmes offer classes only in the morning, so you can visit interesting places as well as studying. — What do you like doing in your free time? You can do different activities in a city or in the country. If you like doing sport, or enjoy being in a quiet place, you should choose a programme in the countryside. But if you like going to museums or the cinema, choose a programme in the city, or you might get bored. — How do you learn best? Some people like to be in small classes where they can get plenty of attention and help from their teachers. Others work better in larger groups where they can share their ideas with a lot of other people. Think about what type of class you like best, and find out about class size in the programmes you're interested in. — Study programmes are an exciting way to meet new people, learn new things and have fun. If you choose the right programme, you will have a fantastic time." Contact line: "For more information, email us at summerprogramme@summer.edu or visit our website." Functional Grammar box — **Describing feeling: Adjectives ending in -ed and -ing**: "-ed to talk about how a person feels (excited, confused); -ing to talk about the person, place or thing that makes them feel that way (exciting, confusing). Sami is very interested in history. He thinks the British Museum is very interesting."
- **L4 Getting Familiar (p.12).** Track 3: Omar and Adam talk about study programmes (the questions: what Adam likes about his programme, what Omar likes about the other, why Omar would prefer to stay with a family, what Adam wants to find out). Six opinion bubbles matched to six responses — the agreeing/disagreeing language of the unit: 1 "I'd love to go on a study programme." → B "So would I. I love art." (as printed, the responses run A–F: A "I agree. It might be very different from ours." · B "So would I. I love art." · C "I wouldn't. I'd rather go on holiday with my family." · D "So do I. I failed the exam last week." · E "I disagree. I'd rather meet lots of new people." · F "I don't. I think it's boring. Cities are much more interesting.") The six opinions: 2 "I need to work on my English." · 3 "I think it's really important to learn about the culture of the country you're in." · 4 "I want to be in the countryside where there's lots of fresh air." · 5 "I think it's much better to stay with a family." · 6 "I'd like to take drawing classes."
- **L5 Asking for Information (p.13).** Grammar box — **Asking politely: Indirect questions**, direct → indirect → reply: "What sports do you offer?" → "Could you tell me what sports you offer?" → "Certainly …" · "Do you have drawing classes?" → "Can you tell me if you have drawing classes?" → "Yes, we do." · "Do you have a pool?" → "Could you tell me if you have a pool?" → "I'm afraid we don't." · "Where is the school?" → "Can you tell me where the school is?" → "Of course." · "How much does it cost?" → "Could you tell me how much it costs?" → "It depends …" → p.97. **Adam's application form** (the lesson's reading): Surname AL SUDANI · Forenames ADAM · Address P.O. Box 5674, 100 Hadhbah Road, Tripoli, Libya · Phone +218 2XXXXXX · Email adam@qanet.ly · "WHY ARE YOU INTERESTED IN OUR SCHOOL? I would like to go on a summer programme to improve my English. I am interested in your school because you offer English classes at many levels. I also think I would like to be in the country, and to have an opportunity to do sport."
- **L6 A Tour of the Self-access Centre (p.14).** A floor plan (teachers' room, cafeteria, vending machines, seminar room, lecture theatres, lifts, telephones, stairs) with Track 8's directions to follow; Track 9's tour matched to six pictures; the six tour sentences to match: 1 "Don't try to change channels." · 2 "Don't take the magazines out of the self-access centre." · 3 "Put on the headphones so you don't disturb other people." · 4 "Fill out a card and bring it to the desk." · 5 "Just sit down and type your password." · 6 "Click on the icon for the program you want to use." (The full tour script is filed in the Transcripts section below.)
- **L7 Arabic and English (p.15).** Adam is giving a talk on Arabic in his English class; pupils read his handwritten notes and decide what they mean. The notes, as printed (handwriting, with the pupil's own slips): "ARABIC TODAY — ..420 m. people — 5th lang. in the world — One of official langs. of UN. TYPES OF ARABIC — People from different countries maybe don't understand each other — dialects (e.g. Moroc…) — Modern Standard Arabic in books/newspapers, on radio/TV, in mosques all over the world. HISTORY — Arabic from Semitic family. Most Semitic langs. dead. Spread across Europe (Science, Maths, etc.) into French, Spanish and English, esp. 800 yrs ago. THE HOLY QUR'AN — Pure lang. of Holy Qur'an v. similar to Modern Arabic. Helped Arabic survive for 1,400 yrs." Track 11 (the talk itself) is then heard to check — its settled figures differ from the notes and are filed in the Transcripts section. The lesson's article — **why English has so many synonyms**, in full: "Why are there so many words with similar meanings in English? Why do we have two words – sick and ill – that look very different but both mean *not very well*? Why is a reply also an answer? And why are there weddings and marriages in English? The answer lies in the history of Britain. Old English was a very different language from the English we know today. It included words brought to England by Anglo-Saxons and Vikings. The Anglo-Saxons brought their language with them in the 5th century. The Vikings invaded in the 9th century and brought more words. This mixture of languages was first written down in the 10th century. The same thing happened after 1066, when the Normans invaded Britain. They brought the French language and this added more words to the mixture (close for shut, desire for wish and liberty for freedom). Over time, the pairs of words – or synonyms – started to have very small differences in meaning. In fact, it is almost impossible to find two words that mean exactly the same thing. One word in a pair of synonyms is usually more formal or more specific than the other. The example sentences in dictionaries will help you decide if you can use either word, or if you must choose one."
- **L8 Official Languages (p.16).** Map-matching: Greece–Greek, Italy–Italian, Jordan–Arabic, India–Hindi, Kenya–English, Pakistan–Urdu (language box: Greek, Hindi, Arabic, Italian, Urdu, English). The reading is a pupil's assignment on **Canada**, handwritten, in full: "1 Canada is the second largest country in the world, after Russia. It is 9,979,619 km². It has a population of 31,946,300, and that number is growing. But Canada's population is not large for its size. Most people live near the border with the USA. 2 Canada is a beautiful country. Because it is so big, there are many parks and wilderness areas. If you travel to Canada, you can see rivers, lakes, forests, and even glaciers. One of Canada's most famous natural features is Niagara Falls. 3 How do people live in a country with two official languages? The two official languages of Canada are English and French, because some Canadians are of British origin and some are of French origin. Most people don't speak both languages. About 31% of the population speak French, and about 84% speak English. You will hear more French or English depending on where you are. 4 Canada has several large cities. The biggest of these is Toronto. The capital, Ottawa, is only the fourth largest city. In Ottawa, about half the people speak both French and English. Communication is easier there!"
- **Round up (p.17).** "Now you can": use the present simple to talk about facts and habits ("The Earth rotates around the Sun. (fact) · Libya is the fourth largest country in Africa. (fact) · I wake up at 6 in the morning. (habit) · I don't go to bed early at the weekend. (habit)") · use adjectives ending with -ed to describe how a person feels ("I am interested in History. · He was very bored in the lecture.") · use adjectives ending with -ing ("History is interesting. · The lecture was boring.") · use indirect questions to ask politely (the four L5 pairs repeated).

## Unit 2 — Education (divider book pp.18–19; lessons pp.20–28; Round up p.29)

Divider C21 skills: Media Literacy: Identifying text purpose · Communication Skills: Drawing conclusions · Life Skills: Critical thinking · Information Literacy: Skimming for information. Lessons: 1 At School p.20 · 2 Similarities and Differences p.21 · 3 Helping People Learn p.22 · 4 The Importance of Education p.24 · 5 Denise's Story p.25 · 6 School Memories p.26 · 7 A Special School p.27 · 8 An Interesting Job p.28 · Round up p.29.

- **L1 At School (p.20).** Five photos matched to five activities (use the self-access centre · watch a film · write a report · give a presentation · take an exam). **Adam's timetable** (the lesson's reading): Sunday — Biology, English, Arabic · Monday — Geography, English, Maths, Physics · Tuesday — Arabic, English, Geography, Maths · Wednesday — History, Chemistry, Maths · Thursday — Arabic, Maths, History, English (with a BREAK column; times 8.00–1.30). Model exchange: "How often does Adam have History?" — "He has History twice a week." Track 12 (Adam's talk) is heard here; two of his sentences are picked out for the borrow/lend contrast: "I'm going to the self-access centre to **borrow** some books for my report." · "My cousin is going to **lend** me some maps."

- **L2 Similarities and Differences (p.21).** The reading is a piece of English homework — "English homework October 18th – My school" — by **Abbas of Oman (15)**, kept in full: "My name is Abbas. I'm from Oman. I'm 15 years old and I have a cousin called Kamal who's 15 years old too. He's my best friend, but I can't see him very often because he's spending a year studying in Canada. He sends me letters telling me about his new school. Both Kamal and I are in Secondary 1, but our schools are very different. I go to a private school for children from Primary 1 to Secondary 3. There are about 800 students in the school and only about 25 students in each class. The school is big and beautiful. It has a lot of modern facilities, like a self-access centre, a swimming pool and a football field. It has a mosque too. There are only 500 pupils in Kamal's school, but his classes are as large as mine. That's because, unlike my school, Kamal's school is only for students in secondary school. Kamal says he likes going to a school where everyone is his age. I like going to a school with children of all ages. It's more fun. Kamal's school has pupils from many countries and they speak a lot of different languages. I think that sounds interesting. And Kamal is happy because he is not the only student trying to improve his English. Kamal's school is in the city and it is not as big as mine, so it doesn't have many facilities. Like my school, it has a self-access centre. But it doesn't have a mosque, a swimming pool or even a football field. It just has a gymnasium where the students play basketball and volleyball. But Kamal says there are good things about living in the city. He lives close to the cinema and the sports centre. He lives close to his school too. I live in a dormitory at my school. Neither Kamal nor I have to take the bus to school. We can just walk." Grammar boxes — **Expressing similarity**: "Both Kamal and Abbas are in Year 10. · Like my school, Kamal's school has a self-access centre. · His classes are as large as mine. · He lives close to his school too. · Neither Kamal nor Abbas has to take the bus to school." **Expressing differences**: "Unlike my school, Kamal's school is only for students in secondary school. · Kamal's school is in the city and it is not as big as mine." → p.98.
- **L3 Helping People Learn (pp.22–23).** A three-panel UNICEF article, kept in full. Panel 1, **What is UNICEF?**: "UNICEF was created in 1946, when many children were sick and hungry in Europe after World War II. UNICEF stands for United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund because it was created when there was an emergency. In 1953, UNICEF saw the emergency in Europe was over. However, they realized that children in many countries still needed help. It decided to work with children and families all over the world. It changed its name to United Nations Children's Fund, but people still call it UNICEF. UNICEF believes that all children should have a good education, health care (doctors and medicines) and good nutrition (healthy food)." Panel 2, **Why do some children not go to school?**: "Today, about **121 million** children around the world don't go to school. In countries such as Thailand, natural disasters like floods can destroy schools. Children in these countries can't go to school because they don't have a school building to go to. In other countries, parents don't want to send their children to school because the schools aren't clean or safe. In many parts of Asia, Africa and South America, children start working when they are less than 12 years old, and so they can't go to school. This is called child labour. About **246 million** children do child labour. In some places, like the Ivory Coast, children work in the fields. In the Philippines and other countries, children work in factories, where they make toys, clothes or sports equipment." Panel 3, **How does UNICEF help?**: "UNICEF does different things to help. When schools have been destroyed by natural disasters, UNICEF organizes emergency classes in tents, so children don't miss school. It also helps build new schools for these children. It tries to help schools get clean water and good food so that parents are not afraid to send their children there. UNICEF also works with governments to make sure that children go to school instead of going to work when they are very young." The numbers-matching exercise keys: 1946 = the year UNICEF was founded · 1953 = the year the emergency in Europe was over · 121 million = children who don't go to school · 246 million = children who work. The lesson also teaches reading help-words inside a text (the article's own glosses: health care "(doctors and medicines)", nutrition "(healthy food)", "This is called child labour", "like floods").
- **L4 The Importance of Education (p.24).** An appeal poster, in full: "**Nothing is more important than education.** Unfortunately not all children can go to school. Schools for All is an organization that helps the poorest children go to school. We give them money to pay for books. We make sure their schools are safe. Last year we built two schools in Kenya, and gave books to 500 children. **But Schools for All needs your help. Please donate to help us send more children to school this year.**" The comparison text — **Justine's day** (Uganda), in full: "Justine lives in Uganda, in Africa. Every morning she gets up at 5.00 a.m. and does her homework. She has breakfast at 6.30. Then she has to collect water with her sisters. They carry 10 litres of water – it's very heavy! Justine walks to school. The journey takes 25 minutes. Justine's lessons start at 8 o'clock and finish at 3.30. After school she goes home and helps her mother to water the garden. The family have some goats and Justine has to feed them. She also collects food for the family. They have supper at 9.30 and then Justine goes to bed." Pupils write comparisons starting from the printed model line: "Unlike Justine, I live in Libya."
- **L5 Denise's Story (p.25).** Five pictures (starting school; picking coffee with her mother; the woman from the organization with a letter; back in class; dreaming of being a doctor) with Track 18; four of Denise's sentences are printed for the link-word work, link words picked out in colour: "We didn't have much money any more, **so** I had to leave school." · "I was unhappy **because** many of my friends were going to school." · "The organization pays for my school fees **and** my uniform." · "I want to be a teacher **or** a doctor when I grow up." (Denise's full spoken story is filed in the Transcripts section.)
- **L6 School Memories (p.26).** Six captioned pictures — rickshaw, computer, calculator, abacus, pen and paper, car, slate and chalk — then Track 20, two men remembering school (questions per man: how did he travel, did he like school, which things did he use). Grammar box — **Describing situations: Adjective + infinitive**: "It was fun to ride in the rickshaw. · It was difficult to get paper and pencils. · It is easy to do Maths with a calculator." → p.98. (Both men's full scripts are filed in the Transcripts section.)
- **L7 A Special School (p.27).** A school website, in full: "**The Meade School** for Children in the Arts (MSCA). *Our Mission* — Child actors, musicians and athletes have different needs from other students. They need to have time to rehearse and train, but they need a good education too. Our mission is to give students both these things. At MSCA, students can take classes in the morning or at night. They study Art, Music and Sports, but they also study Biology, Physics and Chemistry so they can go to university when they leave school. *Our History* — In 1910, John Meade was walking in the street when a boy stopped him. The boy wanted John to help him read a street sign. John was surprised that the boy couldn't read. He asked the boy about his school, and the boy told him he was an actor. Child actors spent a lot of time rehearsing and didn't have time to go to school. John decided to start a special school for these children. He decided classes should start later than in other schools, so children would have time to rehearse. When they were working in another city, they could post their lessons to their teachers. *Changes* — Our school has changed with the times. In the beginning, it was only for actors and musicians. Then one day, while John Meade was reading the newspaper, he saw an article about young athletes. He realized that children who did sports needed a special school too. He brought more sports teachers to the school and he built a swimming pool. Other things have changed at the school too. We now have a modern self-access centre and a computer room. Students who are working in other cities don't have to send their lessons by post because they can send them by email." Grammar box — **Describing events in the past: Past simple and past continuous**: "John Meade was walking in the street when a boy stopped him." → p.98.
- **L8 An Interesting Job (p.28).** Track 22 (Sally and Maria) then Track 23 — **Sally Bishop's** two stories from her old job, printed in speech bubbles, in full: "I travelled a lot with my students because films are often made in other countries. I liked travelling and seeing new places, but I didn't like being away from my family. And the job was tiring too. I had to pack all the children's school books and take them to the airport." · "It was tiring for the children too. They had to act and study too, so they worked a lot." · "Once, I went to Italy with two actors. I had a lot of books to pack and it took a long time. I didn't want to be late, so I left the house as fast as possible. When I got on the plane because I couldn't get on the plane because I had forgotten my passport. I had to catch a flight the next day." [the bubble's wording runs together as printed] · "Once, I had to give a student called Ben a Maths exam. He was tired because he had been working on the film set all morning. I gave him his exam paper and started reading a book. Suddenly I heard a snore. I looked up and realized Ben had fallen asleep."
