Write them down as a list or mind map. · Look up any vocabulary that you are unsure of. · Select the ideas you want to use and delete any that are not relevant. You don't have to use everything. · Put your ideas into a logical order and group ideas that go together in sections. · If you can, find a model text and look at the style and layout. Are there any phrases or vocabulary that you can adapt for your own work? (Be careful not to copy chunks of the model text without changing it though.) · Think about who you are writing for and decide on the style and layout. For example, if you are writing a letter to a friend, the style will be different to a formal letter and the layout will be different to a story. **Writing and revising your work:** Use the ideas from your planning to write the first draft. Don't worry too much about accuracy or choosing the right words. · Remember to start a new paragraph every time you move on to a new idea or topic. · Read through your first draft and check that you have answered the question correctly. · Decide if you want to delete, change or add any more ideas. · Decide whether you want to change the paragraphing or the order of the ideas. · Check that you have linked your ideas using connecting words and phrases (e.g., first, next, then, etc.). · Write a second draft more carefully and slowly. Make sure your writing is clear. **Editing your work:** Read through your work. Check you have answered the question correctly, every sentence is clear, the grammar is correct, you have used suitable vocabulary and linking words, the spelling is correct. (Check with a dictionary.) · Show your work to your family or a friend and see if they can help you edit it. · **Editing tip: You could try reading the text in reverse order, starting with the final sentence and working backwards to the first sentence. This will stop you being distracted by the content and allow you to focus on technical details.**

## Transcripts (book pp.107–125) — Tracks 1–65

The pupil book prints every audio script. Filed per track with the settles — places where the script adds, corrects or differs from the lesson pages. Several tracks are marked repeats in the book itself (Tracks 2, 7 and others below).

- **Unit 1.** T1: six learners describe how they learn English (an interpreter hopeful studying Arabic; a France-born boy who visits Libya every summer; a newspaper reader heading for an England summer course; a boy in China on an exchange programme; a girl who writes new words on index cards; a girl in Russia who keeps a learning diary). T2 = repeat of T1. T3: **Omar and Adam** discuss the summer study-programme leaflet (Adam wants drawing classes; Omar wants history and culture; Adam prefers staying with a family). T4: six short agreement/disagreement exchanges. T5: Adam and his mother list what she wants to know (where the school is, sports, art classes, length — four weeks — cost). T6: **Adam phones Fisher School** (near Cambridge): drawing and painting classes yes; volleyball or basketball; **no swimming pool**; bicycles for rent; four weeks "probably around **£1,500**." T7 = repeat of T6. T8: directions to the self-access centre (through the door, turn left, straight on past the vending machines, turn right past the cafeteria; second door on the right, opposite the teachers' room). T9: the self-access centre tour (language-learning software; magazines read in the room only; hundreds of English books, borrowable on a card; monitors for videos only; English films with headphones). T10: syllable counting — software, information, video, monitor, application, password, destination, program, magazine, interesting. T11: **Adam's presentation on Arabic, in full**: "Arabic is spoken by about **230 million people** around the world. Arabic is the fifth most important language in the world. And it's also one of the six official languages of the United Nations. There are lots of different types of Arabic. They are called 'dialects'. If I listen to some people from Egypt speaking Arabic, I might not understand them. But we can all speak to each other in Modern Standard Arabic. This is the type of Arabic you see in books and newspapers. You hear it on TV and on the radio and in mosques in many different countries. Arabic has a very long history. It is part of the Semitic family. Most other languages in this group are now dead, but not Arabic. In fact, lots of words in European languages come from Arabic, especially words in Science and Maths. English borrowed a lot of words from Arabic around **800 years ago**. One important reason why Arabic has survived is the Holy Qur'an. The language in the Holy Qur'an is very pure and it isn't very different from the Modern Standard Arabic we speak today. It has helped Arabic survive for a thousand years. …" The teacher then asks which English words come from Arabic; Adam: "**chess, sofa, … racket and cotton**." (The handwritten Arabic notes on the Lesson 7 page give a different figure than the spoken 230 million — filed in the differences list.)
- **Unit 2 opens.** T12: **Adam's school monologue** — he is in **Year 10**; new subjects Chemistry and Biology; favourite class Geography (a film about Antarctica; a report to write; a presentation on his trip to England to give).

- **Unit 2 (continued).** T13 = repeat of T12. T14/T15/T16: plural-pronunciation practice (/z/ letters, schools, days · /ɪz/ classes, languages, ages) and five sentences. T17: a boy reports the UNICEF lesson (in Lebanon children only have to go to school until they are 11; in some countries not at all; children with poor families have to work) — the audio companion to the L3 article. T18: **Denise's story, in full**: "I know how it feels not to go to school. I started school when I was 6. Then, when I was 8, my father died. We didn't have much money any more, so I had to leave school. I started working on a farm with my mother, picking coffee. It was hard work and I was always tired. Worst of all, I didn't know how to read or write. I was unhappy because many of my friends were going to school. I wanted to go to school too, but I earned very little money so I couldn't pay the school fees. And I couldn't buy a uniform to wear to school. It was too expensive. One day, a woman came to see my mother. She worked with an organization that helps children who want to go to school. She talked to my mother, then she asked me if I wanted to go to school again. I said yes. I was so happy I cried. Now I go to school every day. The organization pays for my school fees and my uniform. I am lucky that they came to help me. Now I can read and write and I am learning to do Maths. Most of all, I'm happy because I can go to university when I am older. I can get a good job and I never have to work on the farm again. **I want to be a doctor or a teacher so I can help children too when I grow up.**" T19: Denise's five key sentences repeated. T20: two men remember school — an **Indian man** (six children in a man-pulled rickshaw; no computers, an abacus for Maths) and an **Algerian man** (walked early to school; wrote on slates with chalk). T21 = repeat of T20. T22: **Sally Bishop and Maria Lennox** meet — Sally is the new Maths teacher; Maria teaches Biology; Sally's old job was teaching child actors on film sets, travelling everywhere. T23: Sally's two stories from that job — flying to **Italy** with two actors and nearly missing the plane over a forgotten passport; and giving a Maths exam to a student called **Ben**, who had been working on the film set all morning and fell asleep snoring over the paper.
- **Unit 3.** T24: **Tariq's family** (with Uncle Bashir) chooses a holiday — Tariq wants Egypt and the Pyramids; Uncle Bashir proposes Greece (he went two years ago); Mother rules out Greece in summer ("It's very hot") and proposes **England** — "We could go to **Stonehenge**." T25 = repeat of T24. T26: two phone calls. **Afriqiyah Airways**: Tripoli→London on **Friday 10th June**, flights at **6.20, 9.45 and 11.30** (Father first mishears 9.35; the clerk corrects him); return **30th June**. **Central Hotel**: double **£65**/night; single with shower **£60**; single without shower **£58**; June–August high season adds **£5**, so the single without shower costs **£63** on 10th June — matching the Lesson 6 checking box. T27 = repeat of T26 conversation 1; T28 = repeat of conversation 2. T29: four hotel scenes (Mrs Smith checks in, single with shower, 5th–7th April; Peter Smith in room 202 asks for a pillow and soap; a restaurant order — boiled eggs, toast, jam, tea; directions to the Natural History Museum on New Street). T30 = repeat of T29 conversations 3–4. T31: a cooked-breakfast order (apple juice replaces the finished orange juice) and Mr **John Adams** checking in for two nights; the gift shop opens at half past nine, closes at eight. T32: the porter's directions (football pitch five minutes via Bank Street to the river; bus stop round the corner; train station on Main Street past the car park; nearest restaurant on Park Avenue near the cinema, left on River Road opposite the museum) — the spoken version of Lesson 7. T33: four map directions from the hotel (school opposite the hotel; theme park on River Road past the restaurant; sports shop two minutes away on the corner of River Road and Main Street; fire station along the street on the right, near the library).
- **Unit 4.** T34: **Sami, Adam and Ahmad** debate mobile phones (Adam's parents won't let him have one; he missed his brother's 4 o'clock pick-up at the sports centre and waited an hour; phones don't work everywhere; leaving them on in cinemas and restaurants is impolite). T35 = repeat of T34. T36: **Ahmad's Eid account** — the lesson prints only his notes and first paragraph; the script carries the whole talk: "After that, we went to visit our family and friends. We gave them our gifts and all ate the special food. There was so much to eat! My aunt and uncle took some of the meat to the poor people in the city and gave them money too. In the afternoon, my cousins and I went to an amusement park and went on all the rides. **I had a fantastic day!**"
- **Unit 5.** T37: **Hala and Noor** go through Noor's birthday-party photos. The family settles: the bald, dark-complexioned man is her uncle; her father has wavy black hair; **Uncle Adel is her mother's brother**; **Aunt Reem** is shorter than her mother; cousins **Basma** (studying Physics at university, very bright, good marks) and **Rania**; niece **Amal is her brother Fuad's daughter**; and the "selfie" is Noor's **twin sister Rasha** — so the lesson's question "How many sisters has Noor got?" answers **one**. T38 = repeat of T37. T39: **Peter and Adam** choose a film at the Mall Cinema — Holiday (a comedy, "extremely funny"), Polar Bear (a documentary), and **A Mystery by the Nile** ("A museum guard has an unexpected adventure when an extremely rare mummy is stolen from the museum where he works"); they settle on the mystery at **6.30**, Adam picking Peter up at six. T40 = repeat of T39. T41: **Mustafa's family** (Amal, Yasmin, Yusef, Mother) plan his birthday present — CDs, a diary (Yasmin: "a terrible idea! It's so boring"), a **computer game** (the audio word; the Course Book's Lesson 3 print says "a PlayStation game" — filed in the differences list), Yusef's line "**He did? That's amazing! I haven't won that game once**," and Mother's decision: a mobile phone this year, more when he goes to university; Amal will get him a new shirt. T42 = repeat of T41. T43: five future-simple sentences for repetition. T44: six Mother-and-family exchanges (Nabil sure they will have time; Nabil will arrange the cake himself; the camera to the repair shop; Farah will order the food; Farah will go to the printer's for the invitations; Farah will help choose the flowers) — the audio for the Lesson 4 party planning. T45: **Waleed and Sean** on Welcome Home Week — Sean goes back to Ireland in August; Uncle (an oil worker in the Gulf) home for a whole month, plays the flute in an Irish band; cousin **Niall**, an aid worker in Africa, home for two weeks, playing Gaelic football with old school friends; Sean's mother excited because **her** mother is coming over from the United States with cousin **Maeve**, who is performing in an Irish dancing competition in Dublin; Aunt Mary, a History teacher, will give a talk on the history of the town.
- **Unit 6 opens.** T46: **Nadia the nutritionist, part 2** (audio-only; part 1 is the Lesson 1 print): "There are many things in picture 1 that can be bad for you. For example, the soft drink is not a healthy choice. It is important to drink a lot, but water and fruit juice are better than soft drinks. They often contain far too much sugar, and they also contain chemicals to give the drinks colour. The crisps have a lot of fat. Fats are bad for you, but the ones in crisps can be bad for your heart. Finally, it is not a good idea to eat too many sweet desserts. This is because sweet desserts often have a lot of calories. And if there are too many calories in your diet, you can become overweight. If you want something that tastes sweet, choose something that has few calories, like an apple." T47 = repeat of T46. T48: the four "How do you feel?" dialogues spoken (Salwa's flu; Sally's sprained ankle — "a few more days" in the bandage; Khalid's fall from a tree climbing after his kite; Tim's sunburn). T49: **Abdulla at the doctor's** (audio-only; the lesson page prints only his problem panels) — the doctor's advice: try to get **eight hours'** sleep (Abdulla sleeps six or seven); go to bed earlier; **don't do homework late at night** — do it in the afternoon or more at the weekend; vitamin pills only if the diet is unbalanced; try different vegetables (tomatoes, carrots, lettuce), raw ones too; and fruit every day — the script continues onto the next page with the five-a-day point, a blood test and a two-week return, filed with the next batch.

- **T49 ending (book p.121):** the doctor finishes — "And fruit, of course. You should try to eat **five things from the fruit and vegetable group every day**." Abdulla: "Five pieces! That's a lot. But, OK. I'll try to do that." On exercise: Abdulla used to enjoy basketball and swimming but is too tired now; the doctor: "You're too young to be so tired, Abdulla. I'll do a **blood test** just to make sure there's nothing wrong, but I think it's your lifestyle. You need to get more sleep and more exercise. You can go for a walk in the park every day even if you don't feel like doing anything else. And you should start eating fruit and vegetables." — "Come and see me again in **two weeks'** time. Now go to the nurse and get a blood test." T50 = repeat of T49. T51: the advice exchanges re-drilled as a listen-and-repeat (eight hours' sleep; homework in the afternoon; vitamin pills "depends on your diet"; "You should change your lifestyle."). T52: **five conversations at the chemist's** — **Noura** (cough syrup for her twelve-year-old daughter, three times a day; see a doctor only if the cough continues more than three days) · **Layla** (throat lozenges; cherry out of stock, lemon taken) · **Sultan** (bad sunburn from the beach; ice first, after-sun cream, aspirin if very painful) · **Adnan** (fell down the stairs and hit his head; sprained wrist; bandages) · **Yassir** (aspirin, cough syrup and throat lozenges; coughing about a week, feels hot; the chemist: "Sir, you really ought to see a doctor."). T53 = repeat of T52. T54: **Farah and Asma** plan summer sport (Farah to the seaside — swimming, sailing with a life-jacket, maybe scuba diving after only snorkelling before; Asma to a summer programme in England, advised to get a bicycle; they agree to play tennis together after the holidays). T55 = repeat of T54.
- **Unit 7.** T56: the Lesson 3 weather forecast, identical to the printed text. T57 = repeat of T56. T58: **Jaber's presentation spoken in the order D–B–A–C–E** (introduction, what a satellite is, what is in a satellite, what satellites are used for, closing) — the answer to the Lesson 7 ordering exercise. T59: the presentation's signpost sentences for repetition ("First I'll tell you what a satellite is. Then I'll talk about what is in a satellite. Finally, I'll talk about some of the things that satellites are used for…"). T60: **the Mercury text (audio-only)**: "Mercury is a rocky planet like the Earth. Mariner 10, a spacecraft, visited Mercury in 1974 and 1975. It took pictures of some parts of the planet. A new spacecraft was launched in 2004 and will start orbiting Mercury in 2011. It takes pictures of the rest of the planet." T61 = repeat of T60.
- **Unit 8.** T62: **Noura, Amani and their mother** work through the Lesson 3 jobs test — Noura wants to be a marine biologist ("I'd like a job where I can travel to different places and be outdoors"); Amani pushes pilot; Mother warns "Money isn't everything. It's important to do something that you really enjoy"; Amani would hate dentistry ("You have to work in the same place every day") and wants to be a doctor; the forester entry sends them to "**page 147**" for what foresters do — a page beyond this book's 141, so the reference is to another component, as printed. T63 = repeat of T62. T64: the two Lesson 4 invitation conversations, identical to the printed texts. T65 = repeat of T64.

## Key Words (book pp.126–139)

Per-unit alphabetical glossaries: word, part of speech, phonetic transcription, and a plain-English definition — the book's own dictionary of its taught vocabulary, roughly 40 words a unit (Unit 8's list is short, 9 words). Filed as a span, not word by word; the slips found in it are in the differences list below (Unit 1 carries "DOB (= date of birth) (n)"; Unit 4 tags "vaccination" as a verb; Unit 5 tags "once" as a noun defined "one time"; Unit 1 also glosses "monitor (v)" with the noun definition "a computer screen").

## Phonetic Symbols (book p.140) and back cover (book p.141)

The chart is the series-standard one; its /e/ keyword is printed correctly as "**men**" here (the Grade 9 Course Book's chart printed "mgn"). Back cover: the four components are **Course Book · Workbook · Teacher's Book · Audio** — no Test booklet is named on this cover; code **2000119**; "For more resources, visit: www.englishforlibya.com"; and the Arabic pupil line «عزيزي التلميذ محافظتك على الكتاب المدرسي قيمة حضارية».

## Differences, slips and notes — Grade 10 Course Book, as printed

1. **Track 41 vs Lesson 3 print (p.54):** the audio family chooses Mustafa "a **computer game**" and Yusef says "He did? That's amazing! **I haven't won that game once**"; the printed conversation on the lesson page says "a **PlayStation** game." Print/audio drift, each filed as found.
2. **Lesson 7 (p.15) handwritten Arabic notes** give a speaker figure that does not match Track 11's spoken **230 million**; the notes are a pupil's rough jottings reproduced in the artwork, filed as printed.
3. **Key Words slips:** Unit 4 lists "**vaccination (v)**" — a noun tagged as a verb; Unit 5 lists "once (n)" defined "one time"; Unit 1 glosses "monitor (v)" as "a computer screen"; Unit 1 carries the abbreviation entry "DOB (= date of birth)".
4. **Unit 8 Lesson 1 (p.90):** one speech mark is mis-set — the report runs "'Paul tried to stand up. I screamed in pain,' he said," putting the opening quote before the reporter's clause instead of before Paul's own "I screamed…".
5. **The Book Map spread (p.5)** sits under the Garnet watermark in this scan and its small type is largely illegible; the unit strands in this filing were taken from the unit dividers, lesson boxes and Round ups instead.
6. **The Wedding Customs article (Unit 5 Lesson 5):** two short stretches sit under the watermark (the groom's friends carrying him to the barber; part of the time-or-money sentence) and are quoted from the visible words, flagged in place in the lesson filing — not silently smoothed over.
7. **The Unit 5 Round up runs over two pages** (pp.60–61); p.61 completes the year's running "Now you can" list.
8. **Track 62 sends the girls to "page 147"** — beyond this book's 141 pages; the reference is to another component, as printed.
9. The back cover names only four components — **no Test booklet** on this cover (the Teacher Book's introduction is checked for it when that book is read).

**Libya Grade 10 Course Book — READ COMPLETE 2026-10-25.** Next per the queue: the Grade 10 Teacher Book (158 pp, already downloaded), then the Work Book.

# Libya — English Grade 10 (Secondary 1): Teacher's Book

**File:** `10_English_for_Libya_Teacher_Book.pdf` — 5,829,082 B, 158 pp, real %PDF, from the grade-10 record's URL `https://liasinstitute.com/PDFs/10_English_for_Libya_Teacher_Book.pdf` (collected 2026-10-22, approval source Libya CERC). Text extraction `teacher10.txt` is 21,646 chars — essentially only the repeated Arabic CERC rights footer — so the book was read visually from renders `teacher10-pages/` (158 PNGs at 100 dpi). **Page rule: book p. = PDF p. − 1** (Introduction opens on printed p.6 = PDF p.7).

**Identity:** "21st Century English for Libya — Secondary 1 Teacher's Book," State of Libya, Ministry of Education, Curricula and Educational Research Centre (CERC). Published by Garnet Publishing Ltd, 8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK. Imprint (book p.2): "First edition, copyright © 2019 Garnet Publishing Ltd" — the same year as its Course Book (the Grade 8 Teacher's Book was a year earlier than its Course Book; not here). No individual authors named. School year 2019–2020 (1440–1441 AH) on the cover, which carries the same white old-town building photograph as the Course Book, in black and white.

**Shape:** Contents (book p.4): Introduction p.6 · Unit 1 Language Learning p.16 · Unit 2 Education p.36 · Unit 3 Travel and Tourism p.56 · Unit 4 Fun with English 1 p.74 · Unit 5 Family and Friends p.86 · Unit 6 Health and Fitness p.106 · Unit 7 Science and Technology p.126 · Unit 8 Fun with English 2 p.144. A two-page Book Map (book pp.4–5) precedes the Introduction; like the Course Book's map, its small type sits degraded under the Garnet watermark in this scan and is largely illegible — nothing below is taken from it. Each unit's teaching notes open with a strand/skills overview page and a Key words page (the unit's words with phonetic transcriptions, matching the Course Book's Key Words), then lesson-by-lesson notes: an objectives box (Objectives · Language · Vocabulary · 21st Century skills · Reference, with suggested timings per stage), procedure notes, **every answer key**, and **all 65 audio scripts printed inline at their lessons**. A standing note under Unit 1 Lesson 1 explains the Key words lists: "This list enables you to set a vocabulary or spelling test at the end of each unit… The Course Book list includes a line next to each word, so that for any problematic words, you can ask students to write a sentence containing the word to remind them of its meaning."

## Introduction (book pp.6–15) — filed in full

- **§1 Overview.** "21st Century English for Libya is a modern, engaging course, which integrates a general English syllabus with an element of 21st century skills, such as study skills, teamwork, critical thinking and problem-solving." This level "has been developed for the teaching of English to students in Secondary 1… the students are starting to become young adults." Grammar "is approached in a more analytical way than in the earlier grades, as the students begin to analyze language patterns and look at detailed examples of the key language presented (see Grammar and Functions)" — for the same three reasons as Preparatory 2/3: the cognitive ability of students at this age; language needs constant recycling; and the state examination system's constraints. Communicative approach, communicative competence as the principal aim. The content reflects that students "will increasingly need to use English in the 'real world'… English as an international language."
- **§2 Approach to teaching.** Topic-based; a clear grammatical and functional syllabus; a coherent strand of skills development. Self-study is "an important strand of the course": learner diaries, vocabulary notebooks, regular homework. And the standing doctrine: "mistakes should be seen as part of the developmental process."
- **§3 Components of the course.** Course Book · Workbook · **Test booklet — NAMED here**, settling the question the Course Book's back cover left open: "The test booklet contains progress tests that can be used to test the students after each unit. Each test assesses the skills of listening, reading and writing, as well as specific language points and vocabulary from the unit." Students are guided through each test first; "they will be assessed not on their ability to understand written instructions, but on their listening, reading and writing skills." Task types listed (listening to a conversation and completing multiple-choice questions or writing notes; speaking in role-plays and using functional language to complain, agree, negotiate, etc.; reading and identifying topic sentences; reordering a conversation; matching sentence halves; completing and correcting sentences; matching words to pictures and definitions; completing an application form with personal information; writing an email). The teacher collects and marks all booklets and keeps a written record per student, breakable into sections (reading, listening, speaking, vocabulary, writing); mark allocation is the teacher's decision — "The actual allocation of marks is less important than the consistency of marking." **Website**: "A website hosting downloadable audio MP3s files, learning resources and training videos can be accessed at englishforlibya.com. The audio extracts are an essential part of the course." **Teacher's Book**: lesson plans laid out as a materials/objectives box plus staged notes with timings; "a total of 45 minutes per lesson. These timings are intended as a guide only."
- **§4 Organization.** "Units 1 to 3 and 5 to 7 are organized on a topic basis and present new language. Units 4 and 8 recycle language from the previous three units, presenting the language in context."
- **§5 Features of the course.** 5.1 Grammar and Functions — "Specially written pages at the back of the Course Book expand on the key language points in each unit." 5.2 Round up pages — students review before the tests; the teacher should be ready to write examples on the board or do oral practice of points students need to revise most. Homework activities; **Vocabulary notebooks** (methods of categorizing and arranging vocabulary are suggested in Unit 2; word maps and diagrams so students "do not always write Arabic translations"); **Using a dictionary** — students move to monolingual dictionaries, and "are shown how to do this in the very first unit of the Secondary course"; by year's end phonemic script is fully introduced, "an essential aid to pronunciation."
- **§6 Advice to teachers of teenagers.** 6.1 Teaching English to teenagers — seven characteristics to design around: (i) clearer personal goals; (ii) firmer opinions; (iii) greater interest in the outside world; (iv) importance of peer pressure — identify influential students "so that their leadership qualities can be used for the good of the class"; (v) understanding of abstract concepts; (vi) critical thinking; (vii) a more reflective attitude to information. 6.2 Lesson planning — read a whole unit's notes before teaching it; plan lessons at least a day in advance; approximate timings are guidelines; use realia (leaflets, photos, posters, timetables in English lessons). 6.3 Recycling language — routines for revision; the revisable-points list includes question tags ("Really? That's nice. Was it?"), opinion phrases ("What do you think? I think … I agree … I disagree. Maybe you're right."), describing location and giving directions, adjectives to describe experiences and places, a range of tenses, timetables, and imperatives and polite requests ("Let's work in groups. Could you help me, Zeena? Can you lend Nadia your book? Can I borrow your pen?"). 6.4 Organizing pairwork and group work — explain or demonstrate before splitting the class; an imaginary 'line' down the middle of the room or letters A/B to divide the class; similar-ability groups generally, with extra work ready for fast finishers. 6.5 Continuous assessment — written tests per unit plus assessment inside normal lessons; keep a written record of individual achievements; for speaking, pick four or five students to concentrate on before a lesson. The standing note, in the same words as the Preparatory books: "**It is important to assess a student's effort, not just the results that he/she achieves.**" 6.6 Homework — keep a record of who hands homework in on time; correction "should promote positive reinforcement at the same time as giving constructive criticism"; and the standing doctrine: "Instead of correcting errors of grammar, spelling or punctuation straight away, it is a good idea to **highlight mistakes** and get the students themselves to work out how to correct them."
- **§7 Teaching in 21st Century English for Libya, Secondary levels.** 7.1 Listening — text types (sounds/words/phrases; conversations; descriptions; narratives; discussions) and 14 sub-skills, from recognizing context to identifying speakers' feelings and attitudes; a basic six-step listening procedure; "The main aim of the tasks is to develop student's listening ability, not to test it." 7.2 Speaking — the year's competency list (greetings formal and informal; personal information; requests; preferences with reasons; arrangements; advice; views and opinions; gratitude; interrupting politely; showing interest; feedback and confirmation; describing people, places and things; checking and clarifying; simple explanations). 7.2.1 discussion/problem-solving work; 7.2.2 Extended turns — "By the end of Secondary 1, students should be able to produce about four sentences in a single conversational turn and around 20 sentences in a presentation." 7.3 Reading — 14 sub-skills (skimming, scanning, purpose, audience, headlines/topic sentences, extracting and collating, prediction, summarizing, guessing meaning, using language knowledge, following texts with unknown vocabulary, dealing with unknown vocabulary, text organization, active reading techniques). 7.4 Writing — "Each of the presentation units contains six writing tasks: Short writing tasks (50–70 words long); One longer end-of-unit writing task (80–120 words long)" — the same lengths as Preparatory 2 and 3. Text types produced: informal messages and postcards, letters, factual texts, descriptions, stories and extracts from stories, instructions, reports, questionnaires. Planning, editing, self-correction and redrafting "are essential skills for a successful writer"; finished work displayed; students edit each other's work collaboratively; and the writing sub-skills list (brainstorming, arranging, sequencing, layout, paragraphing, accuracy, register choice, punctuation, discourse markers and contrastive linkers, proofreading, editing, writing legibly).

## Unit 1 — Language Learning (book pp.16–35)

Strand overview (p.16) and Key words (p.17) as described in the header. Lesson notes cover all eight lessons and the Round up, with every Workbook key. Teacher-only material and settles:

- **Track 1 script difference (book p.18).** Speaker 2 in this book reads: "My parents came **from France to Algeria** when I was a baby. We go to **Algeria** every summer to visit my grandparents, so I can speak Arabic and French." The Course Book's printed transcript (its p.107) reads "came **to France from Libya**… We go to **Libya** every summer." The Teacher's Book version is the odd one on its face (a boy who moved from France to Algeria and speaks Arabic and French fits either way, but the two books name different countries). Filed as printed in both books — the year's first script split, of the same kind as Grade 8's Rashid/Waleed.
- **Track 11 script difference (book p.33).** Adam's Arabic talk here says Arabic is spoken by "about **420 million** people" and "It has helped Arabic survive for **1,400 years**." The Course Book's printed transcript (its p.110) says "**230 million**" and "survive for **a thousand years**." Everything else in the talk agrees word for word, including the teacher's closing question and Adam's "chess, sofa, … racket and cotton." (The Course Book filing noted the Lesson 7 page's handwritten notes give a figure that is not 230 million — the notes follow this book's 420 million.)
- A **model answer** for the Lesson 1 writing task (WB D): "I speak Arabic and French and I am learning English. I like watching English films and I want to go to America. I think it is hard to understand people who talk too fast in English. I often use a bilingual dictionary to help me learn new words."
- A **sample application form** (teacher-only model for WB E, Lesson 5): Surname: Azizi · Forenames: Fatima · DOB: 18/04/01 · Address: 19, Al Ghazaly Avenue, Tripoli, Libya · Phone: +218 21 645 767 · Email: fazizi01@gmail.com · "Why are you interested in our school?: I am interested in your school because I want to improve my English, make new friends and play sports. I really like football and I want to play in a team. With better English I can travel the world and study football everywhere!" The notes add that Adam's own form leaves a line in the postcode box "because he doesn't have a postcode."
- Lesson 2 keys settle the four trend paragraphs against the topic sentences (1→C, 2→B, 3→A, 4→D) and the graph matching (A2, B3, C1, D4).
- Lesson 7's research answers (WB D) add Arabic facts the class is expected to find: Arabic was written for the first time in the 4th century · Ethiopian is related to Arabic · more English words from Arabic: algebra, coffee, zero · about 420 million people speak Arabic as their first language (this book's figure again) · Arabic is the official language or co-official language in 26 countries, and a minority language in Cyprus, Iran, Mali, Niger, Senegal and Turkey.
- Lesson 8 keys: official languages — Greece–Greek, India–Hindi, Italy–Italian, Jordan–Arabic, Kenya–English, Pakistan–Urdu. The Canada assignment answers: official languages English and French; capital printed "**Ottowa**" (sic — the answer key's spelling); area 9,979,619 km²; landscape rivers, lakes, forests, glaciers and waterfalls (e.g., Niagara Falls).

Strand overview (p.36): comparisons both…and / neither…nor / as…as / like-unlike-too; obligation have to/don't have to; ability can/can't, could/couldn't; adjective + infinitive; past simple and continuous; past perfect simple and continuous (listed under Vocabulary in the overview, as printed); collocations (take an exam, watch a film). Key words p.37.

A teacher-only **model answer** for the write-about-yourself task is written as **Sarah, 14, from Libya**: "I go to Secondary School but she is still in Primary School. My school is in the middle of a very big city, but her school is in a much quieter town near our home. My favourite subject is Biology and one day I want to study medicine and become a doctor."
- Lesson 3 (UNICEF): the numbers-and-facts matching key (1b, 2d, 3c, 4a) and the Workbook true/false key: 1 False (UNICEF helps children all over the world) · 2 False (the F stands for *fund*) · 3 True · 4 False (UNICEF tries to make sure they go to school instead) · 5 False (UNICEF works with governments).
- Lesson 4: the Schools for All appeal answers — it helps poor children go to school (buys books, builds schools, makes schools safe), in Kenya; the reader should donate money. **Track 17's script here matches the Course Book transcript** (the boy who learned about UNICEF in class; in Lebanon children only have to go to school until they are 11).
- Lesson 5: **Track 18 (Denise's Story) matches the Course Book transcript word for word**, and Track 19 is its five-sentence link-word version (…because…, so…, and…, because…).

## Unit 3 — Travel and Tourism (book pp.56–73)

Workbook answers: she went with her family; the place about 5,000 years old; tourists had damaged the stones; a pharaoh named Khufu; she likes to visit old buildings.

Unit 3 lessons 3–8 and Round up (book pp.61–73) — keys and settles:

A pronunciation note: "Chamonix is pronounced /'ʃæmɒniː/."
- Lesson 4: **Track 26 matches the Course Book transcript in both conversations** — Afriqiyah Airways (father first mishears 9.35; the clerk corrects to 9.45) and the Central Hotel (double £65, single with shower £60, without shower £58, high season +£5, so £63 on 10th June) — confirming the Course Book's checking box. Tracks 27 and 28 are its repeats, as the Course Book marks them.

Tracks 32–33 are the porter's directions, keyed to the Course Book map (school opposite the hotel; theme park on River Road past the restaurant; sports shop two minutes away on the corner of River Road and Main Street; fire station along the street on the right, near the library).
- Lesson 8: a **teacher-only model answer** — Marco's email from Scotland: "Hi, Alberto! … I've been in Scotland for the last two weeks on holiday with my sister… We took a car to Eilean Donan castle. It's an amazing old castle on a tiny island in the middle of a loch… Camusdarach Beach, near the town of Arisaig… we could see three other islands from the beach… Edinburgh. That's the capital of Scotland. There were people playing bagpipes and they were wearing kilts."

It is not real life"; texting; email: "You can speak to anyone in the world for free… You need internet access"), and a **model essay on texting**: "The method of communication I have chosen to write about is SMS text messaging, or 'texting'… Texting is also cheaper because you don't need to pay for the internet… in public, sending a text is quiet and doesn't disturb other people… The main disadvantage of texting is that it makes people lazy… people don't write in proper sentences. Ahmad's essay-note answers: visited cousins in **Msillata** during Eid Aladha holidays; dressed in new clothes; went to mosque for prayers; thanked Allah. The Workbook's five-mistakes key corrects the paragraph (*to*, *much*, *took*, *went*, *had*).

The not-mentioned item is the bilingual dictionary. - Lesson 4: **Track 44 matches the Course Book's six party-planning exchanges** (Nabil — the time, having a cake made, taking the camera to the repair shop; Farah — ordering the food, getting the invitations printed at the printer's, help choosing flowers at the flower shop).
- Lesson 5 (Wedding Customs): the Workbook key settles the article's questions — 1 The Philippines · 2 henna · 3 **to the barber's** · 4 dance traditional dances · 5 purity — confirming from the key the groom-side content that sits under the watermark in the Course Book scan. A **teacher-only model answer** for the write-about-Libya task: "Traditionally, Libyan wedding celebrations can last for five days. However, nowadays they don't normally last longer than three. They are colourful events with bright clothes, music and lots of food. On the wedding day the newly-married couple eat their dinner separately from their guests. Wedding guests throw rice and sweets over the bride as she walks to her new home. Libyan brides often wear a number of different outfits over the different days of the wedding. Both the bride and the groom have designs painted on their skin in traditional red dye called henna."
- Lesson 6: **Track 45 matches the Course Book transcript**, and the relatives table key adds where each lives: Uncle **Patrick** — The Gulf, oil worker, one month, going to concerts; Cousin **Niall** — Africa, aid worker, two weeks, playing Gaelic football; Aunt **Mary** — USA, History teacher, a few days, giving a talk (on the history of the town); Cousin **Maeve** — USA, a few days, Irish dancing. Teacher notes give the pronunciations (Sean /ʃɔːn/, Niall /naɪl/, Maeve /meɪv/, Gaelic /'ɡeɪlɪk/) and the Gaelic-football note (players can use hands and feet; the other main difference is the scoring system).
- Lesson 7: card key — 1 birth announcement · 2 New Year card · 3 birthday invitation · 4 wedding invitation. Helen's-email key: she is going to have a little cousin; there was an argument about her name when she was born; she likes Alan and Aida; Alan means good-looking/handsome and Aida means very happy/joyful; she offered to look after the baby when it is born.
- Lesson 8 (the National Museum): Workbook key — 1 In Tripoli · 2 Pre-historic Libya · 3 6 · 4 Leptis Magna, Oea and Sabratha · 5 The ground floor · 6 A beautiful cup with a golden pattern on it. Photo key: all four pictures are artefacts — a wall painting, a tomb, a statue, a dagger.

## Unit 6 — Health and Fitness (book pp.106–125)

Strand overview (p.106): reported speech · advice modals could/should/ought to · may/might · health vocabulary, prefixes and affixes, compound words. Key words p.107.

Read the first part of her talk about nutrition") and the Track 46 speaker label. The Course Book names her **Nadia** (its Lesson 1 text and cast). **Track 46's words otherwise match the Course Book's audio-only part 2** (soft drinks with too much sugar and chemicals to give the drinks colour; crisps' fats bad for the heart; too many sweet desserts → too many calories → overweight; choose something with few calories, like an apple). A teacher-only model paragraph (banana pancakes) is given for the eating-habits writing task.

Unit 6 lessons 2–8 and Round up (book pp.110–125) — keys and settles:

- Lesson 2 (sleep article): Workbook key — 1 Eight hours · 2 You can get hurt or get bad marks · 3 During the last stage of sleep · 4 Exercise or watch adventure films · 5 Drinking a glass of (warm) milk. The reported-speech work names the article's doctor **Dr Ansari** ("We asked Dr Ansari if sleep was really important for teenagers. Dr Ansari said he was worried about many of the teenagers he saw in his office"), matching the Course Book.
- Lesson 3: **Track 48 matches the Course Book's four "How do you feel?" dialogues** (Salwa's flu; Sally's sprained ankle from falling off her bicycle; Khalid's arm after falling out of a tree climbing for his kite; Tim's sunburn). Picture order A3, B2, C4, D1; advice key — Salwa 4, Sally 5, Khalid 1, Tim 6, with advice 2 and 3 matching nobody. The weakest modal in the advice box is *could*.
- Lesson 4: **Track 49 matches the Course Book's audio-only doctor conversation in full** — eight hours' sleep; homework in the afternoon or more at the weekend; vitamin pills only if the diet is not balanced; try raw vegetables; **five pieces from the fruit and vegetable group every day**; a daily walk in the park; a blood test; come back in two weeks. Track 51 is its drill version with two boys. A teacher-only model paragraph is written about **Aya** ("My partner Aya does not have a very healthy lifestyle… She should also go to bed much earlier").
- Lesson 5: **Track 52 matches the Course Book's five chemist's conversations** (Noura — cough syrup for her twelve-year-old daughter, three times a day, see a doctor if the cough continues more than three days; Layla — lemon lozenges, the cherry ones come in the afternoon; Sultan — ice then after-sun cream for the sunburn, aspirin for the pain; Adnan — fell down the stairs, sprained wrist, bandage; Yassir — aspirin, cough syrup and lozenges, coughing about a week, ought to see a doctor). Track 53 is its repeat, as the Course Book marks it.
- Lesson 6: the fitness blog's paragraph topics — 1 Introduction (importance of regular exercise) · 2 Before you begin taking exercise · 3 Sports injuries · 4 Ways of avoiding injuries — and the injury key (not warming up; not wearing elbow/knee pads; trying something too hard too quickly; not listening to your body — drink water when thirsty, stop when in pain).
- Lesson 7: **Track 54 matches the Course Book transcript** (Farah to the seaside — sailing, snorkelling, scuba diving; Asma to a summer programme in England — might get a bicycle; tennis together after the holidays). Track 55 is its repeat.
- Lesson 8 (The Asian Games): the numbers key confirms the Course Book article figure by figure — **1951** year of the first Asian Games · **11** countries in the first Games · **6** sports in the first Games · **45** countries in the 15th · **39** sports in the 15th · **47** countries that showed the games on television · **2006** year of the 15th Asian Games · **9,704** athletes competing in the 2010 Games · **476** events in the 16th Asian Games. Quiz key adds: the first Games were in **India**; the 2010 Games in **Guangzhou, China**; new 2010 sports were cricket, dragon boat racing, dancesport and rollersports; volunteers did "various things, such as taking spectators to their seats, checking their tickets and giving information"; the torch "was carried round 23 cities and had an opening and closing ceremony"; the mascot answer is the 'five sporty goats'. A **model article** for the Health and Fitness Day writing task is given in full.

## Unit 7 — Science and Technology (book pp.126–143)

Strand overview (p.126): zero conditional · first conditional · passive voice · however/although/but · adverbs of certainty (perhaps, probably, definitely). Key words p.127.

- Lesson 1 keys: the five scientist texts match pictures 1B, 2C, 3D, 4A, 5E (as the matching key runs), with model definitions: "A marine biologist studies the plants and creatures that live in the sea. A forester works in a forest, taking care of the trees and planting new ones. An aerospace engineer builds aircraft (e.g., rockets), vehicles and equipment to be sent into space. A meteorologist studies the air around the Earth and how it changes, especially in order to predict the weather. A geologist studies the Earth, including the beginning and history of the rocks and soil."

Unit 7 lessons 2–8 and Round up (book pp.129–142) — keys and settles:

- Lesson 2 (Winds): article key — trade winds blow from the subtropics to the equator; the Shamal is a wind that blows in the Arabian Gulf and can cause sandstorms; satellites are used to predict hurricanes; wind happens when air moves from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure. Paragraph matching 1C, 2D, 3E, 4B.
- Lesson 3: **Track 56's forecast matches the Course Book transcript** — the dangerous weather is the United States' hurricane warning; the table key: UAE 38°/31° sunny, no rain · Saudi Arabia 36°/30° sunny, no · Libya 40°/30° cloudy, no · Egypt 28°/22° partly cloudy, no · United Kingdom 23°/13° cloudy, rain yes · United States — stormy, rain yes. A teacher-only model email (Alberto to Marco, about a running race) is given for the writing task.
- Lesson 4 (Aljabal Alakhdar): the key settles Layla's email — she is staying **with a family in Almarj**; the area is one of the few forested areas in Libya and one of the wettest; it is home to **more than 200 thousand people**, most working in farming; the plant (in the large forest) can bloom through the winter and has many medical uses, including treating liver and digestive problems and diabetes; **the Greeks** were there; "It is an amazing place rich in history and resources."
- Lesson 5 (petroleum): picture key — rock samples, radar map, derrick, pipeline, refinery, petrol station; the process order 1b, 2d, 3e, 4c, 5a; passive examples keyed from the text ("Petroleum is not found everywhere… Animals and plants died and were covered by layers of mud and water… When petroleum has been found… cleaner products are being developed by petroleum companies").
- Lesson 6 (online shopping): the key's verdict — "(There is no definite answer, but the last paragraph indicates that the writer is mostly in favour of it)"; a sample summary (under 90 words) is given; the 'it' in lines 7 and 10 refers to online shopping.
- Lesson 7: **Track 58 is Jaber's satellite presentation, and the paragraph-order key (1D, 2B, 3A, 4C, 5E) confirms the spoken order D–B–A–C–E** recorded in the Course Book filing. A **sample presentation about Jupiter** is given in full (teacher-only): fifth planet from the sun; 588 million km from Earth; 142,984 km wide; 63 satellites/moons, discovered by Galileo in 1610; 90% hydrogen and 10% helium; the Great Red Spot; Pioneer 10 flew by in 1973; Galileo orbited for eight years until destroyed in 2003. Track 59 drills the signpost expressions.
- Lesson 8: **Track 60's Mercury text matches the Course Book's audio-only text** (rocky planet; Mariner 10 visited in 1974 and 1975; new spacecraft launched in 2004, orbiting from 2011). The faulty-summary exercise's corrected version is given as the answer: "You can recognize the hawksbill turtle because of its pretty *shell* and its unusual mouth, which looks like a *beak*. It eats sea animals and *plants*. It comes out of the ocean only to *lay its eggs*. These sea turtles are protected, but they are endangered by *pollution*. We can all help protect them by being more careful with our rubbish." A **model report, "The Narwhal,"** is given in full (tusk about 3.1 m on a male; Arctic waters around Canada, Greenland and Russia; can live up to 50 years; Inuits hunt them; North Atlantic oil mining has changed their habitat).

## Unit 8 — Fun with English 2 (book pp.144–154) and back cover

Revision unit: overview (p.144). Its Key words page (p.145) lists only **7 words** (astronaut, dentist, gravity, hiker, nutritionist, receiver, satellite) — a different, shorter list than the Course Book's 9-word Unit 8 glossary, and not the same words (the Course Book lists GPS receiver, hand-held, hiking, snowstorm and spacesuit, which are not here; dentist, gravity and nutritionist are here and not there).

- Lesson 1 (GPS): the Paul Dupont story key — 1 unexpected · 2 because it was autumn, not winter · 3 he was experienced · 4a lost his footing, 4b gathering speed, 4c came to a stop. The how-GPS-works key: used by fishermen and people who do outdoor sports; satellites; maps. Reported-speech answers from the reporter's notes ("Paul said his leg was in a plaster cast and he felt much better. He said he thought the mountain-rescue team was fantastic. He said he couldn't wait to go home. He said he might come back to Scotland, but in the spring.").
- Lesson 2 (astronaut interview): main points 1, 3, 4 and 7; supporting details — muscles can lose up to 20% of their strength and bones get weaker and can break; special exercise machines; food with calcium and a balanced diet; masks and earplugs against light and noise. A sample summary is given.
- Lesson 3 (jobs test): **a second name split.** This book's Track 62 speakers are **Mona and Sarah** with their mother — Mona wants to be a marine biologist; Sarah floats being a dentist and then a doctor ("Money isn't everything" is the mother's line); the forester entry is read from "**page 147**" here too. The Course Book's printed transcript for Track 62 names the girls **Noura and Amani**. Jobs mentioned key: marine biologist, pilot, dentist, doctor, forester.
- Lesson 4: **Track 64 matches the Course Book transcript** (Yousif–Adam — the dentist this afternoon, the beach tomorrow morning; Kamal–Yousif — grandparents all day, the comedy at 6.00 tonight, meet outside the cinema). Track 65 is its repeat.
- Two blank pages (book pp.155–156) precede the **back cover (book p.157)**: components = **Course Book, Workbook, Teacher's Book, Audio** — the same four as the Course Book's back cover, **no Test booklet named on this cover either**; code **21000101**; "For more resources, visit: www.englishforlibya.com".

## Differences and slips found in this book

1. **Track 1, speaker 2 (book p.18): Algeria/France here, Libya/France in the Course Book transcript** — see Unit 1. The year's first script split.
2. **Track 11 (book p.33): 420 million Arabic speakers and 1,400 years here; 230 million and a thousand years in the Course Book transcript** — see Unit 1.
3. **The nutritionist is Wijdan here, Nadia in the Course Book** — see Unit 6.
4. **Track 62's girls are Mona and Sarah here, Noura and Amani in the Course Book transcript** — see Unit 8.
5. The answer key prints Canada's capital "**Ottowa**" (Unit 1, Lesson 8) — a spelling slip in the key.
6. "Read Grammar and **Funtions** on page 98" (Unit 2, Lesson 7 notes) — a typo in a stage heading.
7. This book's Unit 4 and Unit 8 Key words lists do not match the Course Book's Key Words glossaries (Unit 4: 14 words here, and "**vaccination (v)**" is mis-tagged as a verb in both books; Unit 8: 7 words here against the Course Book's 9, with only astronaut and satellite in both).
8. Every other script checked — Tracks 3, 5, 6, 8, 9, 12, 17, 18, 20, 22, 23, 24, 26, 29, 34, 36, 37, 39, 41, 44, 45, 48, 49, 52, 54, 56, 58, 60, 64 and all the marked repeats — **agrees with the Course Book's printed transcripts**. Audio reaches Track 65, matching the Course Book exactly.
9. The Introduction names a **Test booklet** as a course component (progress test after each unit), as the Preparatory teacher's books did. It is **not in the grade-10 library record** and is named on **neither** book's back cover, both of which list only Course Book, Workbook, Teacher's Book and Audio.

# Libya — English Grade 10 (Secondary 1): Work Book

**File:** `10_English_for_Libya_Work_Book.pdf` — 4,689,265 B, 100 pp, real %PDF, from the grade-10 record's URL `https://liasinstitute.com/PDFs/10_English_for_Libya_Work_Book.pdf` (collected 2026-10-22, approval source Libya CERC). Text extraction `work10.txt` is 13,700 chars (partial); the book was read visually from renders `work10-pages/` (100 PNGs at 100 dpi) and checked exercise by exercise against the Course Book and Teacher's Book. **Page rule:** printed folios match PDF pages 1–11; **folio 12 does not appear in this file** — PDF p.12 carries folio 13, and from there book p. = PDF p. + 1 (so the book's last numbered page, 98, is PDF p.97; two unnumbered pages and the back cover close the file).

**Identity:** "21st Century English for Libya — Secondary 1 Workbook," State of Libya, Ministry of Education, CERC. Published by Garnet Publishing Ltd, Reading RG1 4QS, UK. Imprint (book p.2): "First edition, copyright © 2019 Garnet Publishing Ltd" — the whole Secondary 1 set is © 2019. Photography: Amiri Diwan; Clipart.com; Corbis: Bettmann; istockphoto.com; shutterstock.com. No individual authors named.

**Shape:** Contents (book p.3): Unit 1 Language Learning p.4 · Unit 2 Education p.17 · Unit 3 Travel and Tourism p.30 · Unit 4 Fun with English 1 p.43 · Unit 5 Family and Friends p.48 · Unit 6 Health and Fitness p.63 · Unit 7 Science and Technology p.77 · Unit 8 Fun with English 2 p.89. It is the Course Book's practice twin: every lesson's exercises in Course Book order, keyed to the same tracks (its listening exercises carry the same track numbers), with its own short texts where the exercise needs one. **No back matter of its own** — no tapescript, no wordlist; the Teacher's Book holds the keys.

## Work-Book-only texts, in full

- **The two study-programme posters (Unit 1 Lesson 4, book p.9)** — printed only here: **The Fisher School** — "We are a small school in the English countryside. We offer classes at all levels, with no more than six people per class. Come and improve your English with us. Do sports and arts activities too. You will stay in our dormitories with other students your age. It's a great way to meet people and make new friends. We are open to students between 14 and 18 years old. For more information, call us on **+44 18 8793 9120**." · **Study Abroad** — "Come to our school in London · close to museums and monuments · morning or afternoon classes · maximum 15 students per class · stay with a family near the school · open to students 16–25. For more information, see our website **www.thetowerschool.edu**."
- **Abdulla and Liam (Unit 2 Lesson 2, book p.19)** — the comparison pair the Teacher's Book keys work from: "Abdulla starts school at 8.00. His favourite subject is Chemistry. He goes to school from Sunday to Thursday. He is in Year 10. His brother drives him to school every day. The journey to school takes 30 minutes. There are 300 students in his school. Abdulla plays basketball and football at school." · "Liam starts school at 8.30. His favourite subject is Chemistry. He goes to school from Monday to Friday. He is in Year 10. He takes the bus to school. The bus ride takes 45 minutes. There are 200 students in Liam's school. He plays football and tennis at school."
- **Amal's story (Unit 2 Lesson 7, book p.26)** — the past-tense gap-fill story, verbs as keyed by the Teacher's Book: "Amal was very good at the piano. She *wanted* to be a famous musician. One day, when she *was reading* a magazine, she *saw* an advertisement for a special school for musicians. She *sent* them a letter. A week later, the telephone *rang* while she *was doing* her homework. Amal *answered* the phone, then *ran* into the kitchen, where her mother *was cooking* dinner. 'I got in!' she *shouted*."
- **Sarah's day and Julie's day (Unit 2 Lesson 8, book pp.28–29)** — the two past-perfect practice texts (Sarah's exam that never happened because the teacher had left her papers at home; Julie's bad day — the bicycle puncture, Mariam angry after waiting 15 minutes, the bakery closed), told in full on the pages and keyed in the Teacher's Book.
- **The Egypt itinerary and the museum/shuttle posters (Unit 3 Lesson 5, book pp.36–37)** — printed only here: "Best Travel Tours Itinerary: Visit to Egypt May 3rd – 6th" — May 3rd: leave 10.15 a.m., arrive Cairo, shuttle bus to hotel 8.00 p.m.; May 4th: morning Tour of the Pyramids, afternoon Visit the Khan El Khalili Bazaar, evening Dinner on river boat 6.00 p.m. with a special menu; May 5th: morning Egyptian Museum — "See the treasure of Tutankhamun!" — then to lunch, afternoon relax at the hotel or go on a camel ride. · **Natural History Museum** poster: "Opening hours: Monday to Sunday, 10 a.m. to 9 p.m." · **Free shuttle bus service** poster: Saturday to Thursday 0900, 0930, 1000, 1030, 1100, 1130, 1200 … until 2200; Friday 1000, 1200, 1400, 1600, 1800, 2000. (The Teacher's Book keys for the poster questions: opens 10 a.m., closes 9 p.m., open seven days, six times on Friday, last bus Wednesday 2200.)
- **Ahmad's Eid paragraph, with its five planted mistakes (Unit 4 Lesson 3, book p.45)** — printed only here, as the correct-the-mistakes exercise (the mistakes are the Teacher's Book's key: *to, much, took, went, had*): "After that, we **went visit** our family and friends. We gave them our gifts and we all ate the special food. There was **so many** to eat! My aunt and uncle **take** some of the meal to the poor people in the city and gave them money too. In the afternoon, my cousins and I went to an amusement park and **go** on all the rides. I **has** a fantastic day!" Corrected, it is Track 36's second paragraph — the same words the Course Book transcript prints as audio-only material.
- **Fatin's blog, "A New Friend" (Unit 5 Lesson 1, book p.50)** — printed only here: "I met someone **really nice** yesterday. Her name is Nora. I met her at my cousin Hamid's birthday party. Hamid is only three, so my aunt asked me to come and help watch the children. It was **absolutely exhausting**! There were 20 children there and it was **extremely noisy**. Nora was there with her little brother, but she didn't seem to mind the noise at all. She was **incredibly patient**. While the children were playing, I had a chance to talk to her a little. I was a little nervous at first because she looked really smart and a bit serious, but after a few minutes I realized she is **extremely lively** and fun to talk to. She is also **incredibly bright**. She is just my age, but she is already studying Chemistry at university. I hope we will become good friends. Bye for now, Fatin" (the bold words are the lesson's intensifiers, bold in the book).
- **Somar's email (Unit 7 Lesson 3, book p.80)** — a gap-fill email printed only here: From Somar, To Khalid, Subject "Re: Tomorrow" — "Hi Khalid, Thanks for your email. I don't know what I'm doing tomorrow yet. If my father ___ me to help him fix the car, I'll have to be at home all afternoon. But I don't know if he's got the part he needs. If he ___ it, I ___ free to go to the cinema. But have you heard the weather forecast? I think it ___ a beautiful day. I ___ the beach to the cinema if the weather ___ nice. Let's talk tomorrow and decide then. Somar" (box: *doesn't have, is, prefer, needs, might be, 'll be* — the first conditional practised inside a real email).
- **The Mercury text (Unit 7 Lesson 7, book p.87)** — Track 60 printed here in full as the pause-marking exercise: "Mercury is a rocky planet like the Earth. Mariner 10, a spacecraft, visited Mercury in 1974 and 1975. It took pictures of some parts of the planet. A new spacecraft was launched in 2004 and started orbiting Mercury in 2011. It takes pictures of the rest of the planet." It agrees word for word with both other books' scripts.

## Checks against the other two books

- **Track 62's speakers are pictured and named here as Mother, Sarah and Mona (Unit 8 Lesson 3, book p.94)** — the Workbook sides with the Teacher's Book's names (Mona wants a job that lets her travel / the marine biologist in the Teacher's Book script; Sarah: "I want to help people"; Mother: "Money isn't everything. It's important to have a job you enjoy") against the Course Book transcript's Noura and Amani. The name split is therefore two books against one.
- **CORRECTION to the Teacher's Book filing (Unit 4 Lesson 1 visitor table):** the Teacher's Book section of this file records the trend key as "China ↘". That was a misread of the small printed arrow. The Workbook prints the table itself in two halves (Student A, book p.43: France ↗, Spain ↗, United States ↘; Student B, book p.44: China ↗, Mexico ↘, Italy ↘) with the model exchange "Is the number of visitors to China falling?" — "No, it's going up," and re-checking the Teacher's Book key (its book p.78) shows China ↗ there too. **Correct key: rising — France, Spain, China; falling — United States, Mexico, Italy.** The Teacher's Book section line is corrected in the same commit as this filing.
- Everything else checked exercise by exercise agrees with the Course Book and the Teacher's Book keys — the Fisher School call (Track 6/7), the Abdulla/Liam comparison, Denise's story answers, the hotel conversations, the party-planning exchanges (Track 44), the doctor's advice T/F set (Track 50 — its statement 5 here reads "You could do your homework in the evening," where the Teacher's Book's false-version note paraphrases "in the afternoon or at the weekend"; the audio drill, Track 51, says "in the afternoon"), the Asian Games numbers, the GPS reporter's notes ("leg in plaster cast, but feels fine · thinks mountain-rescue team were fantastic · can't wait to go home · may come back to Scotland, but in the spring"), and the astronaut interview's seven statements (main points 1, 3, 4, 7, as the Teacher's Book keys).

Code **20000120**. "For more resources, visit: www.englishforlibya.com"; the Arabic pupil line «عزيزي التلميذ محافظتك على الكتاب المدرسي قيمة حضارية»; printed by International Press, Lebanon.

# Libya — English Grade 10 (Secondary 1): FULLY READ 2026-10-25

**Libya Grade 10 — FULLY READ 2026-10-25** (Course Book + Teacher Book + Work Book; the grade-10 record's three books). The course names a fourth component, a **Test booklet** (named only in the Teacher's Book's Introduction), which is not in the library record. The grade's grammar spine: present simple/continuous revision · indirect questions · -ed/-ing adjectives · comparatives/superlatives, as…as, both/neither · past simple/continuous/perfect/perfect continuous · present perfect with ever/never/yet/already · articles and at/on/in · present perfect continuous · invitations/offers/promises · future simple · get/have something done · determiners few/some/both/all · future continuous · verb + to-infinitive · reported speech · advice modals should/ought to/could · prefixes/suffixes · may/might · zero + first conditionals · adverbs of certainty · the passive (present simple/continuous, past simple, present perfect, future) · however/although/but. The year's four cross-book splits, all verified firsthand: Track 1 (Teacher's Book Algeria vs Course Book Libya), Track 11 (Teacher's Book 420 million / 1,400 years vs Course Book transcript 230 million / a thousand years), the nutritionist (Teacher's Book Wijdan vs Course Book Nadia), Track 62 (Teacher's Book + Work Book Mona and Sarah vs Course Book transcript Noura and Amani). Next per the queue: **Libya Grade 11** — record check first, then the re-cover check, one book at a time.

# Libya — English Grade 11 (Secondary 2), Literary Section: Course Book

**File:** `11-1_English_for_Libya_Course_Book.pdf` — 20,146,040 B, 218 pp, real %PDF, from the grade-11 record's URL `https://liasinstitute.com/PDFs/11-1_English_for_Libya_Course_Book.pdf` (approval source Libya CERC). Text extraction `course11-1.txt` is 29,866 chars (partial); the book is read visually from renders `course11-1-pages/` (218 PNGs at 100 dpi). **Page rule: book p. = PDF p. − 1** (verified against the printed folio across the whole book — e.g. U1 L1 folio 8 = PDF p.9, U2 L1 folio 20 = PDF p.21, Key Words folio 148 = PDF p.149).

**The grade-11 shape, new this year:** Libyan Secondary splits into sections, and the grade-11 record holds **six** English books — a full set (Course Book + Teacher Book + Work Book) for the **Literary Section** (`11-1`) and a second full set for the **Science Section** (`11-2`). This is the Literary set.

**Identity:** "21st Century English for Libya — Secondary 2 Course Book — Literary Section (القسم الأدبي)," State of Libya, Ministry of Education, CERC. Cover: the Roman theatre at Sabratha. Garnet Publishing Ltd, Reading RG1 4QS, UK. Imprint (book p.3): "First edition, copyright © 2019 Garnet Publishing Ltd." Photography: Alamy, iStockphoto, arabianEye, Shutterstock, Superstock, Getty Images, Science Photo Library, Lebrecht Photo Library. School year 2019–2020 (1440–1441 AH). No individual authors named.

**Shape:** eight units — six teaching units of eight lessons each plus a Round up (U1 Culture and Customs · U2 Communication · U3 The Environment · U5 Food and Drink · U6 The Media · U7 Leisure) and two four-lesson revision units (U4 Fun with English 1, U8 Fun with English 2). Back matter (Contents, book p.4): Grammar and Functions p.98 · Checklist for Written Work p.107 · Transcript p.108 · Key Words p.128 · Phonetic Symbols p.149 · **Readers p.150** — graded reader stories bound into the back of the book, a new component that explains this book's 218 pages against Secondary 1's 141. The Book Map spreads (book pp.5–6) print the four strands per unit, but their small type sits under the Garnet watermark in this scan and is largely illegible, as in Secondary 1; strand detail is taken from dividers, lesson boxes and Round ups.

## Unit 1 Culture and Customs (book pp.6–17)

- **L1 Mother's Day (p.8).** Track 1 dialog, in full: **Fareed** asks **Alex** why he is writing to his mother when they live in the same house — "Well, it's Mother's Day tomorrow… it's a day in the spring when you say thank you to your mother for everything. Most people give their mother a card and a present." Alex has flowers and chocolates; his card carries the poem "On this special day, I'm writing to say, you're the best mother in every way." Fareed: "Don't you send Mother's Day cards in Libya?" — "Not really. We send congratulations and birthday cards but we never send Mother's Day cards." Fareed leaves "To buy a Mother's Day card for my mum. She's also the best!" Functional Grammar: present simple vs present continuous (facts/habits vs now), ref p.98.
- **L2 Ramadan in Libya (p.9).** **Samira's email to Claire** (gap-fill with six phrases), in full: "Thanks for your email. You asked me about [traditions in my country]. Well, at the moment it's Ramadan, so I'll tell you about that. Ramadan is a very special time for us. It's a thirty-day religious fast from dawn to sunset. So there's no eating, drinking or smoking [all through the day]. During Ramadan, Muslim people spend more time reading the Holy Quran and attending religious lessons and sermons. It's a time when we try to pray more, to be extra patient with each other, and be kind and generous to people who are less fortunate than us. At sunset, we stop fasting, and after prayers, [we have an evening meal]. It's called iftar. My mother is a very traditional person. (She's also a little bit bossy, [but that's another story]!) Anyway, she makes sure that iftar in our house is a very traditional meal. We have things like dates, milk, fruit juices and a traditional Libyan soup. The main course is usually a stew with meat or chicken and vegetables, served with rice. Then we have tea and cakes. During Ramadan, we always have lots of pastries in the house [to offer visitors in the evening]. One thing I really like about Ramadan here in Tripoli is that people are more sociable. Families and friends [spend a lot more time with each other] in the evenings and neighbours share food. After iftar, people go to the mosque to take part in the Taraweeh and Tahajud night prayer. Towards the end of the month, some people can feel quite tired. But people don't get bad-tempered because Ramadan is so important for everyone. Even when people do feel hungry or tired, they are easy-going and remain calm." PS: "Here's a photo of you and me in Liverpool last year!"
- **L3 Have you got a problem? (p.10).** Track 4 phone call gap-fill: **Hadi** phones his **Mum** — "I've had a scooter [accident]… I haven't hurt myself badly. I've only broken my glasses and cut my [hand]… A [cat] ran in front of the scooter. I tried to stop but the road was wet and I crashed into a [tree]." Mum: "I really [hate] that scooter of yours… I'm coming to [help] you now." Functional Grammar: present perfect simple vs past simple, ref pp.98–99.
- **L4 A Trip to Scotland (p.11).** Photo matching (a castle about 400 years old; **Ian's son** caught a huge fish; the loch near **Ian Maclaren's** castle; Scottish music) and have-you-ever conversations ("Have you ever been to a theme park? — Yes, I have. I've been to Aladdin's Kingdom").
- **L5 You haven't met him, have you? (p.12).** Track 8: **Khalid** and **Martin** (Martin staying with Khalid; Ramadan; iftar; Hasan not yet met). Functional Grammar: question tags, ref p.99.
- **L6 What have they been doing? (p.13).** Track 10 table: Huda — "She's been taking photos"; Adam, Anne Marie, Rob & Mark, Issa, Nadia. Functional Grammar: present perfect continuous; for (period) vs since (start point), ref p.100.
- **L7 Ghadames Festival (pp.14–15).** Two texts: (1) the online article **GHADAMES FESTIVAL**, in full: "Ghadames is one of Libya's most historically important cities. The city of Ghadames was not only a major route, but it is also home to a fresh water spring that kept the town's oasis full. It hosts the Ghadames Festival, which is held in mid-October each year. The festival is a vibrant event where townsfolk meet to eat, drink, sing and dance. It is truly a wonderful experience for those visiting the country to witness such a celebration. Visitors are urged to join in and enjoy the festivities. The festival is a celebration of local Tuareg culture and traditions. The festival starts with an opening ceremony on the first day, followed by exhibitions of traditional industries on the second day. The third day includes races and an introduction to Tuareg life. The third day concludes with a feast of dates, and dancing. The Ghadames Festival revolves around the town's premier export, dates. The festival converts the whole town into a market place, holding horse and camel races outside the city walls. The original Ghadames Festival held by the residents of Ghadames is normally held in October. The Tuareg festival occurs at the beginning of September each year, delivered by Tuaregs living outside the old city." (2) **Bilal's email to Mike**, in full: "Thanks for your email. I'm having a great time at the moment. We've got the Ghadames Festival here this month, so there's lots to see and do. I've been to three camel races so far this week! Two of the races were won by the same camel. They were both excellent races. I have also been to the date and spice market. It has an amazing selection of the best spices. I've also attended an interesting performance of traditional dancing and music. It was an amazing show. The festival really reflects the local culture. I now want to be a historian and professional photographer! I want to study more about the rich history of Ghadames and its people. I also want to take beautiful photographs of the local area, its festivities and nature, to share with the world. Write to me soon, Bilal. P.S. I'm sending you my schedule about my experiences at the Ghadames Festival."
- **L8 Describing Yourself (p.16).** The "What are you really like?" quiz (10 questions) and adjective + preposition phrases (good at, interested in, annoyed with, proud of…), ref p.101. Model: "My friend Ameena isn't very good at sport, but she's brilliant at art. I like her because she's honest with everyone…"
- **Round up (p.17)** — "Now you can": talk about routines/habits (present simple) · talk about what is happening now (present continuous) · check information (question tags) · talk about recent experiences (present perfect) · talk about recent activities (present perfect continuous) · talk about feelings (adjectives with prepositions) · keep conversations going by showing interest ("Really? That's nice. Not really. Oh no! How about you?").

## Unit 2 Communication (book pp.18–31)

Divider spread (book pp.18–19): lessons — L1 Keeping in Touch p.20 · L2 Giving Instructions p.21 · L3 The Swimmer p.22 · L4 On the Phone p.24 · L5 Time and Place p.25 · L6 The Internet p.26 · L7 They must have forgotten p.28 · L8 Advertising p.29 · Round up p.30.

- **L1 Keeping in Touch (p.20).** Communication words (e-card, text message, chatting on a mobile phone, mobile game, laptop, camera phone); text-speak decoding; Track 13 — six speakers (Simon, Kamal, Nadia, Ameera, Aziz, Nisrin) matched to pictures.
- **L2 Giving Instructions (p.21).** Track 15 dialog, in full: **Ammar** hurries **David** — "My hair's still wet." — "Here! Don't waste time with a towel. Use this hairdryer." — "Is this thing yours?" — "Of course not. It's my sister's." — "How does it work?" — "It's simple. Plug it in and turn it on." — "Turn it on?" — "Yes. Press the black button to turn it on." — "But what's this red button?" — "The red button has got three positions. If you push it down, you get cool air. If you keep it in the middle, you get warm air. And you get hot air if you push it up." — "OK. I suppose I want cool air, don't I?" — "No. Don't use the cool setting. It's best to select hot if you want your hair to dry fast." — "You really know a lot about it, don't you? Are you any good at fixing computers?" Functional Grammar: giving instructions — imperatives + zero conditionals, ref p.101.
- **L3 The Swimmer (pp.22–23).** Six texts to order — the Omar Zeid story: (D) Omar's letter to **Marjory's Problem Page**, in full: "Dear Marjory, I am writing to you because I am 16 but I can't swim and I'm frightened of the sea. When I was seven, my twin sister drowned in an accident at the beach. After that my mother never allowed me to go swimming. But now I want to learn to swim. Is it too late? Omar" · (A) Marjory's reply: "Dear Omar, It is not too late for you to learn to swim. Many swimming pools have classes for people who are afraid of water. Join one of these classes and say goodbye to the past. Good luck! Marjory" · (C) the Oasis Sports Centre advert: "Swimming classes for teenagers — Are you afraid of water? Do you want to learn to swim? Why not join one of our classes? Complete beginners: Tuesday and Thursday 6.00–8.00 p.m. Intermediate swimmers: Wednesday and Friday 6.00–8.00 p.m." · (E) Omar's blog, 10th July: "I have paid for a swimming course. My first class is next Tuesday. I am really scared. But I am going to be strong. I AM GOING TO LEARN TO SWIM. NOTHING IS GOING TO STOP ME NOW." · (B) the news article **SAVED BY A TEENAGE HERO**, in full: "Gemma Green nearly drowned yesterday at Sennen Cove, Cornwall. Seven-year-old Gemma was playing on the beach when a huge wave pulled her out to sea. Seventeen-year-old Omar Zeid was walking on the beach with a friend when it happened. 'One minute the little girl was playing on the sand and the next minute she was far out at sea,' he told reporters. Omar's friend **Justin Cartwright** said, 'The water was rough and we could see she was in trouble so I phoned the coastguards on my mobile. I didn't think it was a good idea to go in after her. But Omar didn't waste a minute. He dived into the water, swam out to her and brought her back. He's a real hero.' Omar, who only learnt to swim last year, said, 'I didn't feel scared. I just knew I had to save her. My twin sister drowned when she was seven and it was the worst day of my life. This is the best day of my life.'" · (F) Omar's email to Justin inviting him on a Cornwall camping holiday: "You won't believe this but I have learnt to swim. In fact, I am writing to ask you to come on holiday with me in Cornwall. We'll camp near the beach and go swimming every day. I hope you can come. I know we'll have a great time. Omar. P.S. Here are some pictures of sunny Cornwall!"
- **L4 On the Phone (p.24).** Sixteen phone phrases sorted caller vs answerer ("Hello. Is that Ian Murphy?" · "May I ask who's calling?" · "I'd like to speak to Salwa Halabi, please" · "Is that 0207-681-432?" · "Please speak clearly after the tone").
- **L5 Time and Place (p.25).** Functional Grammar: *so* + adjective, *such* + adjective + noun, and cause/result with so/such … that, ref p.101.
- **L6 The Internet (pp.26–27).** The article **INVENTIONS THAT HAVE CHANGED THE WORLD**, in full: "What do you do if you want to know about a high school volleyball team in Beirut? Who can your brother ask if he wants to find a language school in Scotland where he can study English this summer? Where can your sister get the latest information if she has to write an essay about world climate change? The answer is simple. You go onto the internet and look for a useful website. The internet — sometimes called the World Wide Web (www) — is a huge international network of computers. It was invented in 1990 by two computer scientists — Tim Berners-Lee from Britain and Robert Cailliau, from Belgium. At the time, they were both working at a physics laboratory in Switzerland. They invented the system to help scientists to share ideas and information. In 1993 the internet became public and it grew very fast. Today it is used by millions of people all over the world and its use is still growing. To go online, all you need is a computer and an internet router or wi-fi. And if you look at the statistics, most teenagers in the United States are connecting regularly. A recent survey showed that 82% of teenagers are sending and receiving email. Most of these also say they frequently surf the net for information. Another popular online activity among teens is instant messaging (IM). Over 80% have listened to music online and 50% have downloaded music from the internet. Only 13% of American teens are not using the internet at all."
