Plus four reader comments, in full: **Claudia Flight — United Kingdom** (school research on sharks: "It was quicker than going to the library and looking at lots of different books"); **Samer Khoury — Lebanon** (homework, emails, app downloads, cinema times and film reviews; "The only trouble with using the internet is that you sit in the same place for too long. If I look at a computer screen for too long, my eyes sometimes hurt"); **Nadia Hashim — Libya** ("The internet is very useful, but you have to be careful if you don't want to waste time… yesterday I wanted to find out something about the River Nile. I looked at 17 different sites and it took me two hours to find what I needed"); **Mike Allen — Australia** (stamp business: "I do 70% of my business through the internet. I sell stamps to people in Malaysia, Japan, Europe and the Middle East… I also buy plane tickets and train tickets online. I always get my theatre and concert tickets online. I also buy books, computer programs, CDs and DVDs").
- **L7 They must have forgotten (p.28).** Track 20 dialog, in full: **Ammar** phones **Mrs Crosby (Anna Crosby)** for **David** — David went out with **Rob** at eight that morning to go fishing, Rob's father driving them to the lake; the match was at three and it is now 3.15. Functional Grammar: deductions about the past — must have / can't have / might have + past participle, ref pp.101–102.
- **L8 Advertising (p.29).** Four adverts completed from a phrase box: **NANA** mints ("so cool, so refreshing! … say goodbye to bad breath with Nana mints! … Does not contain sugar. Recommended by dentists"); **Movie-pedia** from Alana Publishing ("The biggest and best encyclopedia of film on CD-ROM… try Movie-pedia for one month totally free!… if you don't think Movie-pedia is fantastic, you can have your money back — immediately!"); **SHINE shampoo** ("Are you tired of dull, lifeless hair? Are you in hair despair? Don't worry! We have a solution to your problem…"); **Oasis Sports Centre and Pool** ("We are open every day of the week from 7 a.m. to 10 p.m. Hurry! join the club before the end of December, you get ten free guest passes. Don't delay! come to the Oasis today").
- **Round up (pp.30–31)** — "Now you can": talk about the past (past simple + past continuous) · give instructions (imperative + zero conditional) · use compound nouns of communication · use phrasal verbs of communication · express cause and result with so/such … (that) · make deductions (must have, can't have, might have) · communicate on the telephone and leave messages.

## Unit 3 The Environment (book pp.32–43)

Divider spread (book pp.32–33): lessons — L1 Endangered Animals p.34 · L2 Almost Extinct p.35 · L3 Recycling Mobile Phones p.36 · L4 Now you too can be green! p.37 · L5 Offering Help p.38 · L6 The Best of Both Worlds p.39 · L7 The Arabian Oryx p.40 · L8 The Aswan Dam p.41 · Round up p.42.

- **L1 Endangered Animals (p.34).** Six animals matched to photos and range maps (Track 25): giant panda (Sichuan, China), tiger (India/China), mountain gorilla (Uganda/Rwanda), dugong (Indian Ocean), right whale (Atlantic), black rhino (Africa). Functional Grammar: adjective order, ref p.102.
- **L2 Almost Extinct (p.35).** Four fact files, in full: **North Atlantic Right Whale** — "Two hundred years ago the Americans called it the 'right' whale because it was the right whale to hunt. It is slow and easy to catch, and it has lots of oil in its body. Hunters killed almost all of them. Today there are only about 350 in the North Atlantic. Hunting has stopped, but the population is not increasing. One reason may be pollution in the sea. If the right whale becomes extinct, it will be very sad. But at the moment nobody knows how to help them." · **Giant Panda** — "Pandas eat bamboo — sometimes 30 kilos per day of it! This means they can only live in places with lots of bamboo. So these beautiful animals have a problem: if people destroy their habitat, they will not be able to move to other places. And the bad news is — people are destroying their habitat. There are less than 1,000 wild pandas today. Zoos have about 60 and are trying to breed them." · **Mountain Gorilla** — "Gorillas live in the forest. People cut down trees and destroy the forest. So the gorillas' habitat is getting smaller. Hunting gorillas is against the law, but illegal hunters still kill them. Some people eat gorilla meat. If we do nothing, mountain gorillas will become extinct in a few years. It is possible to breed gorillas in zoos. Many zoos around the world have gorilla-breeding programmes." · **Tiger** — "Although it is against the law, there is still a lot of tiger hunting. There are three reasons: (1) people think that tigers are dangerous; (2) tigers sometimes eat cows and sheep, so farmers don't like them; (3) in Chinese medicine they use tiger bones and other parts of the body. There are very few wild tigers left — perhaps 5,000. In China there are only about 30. Tigers will be extinct in twenty years if hunters go on killing them. We can breed tigers in zoos. But they have another problem, too — people are destroying their habitat." Functional Grammar: first conditional, ref p.102.
- **L3 Recycling Mobile Phones (p.36).** The life cycle of a mobile phone in eight captioned pictures, in order: the factory makes the phone and battery (plastic and metals — "Some of these metals – e.g., lead, cadmium and mercury – are toxic") → a customer buys it at the shop → the owner lends it to his sister Noura, who lets her little brother Tariq play games on it → Tariq drops it twice, Noura drops it once ("It still works, but it doesn't look very good") → after two years the battery stops working, a new battery is expensive, so the phone is thrown away → it goes to the rubbish dump → after ten years the metals go down slowly into the earth and get into the water → "People and animals drink the water – and the toxic metals."
- **L4 Now you too can be green! (p.37).** The recycling poster: "Now you too can be green! Do you want to protect the environment? Do you want to save energy? We have put 2,000 recycling bins around the city. Please use them!" (glass — bottles of all colours, remove metal tops; metal — drink cans, food tins, aerosol cans; paper — newspapers, magazines, cardboard boxes, no books). Functional Grammar: re- + verb (recycle, redo, reheated), repeated actions.
- **L5 Offering Help (p.38).** Track 29: volunteers **Victoria, David and Habib** offer help to their boss **Mr Obuya** at the Serengeti National Reserve in Tanzania (offers from a phrase box: feed the baby lion — Victoria's offer, crossed out/not accepted — clean the windows of the minibus, make a new no-smoking sign, type a list of animals, drive the Land Rover, check the fence around the camp, show people where to park, fix the broken picnic table, clean up the picnic area). Functional Grammar: polite suggestions and offers (Shall I…? Shall we…? Would you like me to…?), accepting and refusing.
- **L6 The Best of Both Worlds (p.39).** The **Hybrid Car Sales L.A.** advertisement ("Do you want all the advantages of a petrol engine PLUS all the advantages of an electric motor? Have fun AND protect the environment. Get a hybrid! We sell Honda, Toyota, Ford & General Motors hybrid cars. Hybrid Car Sales, 9466 Beverly Boulevard, Los Angeles, CA 90048 — www.hybridcarsales.com") and the "Which Car To Buy" interview **A HYBRID FOR TERRY?**, in full: Terry likes the new hybrid cars, especially Ford and Toyota; they are expensive but he will buy one anyway — "I live in Los Angeles and we have a big pollution problem there… Not just the smog. There are almost four million people in Los Angeles – and everyone drives a car." On why they help: "In the city, you use the electric motor, so there's no pollution at all." Why not all-electric: "Well, the batteries are not good enough for long journeys – that's the problem. I often drive to my sister's place in San Francisco, for example. That's nearly 650 kilometres." — "And the hybrid car uses its petrol engine on the open road?" — "Exactly. You get the best of both worlds."
- **L7 The Arabian Oryx (p.40).** The article **The oryx is back**, in full: "The oryx is a beautiful antelope with two long horns. It is one of the few big animals that can live in the desert. It gets most of its water from plants; it eats at night when there is some water on the leaves. It walks hundreds of kilometres to find food. In the 1960s the population of oryx in the Arabian Peninsula was very small. The animal was endangered because of hunting. It was hunted for two reasons: the local Bedouin people hunted it for meat, and city people hunted it for sport. In the past, when the hunters were on horses, the oryx survived. But hunters in big four-wheel-drive cars were able to follow the oryx everywhere – it could not get away from them. In 1972 the last wild oryx was shot. But, happily, that was not the end of the story. Zoos around the Middle East had quite a good number of oryx. They got the animals to breed, to build up their numbers. Then in 1982 they put some oryx back into the wild in Oman. The programme was a success, and in 1990 they did the same thing in Saudi Arabia. Of course, hunting the oryx is no longer allowed. Today the wild population of these wonderful animals is about 900. Experts say it will probably soon be 1,000." Functional Grammar: contractions in speech vs writing.
- **L8 The Aswan Dam (p.41).** **The Aswan Dam: Triumph or Tragedy?** by environment correspondent **Natasha Ward**, in full: "I am standing by the Nile in the wonderful town of Aswan in Southern Egypt. The air is clean and hot, and the cool water of the Nile is flowing silently past me. It looks beautiful. But is everything as perfect as it looks? In the 1960s the Aswan Dam was built, and it changed the ecology of the Nile dramatically. Before then, the river used to flood in the summer every year. The water brought earth, which was very good for the farmers. The land was rich, and it was easy to grow food. Now farmers have to use chemical fertilizer on their land – that costs money, and is not as good as the earth from the river. On the other hand, farmers are happy that the river is always the same; before, there was sometimes too much water, and sometimes not enough. The dam caused other problems. The river also used to carry food for fish. After the dam was built, there were fewer fish in the river and in the sea along the coast of Egypt. And of course it was terrible for the people who lived south of the dam – they lost their homes when Lake Nasser was created. But let's not forget the best thing about the Aswan Dam: its hydroelectric power station produces lots of 'clean' electricity for Egyptian people and their industries. And it will continue to do so for many years."
- **Round up (pp.42–43)** — "Now you can": describe things using adjectives in the correct order (general + colour + material) · make predictions about the future (first conditional) · talk about the future using different verb forms and adverbs · use adjectives to describe places · make offers · use contractions in spoken English.

## Unit 4 Fun with English 1 (book pp.44–49)

Four revision lessons (divider book pp.44–45): L1 Bakkar's Adventure p.46 · L2 Shall I help you? p.47 · L3 On the Phone p.48 · L4 I've just won first prize! p.49.

- **L1 Bakkar's Adventure (p.46).** The article **HAVE YOU HEARD OF BAKKAR?**, in full: "Bakkar is a young country boy in Upper Egypt. His father is dead and he lives with his mother. He loves art and drawing, and he cares for animals. He is not a hero but he is a very kind and sociable child. You might have watched Bakkar cartoons during Ramadan. In Egypt, 40 million Egyptians turn on their TVs to watch him every day of Ramadan. The series is also very popular in the United Arab Emirates, Oman and Tunisia. **Mona Abou El Naser**, who invented and created Bakkar, explains why people of different ages and classes like watching the series during the month of Ramadan. She says it's because people can recognize Bakkar's Muslim values. She also says that he is a nice change from Western cartoon characters, and he speaks the language, even if he has got an unusual Arabic accent. The Bakkar shows often have serious topics. For example, one episode is about sympathy for less fortunate people. Habiba, a little blind girl, saves her school friends from danger. Another episode about education shows the importance of teaching everyone in society to read and write. In a more recent show, the main topic is environmental problems. Bakkar works with a Red Sea Ranger. They want to rescue endangered animals from a cruel zookeeper. The cartoon shows children that it's a bad idea to go fishing or collect sea animals in protected areas. Mona Abou El Naser believes that it's important for the Arab World to have its own cartoon characters. She opened Cairo Cartoon Studios in 1990, and she has been making Egyptian cartoons for TV since then. She started with Kani wi Mani, then made Sinbad and finally Bakkar. Perhaps the Bakkar character is so successful because it is the most similar to Mona Abou El Naser herself. Bakkar loves art and Mona loves art. 'Art is in my family,' she says. Mona's older sister was a painter and her uncle always loved drawing funny characters in bright colours in her notebooks at school."
- **L2 Shall I help you? (p.47).** Track 36 comic-strip dialogue: **Zeina** helps **Helen**, who has hurt her knee at tennis ("Don't waste time… I've hurt my knee. It hurts so much that I can't stand up… Shall I help you walk to that seat?… If you put your arm around my shoulder, you'll be able to walk… Would you like me to get you a glass of water?… I think I've broken my leg, Zeina. If it is broken, I won't be able to play in the tennis competition on Saturday").
- **L3 On the Phone (p.48).** Track 37 — phone calls matched to pictures (a UGC cinema ticket, a guitar, a laptop, photographs, a sports bag), plus two role-play situations (buying the laptop; a lost sports bag at a sports club).
- **L4 I've just won first prize! (p.49).** Track 41 feelings dialogues: **Omar** and **Maher** ("Some careless person has just crashed into my car") and six model exchanges (sad — "My grandfather has just died"; worried — the unrevised exam; bored — surfing the net all day; tired — working in my uncle's shop all day; happy — "I've just won first prize in a painting competition"; hot — playing tennis all morning).

## Unit 5 Food and Drink (book pp.50–63)

Divider spread (book pp.50–51): lessons — L1 Food Preferences p.52 · L2 Healthy Habits p.53 · L3 Expressing Necessity p.54 · L4 You'd better wash it first p.55 · L5 If I were in your shoes … p.56 · L6 If I were you, I wouldn't worry p.58 · L7 I wish I could fly p.59 · L8 If only I'd listened p.60 · Round up p.62.

- **L1 Food Preferences (p.52).** Tracks 42–43: five people (Latifa, Adam, Hanaa, Nasseem, Ross) judge fast food (boring, cheap, delicious, quick, spicy, unhealthy, fresh); food-talk exchanges ("I really like chicken, especially stewed chicken with lemon and carrots" · "Do you like fish? I love it when it's really fresh" · "I don't like fish because of all the little bones" · "I prefer meat to fish" · "Have you ever tried Indian food? It's really hot and spicy").
- **L2 Healthy Habits (p.53).** Track 44 gap-fill conversation, in full: **Layla** puts **Rashid** on a diet — "You don't have much [energy] at the moment, do you, Rashid?" — "You're right, Layla. I never feel very energetic." — "I think perhaps you're eating the [wrong] kind of food." — "Yes. Your coach and I have decided to give you a special diet. I've written a list of [food] that you can and can't eat. Have a look at it." — "This is ridiculous, Layla. I can't eat burgers. I mustn't eat [chips]. I must avoid fried food." — "But there are lots of things you can eat. You can have [grilled] chicken, for example, with [boiled] potatoes and green [vegetables]." — "Can I drink [lemonade]?" — "No, you can't. You must drink lots of water. In fact, you should drink eight [glasses] of water a day. And another thing, Rashid, you shouldn't go to bed late." — "How many hours' sleep should I get?" — "You should have eight hours of sleep a night." Functional Grammar: prohibition modals — can/can't, must/mustn't, should/shouldn't (must/mustn't strongest), ref pp.102–103.
- **L3 Expressing Necessity (p.54).** have to + infinitive, present and past; jobs matching (weightlifter, basketball player, teacher, flight attendant — "We had to eat fourteen eggs for breakfast this morning" · "I have to fly to Dubai twice tomorrow" · "I didn't have to go to work yesterday. The school was closed"), ref p.103.
- **L4 You'd better wash it first (p.55).** Track 45 dialog, in full: **Mariam** and her brother **Khaled** are cooking — "Oh, no! I've put too much salt in the stew. What shall I do?" — "You'd better add a tin of tomatoes. That'll make it less salty." — "And perhaps I'd better add some potatoes too." — "Good idea." — "Shall I add some yoghurt as well?" — "You'd better not. It'll make the stew go very white." Plus six problem/response pairs ("You'd better wash it first. It might be dirty" — of spinach).
- **L5 If I were in your shoes … (pp.56–57).** The magazine article **"If …"**, in full: "We asked two teenagers about their ambitions, their dreams and their fears. Here are their answers." **Camilla** is 17 and lives in Brighton, England; **Aziz** is 18 and lives in Sabha, Libya. — *If you won £1,000 in a competition, what would you buy?* Camilla: "I'd spend half the money on driving lessons and the rest on a mobile phone and a digital camera." Aziz: "I'd buy a smartphone and a mountain bike." — *Which famous person would you like to meet if you could choose anyone in the world?* Camilla: "I'd choose J. K. Rowling, the writer of the Harry Potter books. Her books are brilliant and they've made children all over the world happy. She's now the richest woman in Britain and she got rich all by herself." Aziz: "I'd like to meet the French football player, Zinedine Zidane. I'm crazy about football and he's amazing." — *If you could visit any place in the world, where would you go?* Camilla: "I'd like to go to Petra in Jordan. I've seen photos of all the pink stone buildings, but it's my dream to go there. Apparently tourists visiting Petra can even spend a few nights in Bedouin tents. I think it would be so romantic and exciting." Aziz: "I'd like to go to Kenya. I'd love to go on safari and see elephants, lions, giraffes and all those beautiful animals in the wild. It wouldn't be the same as seeing them in zoos. It would be really interesting. That's my dream." — *What changes would you make if you were the Prime Minister of your country?* Camilla: "I'd open more nursery schools. Lots of women who want to work can't because they have to look after young children all the time." Aziz: "I'd improve sports facilities in primary and secondary schools. If children started tennis, swimming and athletics at a younger age, we'd have more champions in this country." — *What would you change in your looks if you could change one thing?* Camilla: "I wouldn't change anything. I'm perfect already! Only joking! Of course I'd like to change my looks. For one thing, I'd like to have bigger eyes!" Aziz: "I'd like to be stronger. I think I'm a bit thin." — *If you were in some kind of serious trouble, who would you ask for help?* Camilla: "I'd go to my grandmother. She always has good advice." Aziz: "I'd ask my great uncle Salem for help. I trust him completely." — *If you had to leave home in an emergency, which two things would you take with you?* Camilla: "I'd take a bottle of water and my phone. Then I'd be able to use my phone to take pictures, make calls and play games." Aziz: "I'd take a photo of my family and my camera. I'm always happy when I'm taking photos." Functional Grammar: second conditional, ref pp.103–104.
- **L6 If I were you, I wouldn't worry (p.58).** Track 48 dialog: **Layla** — "I get spots quite often. What do you think I should do?" **Suha** — "If I were you, I'd wash my face three times a day… And you should stop eating sugar and sweets." Layla: "I'm not sure about that. I tried it, but it didn't work." Plus eight problem/advice pairs and the advice box (What do you think I should do…? If I were you, I wouldn't worry. Why don't you…? You should… You shouldn't…), ref p.104.
- **L7 I wish I could fly (p.59).** Track 52: **Huda's** wishes completed from a box (was, knew, didn't have): "I can't go with you. I really wish I could. I have to do my Maths homework. If only I didn't have to do it." · "I wish I [knew] the answer to this Maths problem. I wish I [didn't have] to study Maths at school. If only I [was] good with numbers." Plus wish/if-only picture sentences ("I wish I could go to France for my holidays · I wish I spoke perfect French · I wish I didn't have to stay in England this summer · If only I lived in Paris · If only I didn't live in London").
- **L8 If only I'd listened (pp.60–61).** **DANA'S STORY** — "Dana El Nasser tells Teen Magazine about the worst day of her life and her biggest regret," in full: "Last month, my friend Layla El Fayed invited me to go to the park for a picnic with her family. I was really looking forward to it. I always have fun when I'm with Layla and it's always nice to get away from my own family for a change. Unfortunately, my little brother Faisal heard about the picnic and said he wanted to come too. He's only nine and he's a bit silly sometimes. For example, he likes pulling Layla's long hair and he usually tries to make Layla and me play football with him. It can be quite annoying for us when we just want to chat and spend time together. So, when he asked me to take him on the picnic with Layla, of course I said no and then I didn't think about it again. On the day of the picnic, I got up early and got my things ready. I had made two big cakes to take with me and I'd bought fruit and some bottles of lemonade. Layla and her family had agreed to pick me up at my house at ten o'clock. At half past nine, Faisal came into the sitting room with his sports bag. 'I'm ready to go,' he said. 'When's Layla going to be here?' I was so angry I didn't answer him and he went to look for Mum. A few minutes later, my mother came into the sitting room with him. 'Why can't Faisal go with you?' she asked. 'He'll be good this time.' I said, 'Sorry, Mum, I can't take him. Layla and her family don't have room in the car.' I didn't know if this was true, but it stopped Faisal and my mother arguing with me. At ten o'clock, I heard the El Fayed's car and ran outside to meet them. My mother and Faisal never saw that the El Fayeds were taking two cars to the park and there was plenty of space in both of them that day. It was fun at the park to start with. Everyone loved my cakes. Layla and I talked for ages about our plans for the future. We played on the swings and ran about, and we played cards. The weather was perfect – not too hot and with a nice breeze. Suddenly, at two o'clock, everything changed. I got a phone call on my mobile. It was my mother. She told me that Faisal was in hospital. After I'd left the house, he had gone out on his bike without telling her. A car had hit him. He was unconscious and he had a broken wrist. My mother was crying. The El Fayeds drove me straight to the hospital. My parents were there already. Faisal was still unconscious and he looked very white. There was some blood on his forehead and a big cut on his cheek. The doctor told me that my brother was going to be OK, but I felt terrible. My first words to my mum were, 'I wish I'd taken him to the park. I wish I hadn't been so selfish. If only I had listened to you, Mum.' Faisal was unconscious for three days. We were all sick with worry for those three days, but in the end everything was OK. On the fourth day, he woke up and asked for ice-cream, sweets and lemonade. Everyone laughed and the doctors said he could come home. Layla came to see him a few days later at home. Although his broken wrist was in a plaster cast he still managed to pull her hair!" Functional Grammar: regrets — I wish / If only + past perfect, ref p.104.
- **Round up (pp.62–63)** — "Now you can": describe food using adjectives · give advice · talk about obligation and necessity · talk about prohibition · express wishes for the present and future · express regrets · talk about imagined situations (second conditional).

## Unit 6 The Media (book pp.64–77)

Divider spread (book pp.64–65): lessons — L1 What's on? p.66 · L2 The Language of the News p.67 · L3 You said you were happy p.68 · L4 Different Opinions p.69 · L5 A Famous Libyan Artist p.70 · L6 Headlines p.72 · L7 At the Museum p.74 · L8 Magazine Start Up p.75 · Round up p.76.

- **L1 What's on? (p.66).** Ten TV programme types (Animation, Chat show, Education, Film/movie, Music, News, Soap opera, Sport, Lifestyle, Weather forecast) matched to screens, with model definitions ("On a chat show, a presenter interviews famous people. They usually sit on a sofa" · "A soap opera is a drama on TV about the everyday lives of people. It may be on several times a week" · "A lifestyle programme is about cooking, gardening, decorating your house, or holidays").
- **L2 The Language of the News (p.67).** The Manchester office-fire article by reporter **Dan Williams**, in full: "THE FIRE started on the ground floor of number 218 Queen Street at 10.10 a.m. and reached the fourth floor within minutes. The police and fire brigade arrived at 10.19 and they immediately began to evacuate everyone from number 218 and the buildings on either side. Three minutes later, there was a loud explosion. The police instructed the crowd in the street to move away from the building. They placed police vehicles at the end of Queen Street and they prevented members of the public from entering the street. The fire was brought under control around 11 o'clock, but the interior of the building had been completely destroyed by then. Ambulances took five people to hospital, but their condition was not serious." Four candidate headlines are given (POLICE CLOSE QUEEN STREET · FIRE DESTROYS OFFICE BUILDING · OFFICE FIRE – FIRE BRIGADE TOO SLOW · EXPLOSION DESTROYS OFFICE BUILDING); the class picks the best. Speaking tip: formal written vs informal spoken English.
- **L3 You said you were happy (p.68).** The chat-show conversation, in full: host **Alan Jones** re-interviews novelist **William Lee** — his novel *Memories* won this year's First Book Award; last year, aged 21, he had said he was very happy, hadn't expected *Memories* to be a great success, wasn't sure he'd continue writing, and thought writing a very lonely occupation; after a short holiday he started again and has nearly finished another novel; when he felt like giving up, "I went to the gym and worked out really hard for an hour or two. After that, I found I could settle down again." Functional Grammar: reported speech — tense and pronoun changes (have → had, have to/must → had to, is going to → was going to, will → would), ref pp.104–105.
- **L4 Different Opinions (p.69).** The film poster for **The Spanish Captain** ("A THRILLING STORY" — Joe McGregor & Emma Knightly; directed by Pedro Garcia; music by David Williams; filmed on location in Morocco, Spain and England; "a beautiful story" — The Daily News; "fantastic special effects" — James Woss, Film Night) and **Sarah Osborne's two-star FILM REVIEW**, in full: "I was very disappointed in this film. They said it was a beautiful story, but in fact it is a rather ridiculous story. As his new wife Mary is English, Captain Santos is worried about going to war against the English. However, he is an officer. The king tells him to go, so he goes. Then Mary is forgotten and we move on to ships, storms and fighting. The first ten minutes of the film are romantic. From then on, it is a war film. The special effects are extremely good. The storm at sea is totally realistic. I have no idea how they did it. There seemed to be about 200 ships in the sea battles. It is very clever, but these scenes are too long. After 40 minutes, I was bored. I used to think Joe McGregor was a great actor. Now I am not so sure. He looks good, as always, but it is difficult to believe that he is a Spanish sea captain. Emma Knightly is not bad, but we only see her for five minutes. There was one thing which I did love about the film — David Williams's music is wonderful. I have already bought the CD. Teenage boys might enjoy this film, but it is not for me." Track 55 is the film's trailer.
- **L5 A Famous Libyan Artist (pp.70–71).** **Eman Ahmed's** profile **ALI OMAR ERMES**, in full: "Ali Omar Ermes was born in Libya in 1945. After he finished school, he moved to England to continue his education. He studied Design at the Plymouth School of Art and Design and graduated with a diploma in 1970. On his return to Libya, he quickly became an important figure in the arts community. He developed a style of painting that combined Arabic lettering (or 'calligraphy') with colour and texture. His bold, colourful paintings also include extracts from Arabic literature. In the 1970s, Ermes also read widely on many subjects. As an editor for *All Arts* magazine, he wrote many articles on the Arabic language, education, art and the media and community. His work became very well known in the Muslim world, and in 1974 he worked with the director of the World of Islam Festival. He became an important voice in the Arabic world and beyond. His paintings are now well known and loved across the whole world. In the 21st century, his work has been exhibited in many of the world's most famous galleries, from Dubai to London. His powerful art connects both with Muslims and people from other traditions and faiths. Today he lives in Surrey, near London, where he continues to pass on Muslim ideas and traditions to younger generations. As the chairman of the Muslim Cultural Heritage Centre, he helps to spread an important message of human responsibility, understanding and peace." Caption: "Ermes' work has been shown at the Dubai International Financial Centre, the National Museum of African Art, Washington, the State Hermitage Museum in St. Petersburg and the British Museum." The lesson's second page works reported questions (if/whether) from the interview behind the profile, ref p.106.
- **L6 Headlines (pp.72–73).** Four short news articles with eight candidate headlines to match (BOY BITES SNAKE · SNAKE BITES BOY · TOP PRICE FOR TOP CAMEL · CAMEL RACING POPULAR IN THE UAE · Golf professionals in the Gulf · Opportunity for young golf players · PETROL TANKER IN RIVER KILLS FISH · 5,000 litres of petrol in river: Fish die), in full: (A) "A little South African boy had a very lucky escape yesterday. Sitting by a river near Cape Town, Mrs Betty Paton saw her three-year-old son Max holding a snake and biting it. 'It was terrifying,' she said. 'It was a poisonous snake, but Max had almost bitten the head off.' Mrs Paton took Max to hospital, but doctors said that the snake had not bitten him." (B) "Some of the world's best golf players have arrived in the Gulf States for a ten-day tour. The aim of the tour is to promote the sport among local young people. The professionals will play a round or two with keen young members of golf clubs and give them tips on how to improve their game. Don't miss this fantastic opportunity!" (C) "Thousands of fish have died in the River Thames, near London, after a traffic accident on a bridge. A petrol tanker carrying 10,000 litres of petrol hit another lorry as they were crossing the narrow bridge in Richmond. A hole was made in the side of the tanker and at least 50% of the petrol poured onto the road and then down into the river. This is the worst case of pollution in the Thames since 1992." (D) "A camel has been sold for $150,000 in the United Arab Emirates (UAE). This is not just an ordinary camel, of course! It is top-quality, racing camel — it has already won more than fifty races. Camel racing is very popular in the UAE and high prices are often paid for the best animals. The new owner is a businessman from Dubai, Omar Abdel Hamid. He said, 'Yes, this is an expensive camel. But I'm sure that I'll make a lot of money with it.'" Writing tip: writing headlines — keep it simple and not too long; state the main idea clearly; focus on key words; leave out unnecessary words (articles, reference words).
- **L7 At the Museum (p.74).** Track 59: **Waleed** visits the National Museum in Tripoli with his teacher and a school group; pupils tick which of twelve reported instructions the museum guide gave (listen carefully, sit down, leave bags outside, stay together, not touch things, not talk loudly, sing a song, fill in worksheets, not eat or drink, not take photos with flash, walk slowly, get tickets at the ticket office). Functional Grammar: reporting commands and requests — tell for commands, ask for requests, ref p.105.
- **L8 Magazine Start Up (p.75).** Two openings from the class magazine **TOP CLASS**: "NEW BUILDING FOR SCIENCE DEPARTMENT — The Head Teacher said yesterday that the government had given her money to build a new Science Department. She was delighted with the news and said that it would make a big difference to Science teaching in the school. She added that work would begin in October." · "A HOLIDAY IN MALAYSIA — Noura Hussein has told Top Class magazine all about her holiday in Malaysia earlier this year. What is special about Malaysia? Noura: Actually, there are lots of special things about it. First of all, there's the sea. It's fantastic! The beaches are beautiful, with white sand and tall palm trees." Plus the committee text: to start a school magazine you set up a committee of three — **the editor** (the boss; makes a list of articles, asks students to write them, chooses the best), **the sub-editor** (checks spelling, grammar and punctuation; can change words to improve), **the designer** (cuts up the articles and chooses the font and pictures to fit the page) — then vote for the committee and choose a title.
- **Round up (pp.76–77)** — "Now you can": classify TV programmes and film types · use newspaper words (article, headline, editor, sub-editor, designer) · identify features of headlines · use formal/informal English appropriately · change tenses and pronouns in reported speech · use the rules for reported speech to report questions.

## Unit 7 Leisure (book pp.78–91)

Divider spread (book pp.78–79): lessons — L1 Staying at the Ice Hotel p.80 · L2 Advertisements p.82 · L3 A Market Where You Can Buy Spices p.83 · L4 Fifteen Questions p.84 · L5 Explaining Situations in the Past p.86 · L6 Positive and Negative Vibes p.87 · L7 Two Magnificent Goals p.88 · L8 Use Blue or Black Ink p.89 · Round up p.90.
- **L1 Staying at the Ice Hotel (pp.80–81).** The **ICE TOURS** brochure, in full: "Come to Sweden's Ice Hotel for a different kind of holiday! All the rooms are made of ice. All the cold drinks are served in glasses made of ice! The Ice Hotel was opened for the first time in 1989 in the village of Jukkasjarvi, 200 kilometres north of the Arctic Circle. It is built totally from snow and ice. Thousands of tonnes of snow and ice are used to construct it and every year it is rebuilt. Ice is brought from the River Torne and is stored until the weather is right for building to begin. In November, when there is no sunlight at all, building work is started. The hotel is ready by the middle of December, and the doors are opened to guests. The last guests leave in April, the Ice Hotel melts and the water goes back to the River Torne." Plus the gap-fill **HIGHLIGHTS OF YOUR 3-DAY HOLIDAY IN LAPLAND** (Track 62 completes it): Day 1 — served cold [drinks] in glasses made of ice; shown to your rooms and given reindeer skins and a [warm] sleeping bag. Day 2 — taken on a reindeer sled through the cold [desert] of Lapland; shown how to [catch] a reindeer with a rope; dinner served in a [traditional] Lapland home. Day 3 — driven on a dog sled to the [mountains]; taught how to [drive] sled dogs yourselves; dinner at the Ice Hotel, "in the coolest [restaurant] in the world." Functional Grammar: the passive — present simple, past simple, future (and continuous/perfect examples), ref pp.105–106.
- **L2 Advertisements (p.82).** Track 69 — four adverts completed from a word box: **Dahab GOLD** ("Necklaces, bracelets, rings, [earrings]. Every girl in Tripoli has her ears pierced here. It's quick, [clean] and painless. Special low [prices] for children"); **Jameela the hairdresser** ("Are you getting married soon? Where are you going to have your hair done? I can make your life [easier]! Call me and you can have your hair cut, washed and styled in your own home. I am a [professional] hairdresser and I also specialize in hairdressing for [weddings]. Jameela – 55519362"); **THE SNAP SHOP** ("Do you need to have your [passport] photo taken? Then you need The Snap Shop! Your photos will be [ready] in one hour. Last year, over a million [people] had passport photos taken at The Snap Shop"); **AL HAZEM OPTICIANS** ("Do you wear glasses or contact lenses? When did you last have your [eyes] tested? Did you know you should have them tested at least [once] a year? Phone us now for an [appointment]!").
- **L3 A Market Where You Can Buy Spices (p.83).** **The Best of Libya** — five paragraphs matched to photos, in full: (1) "Everyone who has been there loves Souq Al Mushir. It is full of magnificent souvenir shops that have many ornaments, swords and daggers, which artistically represent the history and ancestry of Libya." (2) "If you're looking for a market where you can buy traditional things like incense and perfumes, Souq Al-Siagha is the place for you. Anyone who is interested in jewellery or embroidery should visit this exciting place." (3) "Tourists who want an adventure should go to our oasis, found in the desert of South Libya. The scenes there look their best at dawn or sunset. Make sure you go with someone who knows the area well." (4) "You probably won't want to buy an animal during your visit to Libya, but it's still worth going to the animal market. The animals which you'll see there include parrots, monkeys and falcons." (5) "For spices, the best place to go is Bab Al-Hurya market. You'll have a wide range of shops to choose from." Functional Grammar: relative clauses, ref p.106.
- **L4 Fifteen Questions (pp.84–85).** The article **WHO WANTS TO BE A MILLIONAIRE?**, in full: "'Is that your final answer?' Chris Tarrant asks in English. 'Jawaab nihaa'ee?' asks George Kurdahi in Arabic. 'Yes, my final answer,' the contestant says weakly and waits to be told the result. There's a pause, then the music starts. The suspense is unbearable. A few seconds later, the contestant and the audience know if money has been won or lost. Do you recognize the TV programme that is being described? It is called 'Who Wants To Be A Millionaire?' and it is the world's most popular game show. It was invented in Britain in 1998 and was taken to the United States a year later. In 1999 it was being shown six nights a week on the American channel ABC and now it has been exported to 70 other countries, including Pakistan, Indonesia and Turkey. At the beginning of each show, ten contestants take part in a first round called 'Fastest Finger First'. They are asked to put four answers in a particular order. The successful contestant is allowed to sit in the chair and play the main game. The contestant is asked increasingly difficult questions. Each time, the correct answer must be chosen from four possibilities. Money is won for each correct answer. If a contestant answers incorrectly, he usually loses all the money he has won. However, there are some amounts of money that are guaranteed – £1,000 and £32,000. If a contestant is not sure of the answer, he is allowed to use one of three 'lifelines', but each lifeline can be used once only. He can phone a friend, ask the audience, or ask for a 50:50. When a contestant asks for a 50:50, two incorrect answers will be taken away by the computer and two answers will be left. One of these is right and one is wrong. When all three lifelines have been used, the game gets more difficult, but contestants don't have to answer a question if it is too difficult. Instead, they can keep their winnings and stop playing. To win a million, a total of fifteen questions must be answered. In Britain, by 2005 the top prize of a million pounds had been won four times. The Arabic version of the game was first shown in November 2000 and already two contestants have won the top prize of one million Saudi riyals. The last series of the British show was watched by 18 million viewers. What makes the show so popular? Firstly, there is no time limit for answering the questions. This means that a lot of suspense is built up while the contestant thinks about the answer. Another reason is that viewers always imagine they are playing the game. While they watch, they decide which of the four answers they would choose and they often share the contestant's anxiety and indecision. Finally, the show is quite educational. At the end of each show, most people watching it have learnt at least two or three new facts." Track 72 reads the first two paragraphs aloud for imitation.
- **L5 Explaining Situations in the Past (p.86).** Track 63 dialog, in full: **Latifa** is 45 minutes late meeting **Muna** — "Oh, dear! I'm really sorry. But I …" — "Don't tell me – you have a good excuse, Latifa. You always do!" — "No, honestly, Muna, I couldn't help it. Just as I was leaving the house, water started pouring out of a pipe in the kitchen and I had to call a plumber." — "You had to call a plumber!" — "Yes, there was only Grandmother and me at home." — "And did he fix it?" — "Yes, but he took ages. So you see, it isn't really my fault I'm late." — "I guess not. But why didn't you call me?" — "I left my mobile at my cousin's and it's got your mobile number on it." — "You should know my number by heart by now." — "You're right. I promise I'll learn it." — "About time!" — "So, am I forgiven?" — "I suppose so. But you'll have to pay for lunch." — "Sure! It's a deal!" Plus a set of informal expressions to decode (Hi!, About time!, I couldn't help it, I suppose so, He took ages, Sure!, I guess not, It's a deal).
- **L6 Positive and Negative Vibes (p.87).** Track 65 sympathy dialog: **Farah** — "What's the matter?" **Reema** — "My father's in hospital." — "You must be [really] worried." — "I am. I feel [very] anxious." — "You poor thing!" — "Thank you for your kind words." Plus four picture exchanges to sort into positive/negative vibes (passed driving test — delighted, really over the moon, Congratulations! · full marks in maths — feeling proud of yourself, Well done! · car stolen — so angry, absolutely furious, I'm so sorry · fight with best friend — upset, very depressed, What a shame!).
- **L7 Two Magnificent Goals (p.88).** Two blogs on the same match, fact vs opinion: **ENGLAND TEACHES SPAIN A LESSON** by football correspondent **Matt Bradley**, in full: "In a very interesting and, at times, exciting game yesterday in Madrid, England showed that they can be the best in the world. Their defence was magic. For 89 minutes it looked impossible for a ball to get through. In fact it looked impossible for anything to get through. Sadly, late in the first half, the Spanish number 9 managed to score a rather lucky goal. In the end, the Spanish goal was of no consequence. Wayne Rooney scored two magnificent goals early in the second half. He is always good; yesterday he was brilliant. He got a yellow card in the last few minutes of the game, after he had pushed Baraja. In my view, the referee was wrong to give it. This performance by the England team will not be forgotten for a very long time." Against the plain report **England vs Spain in Madrid — England 2, Spain 1**: "The England defence managed to keep out the Spanish attack until the forty-first minute, when Vicente scored a goal from fourteen metres. England fought back in the second half and Rooney scored twice (52nd minute and 59th minute). Then Rooney got angry. He pushed Ruben Baraja to the ground and got a yellow card. This is his fifth yellow card this season. In general, however, it was a good performance by both teams."
- **L8 Use Blue or Black Ink (p.89).** Form-filling vocabulary (address, date of birth, gender, marital status, nationality, occupation, signature, title…) practised on a **Ghadames International Festival** volunteer form, filled in the book for **Adam John Moreton**: surname ADAM, first name JOHN, middle name MORETON, date of birth 18/09/1989, gender Male, title Mr, marital status Single, nationality BRITISH, occupation STUDENT, home address P.O. BOX 1499, LIBYA, telephone 434145, mobile 553268, email adamjmoreton@hotmail.com, signature "Adam John Moreton," date 3rd January 2006, preferred language for correspondence ENGLISH. (A form exercise — Adam Moreton is a different Adam from Secondary 1's Adam Al Sudani.)
- **Round up (pp.90–91)** — "Now you can": talk about processes (present/past simple passive) · talk about a schedule (future passive) · use present continuous, past continuous and present perfect passive · apologize and make excuses · label and complete forms · respond to good and bad news · talk about organizing things (have/get something done).

## Unit 8 Fun with English 2 (book pp.92–97)

Four revision lessons (divider book pp.92–93): L1 Giving Details p.94 · L2 Future Plans p.95 · L3 Travels in Arabia p.96 · L4 Interviews p.97.

- **L1 Giving Details (p.94).** Track 69 — **Layla's** crossword question: "What do you call someone who talks to guests on a TV chat show?" (a presenter/host), then nine definition-matching pairs worked as relative-clause exchanges ("What do you call the sweet stuff that bees make? — Honey" · hairdresser · salon · tent · tie · plumber · audience · exhibition · charcoal).
- **L2 Future Plans (p.95).** Track 70 gap-fill conversation, completed: **Fawzi** — "What are your plans for this summer?" **Justin** — "If I [had] lots of money, I'd buy a [motorcycle] and drive around Europe." — "Could I come with you and sit on the back?" — "Ha, ha. Very funny. I don't have any money. So I won't even be able to go to the [cinema] unless I get a holiday job pretty soon." — "I saw an ad for a [job] that you might be interested in." — "Really? What was it?" — "They're looking for 80 extras in a film. It's a [comedy]." — "Wow! That would be [fun]! How much are they paying?" — "Quite a lot. I'm going to [apply]. Do you want the [number]?" — "Yes, please. I'd better put it straight onto my mobile." — "If I [were] you, I'd call them right away." — "Thanks. Wouldn't it be great if we both got parts!"
- **L3 Travels in Arabia (p.96).** A pair information-gap on a famous 90-year-old traveller in Arabia (the Student A note-frame as printed, one fact filled in as an example): "I first travelled to the south of Arabia in [1945]. The Wali said that [ ] local men must travel with me. We had [ ] to ride, but we walked most of the time. Our food was mostly [ ] and [ ]. Sometimes we killed a [ ]. After [ ] days, we returned to Salalah, but I made many more journeys in the desert. Altogether, I spent [ ] years exploring the Arabian desert. Of course, I could speak Arabic, and [ ] other languages too. After Arabia, I travelled in [ ]. When I became too old to travel, I made my home in [ ]. I've written [ ] books about my travels. My latest book will be published in [ ]. The best thing about travelling in unexplored places is [ ]." Partners compare notes in reported speech ("He told me he had travelled to the south of Arabia in 1945" — "That's different. He told me he had travelled to the south of Arabia in 1946"), so the two versions deliberately differ; the missing values live in the Workbook's Student B version.
- **L4 Interviews (p.97).** Track 73 gap-fill dialogue, completed: **Huda** — "I had my [interview] this morning." **Ameena** — "What for?" — "You know! I applied to be a [volunteer] at the next Olympics." — "Oh, yes. Sorry, I forgot. What did they ask you?" — "The director asked me if I had any previous experience in sports management. And he also asked me how many foreign languages I spoke. Then his assistant asked me if I enjoyed meeting new people." — "Do you think you'll get the [job]?" — "I think so. At the end of the interview, the director asked me if he could get a reference from my [head teacher]." Plus interview-question practice for six jobs (receptionist, flight attendant, TV presenter, journalist, sports instructor, tour guide).

## Back matter — Grammar and Functions Reference (book pp.98–106)

Filed as an index (the full rules text is standard Garnet reference material): **Unit 1** — present simple and present continuous · past simple and past continuous · present perfect simple · question tags · present perfect continuous · for/since. **Unit 2** — giving instructions (imperatives, zero conditional) · so/such · deductions about the past (must have / can't have / might have). **Unit 3** — adjective order · first conditional · re- verbs · offers (Shall I…? / Would you like me to…?). **Unit 5** — prohibition modals · have to · you'd better · second conditional · advice · I wish / If only (present wishes and past regrets). **Unit 6** — reported speech (statements, questions, commands and requests). **Unit 7** — the passive · relative clauses. (Cross-references from the lessons match: U1 refs pp.98–101, U2 pp.101–102, U3 p.102, U5 pp.102–104, U6 pp.104–106, U7 pp.105–106.)

## Checklist for Written Work (book p.107)

Filed in full (same three-part checklist as Secondary 1, unchanged): **Planning your work** — read the question carefully and underline the important parts · brainstorm ideas as a list or mind map · look up unsure vocabulary · select relevant ideas, delete the rest · put ideas into a logical order, grouped in sections · find a model text and adapt its phrases/layout (don't copy chunks) · think about who you are writing for; style and layout follow the reader. **Writing and revising your work** — first draft from the plan, don't worry too much about accuracy · new paragraph for each new idea · re-read and check the question is answered · delete, change or add ideas · check paragraphing and order · check linking words (first, next, then…) · write a second draft more carefully. **Editing your work** — check every sentence is clear, grammar correct, vocabulary suitable, spelling correct (use a dictionary) · show your work to family or a friend for help editing · **editing tip: read the text in reverse order, starting with the final sentence and working backwards to the first — this stops you being distracted by the content and lets you focus on technical details.**

## Transcript (book pp.108–127) — settles and audio-only material

Audio runs **Tracks 1–73**. Every "listen and read" dialog printed on the lesson pages was checked against its script; all agree word for word except the one difference noted at Track 65. Tracks marked as repeats in the book itself: T7=T6 · T14 draws on T13 · T22=T21 · T38/39/40 = calls from T37 · T56=T55 · T68=T67.

- **Track 1** confirms the Mother's Day dialog as printed (Fareed/Alex). **Track 4** gives Hadi's call in full (the lesson page prints it as a gap-fill): "Hello, Mum. I've got a problem." — "What's the matter, Hadi?" — "I've had a scooter accident." — "Oh no! Are you OK?" — "Yes, I'm OK, Mum. Honestly!" — "Have you hurt yourself?" — "Not really. I haven't hurt myself badly. I've only broken my glasses and cut my hand." — "Oh, Hadi. I really hate that scooter of yours." — "Mum, please calm down. It's not very serious." — "How did it happen?" — "A cat ran in front of the scooter. I tried to stop but the road was wet and I crashed into a tree." — "I'm coming to help you now. Where exactly are you?"
- **Tracks 6/7 (U1 L4's audio):** **Waleed** tells **Josie** about Scotland — two weeks so far, one more week before going back to Libya; he stayed in Ian Maclaren's "really old castle near Edinburgh" (about 400 years old; "people say there's a ghost in the kitchen" — he didn't see it); Ian's son caught a huge fish in the loch; Ian's party with about fifty people and nice Scottish music; the men wearing kilts ("they're like skirts. Scottish men sometimes wear them to parties"); Waleed bought a CD of Scottish music.
- **Track 8** confirms the Khalid/Martin dialog and adds: Martin is on holiday; Khalid's brother is **Hasan**; "His English is even better than mine… He lived in London for a year."
- **Track 10 (U1 L6):** **Salwa Bashir** presents the radio programme *Cool Breeze* at Lake View Holiday Camp — Huda has been taking photos all morning (up at six for the early morning light on the lake; lots of pictures of birds); Adam has been playing tennis (didn't win); Anne Marie has been shopping in town (tennis shoes, a skirt, a magazine); Rob and Mark have been windsurfing ("You stand up for a minute then you fall in and get wet"); Issa has been painting a picture of the boats on the lake (the red on his hand is paint, not a cut); Nadia has been making lunch. These are the six rows of the lesson's table.
- **Track 13 (U2 L1):** the six speakers in full — Simon (mobile from his father when he started secondary school; texting; "I always try to find new ways of using letters and numbers in my texts. It's fun … like a new language") · Kamal (bought his mobile with his own pocket money; games on long car trips; favourite game 'Snake') · Nadia (mobile from her parents for her birthday; phoned her mother from the bus to say she'd be late) · Ameera (laptop from her parents when she got a place at medical school; essays, research, emails; "you can use it anywhere – in cafés and libraries and on the train") · Aziz (birthday cards in the post plus e-cards from his sister Nadia) · Nisrin (camera phone; the photo of her brother Ali was too close — "He didn't like it much"). The printed matching key on the transcript page: Simon 2 · Nisrin 3 · Kamal 4 · Ameera 5 · Aziz 6.
- **Track 18 (U2 L4's audio):** three answerphone messages — (1) **Josie** invites **Nadia** to a picnic on Saturday 16th May (Josie, her sister, cousin Jade; meet at the bus stop by the cinema at eleven; ring back around seven). (2) **David Anderson** to **Martin Atwood** about the motorbike advertised in the Western Gazette ("Is it still for sale? If it is, I'd like to come and see it this evening or … tomorrow"; ring 0206-434-911). (3) **Salwa Halabi** to International College: she left her purse in Room 45 after the advanced English class (Mrs Howarth's class, Wednesdays and Fridays); it contains money and a travel pass; phone 0787-050-527.
- **Track 19 (U2 L5's audio):** **Noor** and **Katie** — Abu Nawas Park is "so hot today" / too far / crowded on Fridays (Noor went last Friday with Latifa: "there were such long queues at the drinks stalls that we nearly died of thirst"); they settle on an ice-cream at the Oasis Café in ten minutes.
- **Tracks 21/22 (U2 L8's audio):** the radio programme *Talk Time* with **Salma Shafiq** and guests Faris, Jade, Omar and Liam on advertising — Liam: adverts make people discontented ("they themselves aren't good enough"); Jade: cinema adverts are fun and useful for new products; Omar: advertising is a waste of time and money — "who pays the price in the end? We do"; Faris: adverts make people buy things they don't need (a friend bought a camera after an advert and never uses it), but they also tell us about new products; Omar bought crisps and sweets as a kid because of TV adverts — "They shouldn't make adverts that are specially for children"; Liam: all governments should ban cigarette advertising.
- **Track 23 (U3 L1's audio):** six animal talks backing the map exercise — panda (six small areas in the centre of China) · mountain gorilla ("fewer than 700 mountain gorillas left in the wild… just three small areas in Rwanda and Uganda") · black rhino (persecuted by hunters for 150 years; now only in wildlife parks) · dugong ("thought to be close to extinction… lives along coasts around the Indian Ocean and the north of Australia… a very important area for the dugong is the Arabian Gulf, around Bahrain, Libya and the UAE" — as printed) · tiger (India, Thailand, Cambodia, Vietnam, Russia; a small number in Malaysia and Indonesia) · right whale (two parts of the North Atlantic: the USA/Canada coast and the Norway-to-Spain coast; "showing little sign of recovery").
- **Tracks 26/27 (U3 L3–L4's audio):** "Can Fawzi's phone be recycled?" — the phone's owner is **Fawzi** (the Course Book's pictures name only his sister Noura and little brother Tariq): factory → shop → Fawzi lends it to Noura, Tariq plays games → dropped three times → battery dies after two years → Fawzi takes the old phone back to the shop → they send it to the recycling centre → the phone is broken into parts, plastic to the rubbish dump, metal parts recycled → the factory uses the recycled metal to make a new phone. Track 27: a professor gives two reasons to recycle — rubbish dumps near towns are getting bigger, dirty and dangerous; and economics (paper from trees cut in Norway or Canada and carried thousands of miles, used once and thrown away — reusing old paper is "a cheap and easy process… we will save money and protect the environment").
- **Track 28 (U3 L5) settles the offers:** Victoria — feed the baby lion refused ("I'm sorry, Victoria. I've done that already"); she types the list of animals into the computer instead, then works in the car park showing people where to park. David — fixes the picnic table with the broken leg; offers to drive the Land Rover and is refused ("No way! I'll drive myself, thank you!"); cleans the windows of the minibus. Habib — offers a new 'no smoking' sign; refused (Mr Obuya found the old sign on the ground behind the office that morning); checks the fence around the campsite very carefully; clears up the picnic area ("It's a terrible mess, isn't it?"). **Track 30** adds four more Obuya/Victoria conversations (keep quiet around the birds' nests; put the car-park rubbish bins behind the café; record the number of giraffe calves this week; the satellite tracking program isn't working — "Is it switched on?"). **Track 31:** Obuya's five sentences for matching (minibus to the petrol station; treat the gorilla with caution; a baby lion becomes imprinted on humans; lock the car park gates at closing time; the reserve could not operate without the tourists' financial contributions).
- **Track 32 (U3 L6) names the interviewee in full: Terry Garcia.** The interview otherwise agrees with the printed page.
- **Track 36** confirms the Zeina/Helen dialog word for word. **Track 37 (U4 L3) — the three calls in full:** (1) **Youssef** asks **Suleiman** to pick up his photos from the photo shop in the Centre Mall — ready today, ticket on the desk in his room ("a long white slip of paper"), £5 to pay but already paid; Suleiman will collect them that afternoon. (2) The **UGC Cinema Information line**: Screen 1: *Alone in the Desert*, Certificate 15, showing at 6.30 and 8.45 · Screen 2: *Going Home*, Certificate 12, at 1.30 and 3.30 · Screen 3: *Dancing on Glass*, Certificate 18, at 7.15 and 9.30; tickets £6.50 for adults and £5 for children and students (students must show a valid student card). (3) **Faisal Al Dossari** (spelled "Dossari with a double S and an I at the end") phones **Mike Holland** (1657 89732) about the blue Fender electric guitar advertised in Music Monthly — ten months old, £300; he'll come round at 4.30.
- **Track 42 (U5 L1) settles the five verdicts:** on Rob Marsden's radio show *Speak Out* — Latifa: cheap ("You don't have to pay very much for it. That's the best thing about it") · Adam: boring ("It's always the same. You never get anything different") · Hanaa: quick ("you go in, order it and five minutes later you're eating it") · Nasseem: delicious ("I love it. I eat it two or three times a week. My favourites are fish and chips and kebabs") · Ross: unhealthy ("Doctors say most fast food contains too much oil and fat").
- **Track 44** confirms the Layla/Rashid diet dialog (Rashid's last line in the script: "At least eight hours a night"). **Track 45** confirms Mariam/Khaled. **Track 47** adds six short you'd-better exchanges (Mariam cuts her finger grating cheese — wash it and put a plaster on it; Peter crying because chopping onions makes his eyes water — "You'd better not chop them. I'll do it for you"; lamb kebabs on the barbecue and the charcoal hunt; Majida and the washing-up gloves in the cupboard under the sink; a fork vs an electric mixer for a chocolate cake; Nadia burns her finger on the teapot).
- **Track 49** confirms Huda's wishes. **Track 50:** twelve wishes to match to pictures. **Track 51 (U5 L8's audio):** a survey of a hundred British men and women aged fifty — 30% had regrets about their family life, 15% about careers or working life, 10% about their health; 45% had no regrets at all.
- **Track 52 (U6 L1's audio):** six programme extracts to classify — a weather forecast (cloud over England and Wales; the southwest best for sun; 18–19 degrees) · a chat show (host Alan welcomes **Rose Johnson**, fresh from filming with Tom Marconi) · a cartoon (a cat steals a fish: "Correction – that was your fish!") · the news (Jeremy and **Fiona Bright** in Glasgow on a train crash: ambulances have taken 41 people to hospital, no one killed) · a holiday programme (Hotel Splendid, Phuket; flew to Bangkok then a short flight to Phuket, £579 with British Airways; everything else incredibly cheap) · a nature programme (spiders and their webs; one species on St Kitts builds no web at all).
- **Track 53 (U6 L2's audio):** a witness retells the Queen Street fire in spoken form ("A few minutes later, there was a loud bang. The police told the crowd in the street to get away from the building… By 11 o'clock, the fire seemed to be out") — the spoken twin of the printed article, with small wording differences as spoken.
- **Track 54 (U6 L3's audio):** **William Lee's** first-interview statements for reporting: "I still live at home with my parents · I lived in China until I was fifteen · I've always liked writing stories. I'm no good at anything else · I'm going to relax in Spain for a month · After that, I'll get on with my next novel · It'll probably be set in China · I can't spell, so I have to use 'spell check' a lot · I write in English, but I think in Mandarin."
- **Track 55 (U6 L4) — The Spanish Captain trailer in full:** "Joe McGregor is brilliant as Captain Diego Santos. Emma Knightly is unforgettable as his lovely young bride in The Spanish Captain. He is Spanish; she is English. But the year is 1588 and the two countries are at war. This is the beautiful story. Captain Santos has an impossible choice to make. The king of Spain tells him to go to war. His heart tells him to stay at home. You will laugh. You will cry. You will be amazed by the fantastic special effects. You will love the wonderful music, written for this film by David Williams. You will never forget The Spanish Captain." (The trailer gives the captain's first name, **Diego**, which the poster and review never print.)
- **Track 57 (U6 L5's audio):** the six questions **Aunt Salwa** asked **Fawzi** (how tall are you? which year are you in at school? what are you studying? do you still play tennis? did you receive a birthday present from me? have you learnt to drive yet?) — the raw material for the reported-questions exercise.
- **Track 58 (U6 L7) — the museum guide is Mrs Al-Hamza**, and her script settles the tick list: listen carefully ✓ · stay together at all times ✓ · do not talk loudly ✓ · three worksheets to fill in as we go round — not at the end of the tour ✓ · eating and drinking is not allowed ✓ · you can take photos, but not with flash ✓ · "I have your tickets, so you don't need to stop at the ticket office" ✓. Not asked: sit down, leave bags outside, sing a song ("Last year, one school group started to sing songs! Please don't do that!"), walk slowly.
- **Track 59** confirms the Ice Hotel highlights gap-fill word for word. **Track 60** reads the four U7 L2 adverts aloud, confirming every gap (the jeweller is read as "Dahab Jewellers"; Jameela's number 55519362). **Track 61:** have/get-something-done exchanges — a new kitchen done by **Benghazi Kitchens** (5551 9362).

Reema — "That's a kind offer. Maybe you could give me a lift to the hospital?" Farah — "Of course." The lesson page ends at "Thank you for your kind words."

## Key Words (book pp.128–148) and Phonetic Symbols (book p.149)

Per-unit glossaries with phonetic transcriptions, read in full. Unit lists start: U1 p.128 · U3 p.133 · U4 p.137 (a short list of 9: blind, episode, hero, ranger, receptionist, sociable, sympathy, misfortune, values) · U5 p.138 · U6 p.141 · U7 p.144 · U8 p.148 (the shortest list, 9 words: professionally, film extras, publish, snowboarding, whitewater rafting, quad biking, spectacular, dunes, limit). Slips as printed: U1 tags "**brilliant (n)**" but defines it "very good" (an adjective's meaning), and tags "**fast (n and v) (n)**" twice over. U7 tags "**reopen (adj)**" but defines it "to open again" (a verb's meaning); the same list glosses "What a pity!" as "as above: an expression used to show concern," pointing at "What a shame!" printed below it. The Phonetic Symbols chart (p.149) prints the /e/ keyword correctly as "men" (contrast the G9 Course Book's "mgn").

## Readers (book pp.150–216) — first reader: Robinson Crusoe

The Readers section opens with a complete graded reader bound into the book: **"English for Libya Secondary 2 Arts Reader — Robinson Crusoe — Daniel Defoe — Retold by Peter Viney"** (book p.150). Garnet Publishing Ltd © 2019; ISBN **978-1-78260-228-6**; printed and bound in Lebanon by International Press. Production credits: series editor Peter Viney; project manager Clare Chandler; editorial Lucy Constable, Rachel Finnie, Sally Rabi; design and layout Mike Hinks; illustration ClipArt ETC; cover image Look and Learn; photography Clipart, iStockphoto, Shutterstock. A note on the title page: "The resources in the additional resources section may be photocopied for use by the purchasing institution and the students in its classes."

Book p.151 profiles **Daniel Defoe (1660–1731)**: born in London; worked in business and wrote many books; fought in a rebellion against King James II in 1685; later a secret agent for William III; lost all his money; married a rich woman — eight children; travelled to Scotland, Spain and Portugal in his business; went to prison again in 1703 after King William died. He had read the true story of **Alexander Selkirk**, who lived alone on an island in the Pacific Ocean between 1704 and 1708, was rescued by a ship, arrived in London in 1711 and told his story; Defoe changed the story from the Pacific Ocean to the Atlantic Ocean and started Crusoe's adventure in 1659, the year before Defoe himself was born. Defoe died in 1731 at seventy-one; *Robinson Crusoe* is his most popular story, "one of the first novels in English," translated into many languages, with many films, TV programmes, children's books and theatre plays.

Reader contents (book p.152): 1 My early voyages p.153 · 2 The shipwreck p.154 · 3 The broken ship p.155 · 4 My home p.157 · Understanding the Story 1 p.158 · 5 The earthquake p.159 · 6 The goats p.159 · 7 Around the island p.161 · 8 I have a surprise p.162 · Understanding the Story 2 p.163 · 9 The wild people p.164 · 10 Man Friday p.165 · 11 The education of Friday p.166 · Understanding the Story 3 p.167 · 12 The return of the canoes p.168 · 13 The English boat p.169 · 14 The captain's story p.170 · 15 The mutineers p.170 · 16 The end of my story p.171 · Understanding the Story 4 p.172 · Extra activities p.173 · Glossary p.175.

The retelling is faithful to Defoe's story in simple English, with green study boxes after nearly every episode (Critical thinking · Reflect and communicate · Inferring meaning · Predicting · Literature and language · Literature and art). Chapter map with this retelling's own facts:

- **1 My early voyages (p.153).** Born in York in 1632, the third son; his oldest brother was a soldier killed by the Spanish. First voyage from Hull on September 1st, 1651 — storm, ship sank, the crew reached an English beach and the nearest town gave them food, clothes and money; the captain warned him never to go to sea again. He sailed from London to Africa and came back rich, "with bags of gold dust." On the next voyage a Moroccan ship hunting slaves attacked them (their twelve guns against eighteen); he was a slave for two years in **Sallee, near Rabat**. His escape: ordered to take the master's small fishing boat out, he threw one man into the sea and let the other — a strong swimmer — choose; the boy **Xury** chose to come with him.
- **2 The shipwreck (p.154).** A Portuguese ship rescued them, sailing from Portugal to Brazil; the captain paid eighty pieces of silver for the boat and **sixty-eight pieces of silver for Xury**, promising to let Xury go free after ten years — "Yes, I, the ex-slave, sold a man as a slave." He lived for several years in Brazil buying and selling sugar, then agreed to sail to Africa for gold, elephants' teeth and slaves for Brazil. Exactly eight years after he left his family, the voyage out of Hull was driven north by a storm for twelve days; one crewman died of illness and a man and a boy were washed overboard and drowned; the ship hit a sandbar; eleven men took the rowing boat and the biggest wave he had ever seen sank it; he alone reached the beach, where he found three hats and two shoes — one brown and one black.
- **3 The broken ship (p.155).** He had only a knife, his tobacco pipe and a little tobacco, and slept that first night in a tall tree. The tide had pushed the wreck closer; he swam out, climbed a rope, and found the bread room, three boxes (dried meat and corn; clothes; weapons — three good muskets, two pistols, gunpowder and shot, two sharp swords) and tools (a hammer, an axe and a saw); he built a raft from the masts. In thirteen days he made **eleven journeys** to the ship; on the twelfth he found bowls, knives, forks and spoons and a purse with silver and gold coins, but no maps — the captain had taken them in the rowing boat. That night a storm broke the ship into pieces and washed it away.
- **4 My home (p.157).** He cut a calendar into a piece of wood: "I came onto this island on the **30th day of September, 1659**." He had also brought from the ship some books, pens and paper, a dog and two cats — the cats came on the raft; the dog jumped into the sea and swam after him. He chose a flat grass area in front of a rock wall on the north side of the hill, put his tent under a piece of sail, and built a strong fence with **no door — a ladder** to climb in and lift up after him: "I thought of my home as my fort." Storms were so strong that enlarging the small cave took **eighteen days in November and December of 1659**. Fearing lightning would explode his three barrels of gunpowder, he spent two weeks making about **one hundred small boxes** for it and stored them dry inside the cave. Corn from an old chicken-food cloth bag, thrown away on the ground, grew — carefully watered, it meant bread after a year. He made a chair and a table, started a second chair and stopped: "In all of this island, I am the only human. I am alone."
- **Understanding the Story 1 (p.158)** — a group timeline of Robinson's life so far; ten tick-or-cross statements (he sailed in a ship to China ✗; he sold Xury for sixty-eight pieces of silver ✓; he bought and sold **coffee** in Brazil ✗ — it was sugar; he made two chairs ✗…); and a discussion of the survival skills he has shown: determination, planning, predicting and problem solving.
- **5 The earthquake (p.159).** **April 16th, 1660** — three shocks while he worked inside the cave; the storm that followed moved the wreck closer to the beach, so he salvaged much wood and many pieces of metal from it; he made a wooden ceiling and a second exit at the back of the cave: "So sometimes good comes from bad."
- **6 The goats (p.159).** He shot a female goat and her kid followed him home. The calendar's long lines reached ten years. After the second year he made bread from his corn. A second kid, found with a broken leg, healed and stayed. In his **eleventh year** he dug pit traps: one morning all three holes were full — a large, strong, very angry male (released), and a male and two females kept. A year later he had twelve goats, two years later **forty-three**; he learned to milk the goats and make butter and then cheese. He caught a parrot, knocked down with his stick, and taught it over many years to say several words; he called it **Poll**, and the first word it learned was his name: "'Hello Robin!' he said when I came home."
- **7 Around the island (p.161).** After more than ten years he describes himself: short beard, long moustache, a great high goatskin hat with a piece hanging down the back, a goatskin jacket, trousers to just below his knees, goatskin foot coverings, a belt with two small bags (gunpowder and shot), a knife, a small axe and a small saw, both pistols, a basket on his back, a musket in one hand and a goatskin umbrella in the other. The ship's rowing boat was upside down, holed and too heavy to move; his first canoe, cut from one tree over three months, was too big to move to the sea; after looking for years after every storm he found a smaller tree nearer the sea, worked on that canoe for **two years**, and fitted a mast and a sail; he also had a telescope from the wreck. He sailed around the island in three days and saw land twenty or thirty miles away — "It was a long way from the island, much too far for my small canoe… I was sure it was not another island, but the mainland!"
- **8 I have a surprise (p.162).** The dream: someone calling "Robin! Robin! Robinson Crusoe!" — it was Poll, who had flown across the island while he was at sea and found him. A year later, **during his twelfth lonely year**, walking toward his canoe he saw **the footprint in the sand** — one bare print, all five toes clear. He hid inside the fort for three days with every musket and pistol loaded.
- **Understanding the Story 2 (p.163)** — continue the timeline from April 16th, 1660; list Robinson's survival skills in pairs; match eight sentence halves from the story.
- **9 The wild people (p.164).** He measured the print against his own foot: it was longer, wider and thicker, the sand deeper — a bigger, heavier, probably stronger man. He built a second fence outside the first, with sharp pieces of wood, and planted trees and bushes to hide his fence and fort. Two years passed; then, through his telescope, a canoe moving away and, on the beach, a cold fire surrounded by **human bones — skulls, leg bones, arm bones, shoulder bones, feet bones and hand bones, with blood on them**: cannibals who eat their prisoners after a war. Fifteen months later, one stormy night, he heard a ship's gun and saw flashes: a Spanish ship wrecked on a sandbar; he fed his fire with wood all night so they could see it; in the morning he sailed out — the sea was full of the wreck, and only a hungry, thirsty **dog** swam and jumped into his canoe (his first dog had died years before). Aboard the wreck he found some cooking pots and a little gunpowder, and gold and silver — "no good on an empty island" — and he also took the shoes from the drowned men. In his **twenty-third year** he saw through the telescope, about two miles away, **nine naked men** sitting around a fire where he had found the bones years earlier, dancing around it; three more canoes left the beach about a mile away; afterwards he walked to the beach and saw the skulls and bones and the blood on the sand.
- **10 Man Friday (p.165).** In his **twenty-sixth year**, five canoes brought perhaps thirty men and two prisoners. The first prisoner was knocked down and cut into pieces; the second ran toward the fort; three men chased him, two followed across the river. Robinson jumped from his fence: he knocked the first chaser down with his musket (he did not want to shoot, because the men on the beach would hear), and shot the second man in the chest — "one shot to the heart." Friday then killed the first cannibal — the man he had knocked down, who had stood up — with the sword from Robinson's own belt, laughed, and gave the sword back. They made a hole in the sand and buried the cannibals. He took Friday not to the fort but to the forest, where Friday lay down and put Robinson's foot on his head. He named him for the day of the week: "'You are my man, Friday.' 'Man Friday!' he said." His own introduction: "**I am Master Robinson Crusoe, a poor sailor from England**." He soon taught him to say 'Yes', 'No', 'Food' and 'Water', and found him some clothes from the cave — short trousers and a goatskin jacket and hat.
- **11 The education of Friday (p.166).** Friday made signs: he was one of four prisoners; the cannibals had eaten his three friends; there was a war between his people and theirs on the mainland. Together they made a great fire on the beach and burned all the bones until nothing was left. Friday: tall, handsome, long black hair, dark skin, strong, a friendly smile, about **twenty-six years old**; his tent was put between the two fences, and for the first few weeks Robinson slept in his cave while Friday slept — he believed Friday had tasted human meat. Language teaching came first: Robinson had learned **Arabic, Spanish and Portuguese** in his travels, and Friday "was the quickest student I had ever met." The musket lesson: he shot a goat, and Friday pulled his jacket open and felt his own chest, looking for the hole his shot had made. Salt: Robinson made salt in pans from sea water; Friday knocked the salted meat off the fire — "Bad!" — then tasted salt from Robinson's finger: "Good!" The next year "was the best year of all the many years I lived on the island." Friday confirmed his people eat men: "Yes, we eat men. We bring prisoners here. We eat them" — and the feast on that beach years before had eaten **twenty-three people**. Friday also told him of **sixteen Spanish sailors** living in his country, whose ship had hit the shore in a storm four years earlier; they lived in peace with Friday's people and had weapons but no shot or gunpowder and no metal tools. Asked if they could sail to his country in the first, too-big boat — which the two of them together could move — Friday looked worried: "Why are you angry with Friday? Do not send me away. I want to stay here."
- **Understanding the Story 3 (p.167)** — continue the timeline from the footprint; how Friday's presence helped Robinson survive; Friday's background against Robinson's; what social-interaction and survival skills Robinson taught Friday; correct eight false sentences (Robinson planted **trees** to hide his fort, not flowers; the first words taught were not 'Hello', 'Goodbye' and 'Thank you'…); delete the things not in the picture on page 164.
- **12 The return of the canoes (p.168).** They built a new canoe (Friday knew the best kind of tree) and made sails from the shipwreck sails — "The sails from our shipwreck were now twenty-seven years old." Three canoes came: **twenty-one men with three prisoners**, one already being eaten, another tied on the sand, and a third — a bearded man in European clothes.
