Track 23 (six animal talks, A–F) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book transcript** (panda in just six small areas in the centre of China; fewer than 700 mountain gorillas in Rwanda and Uganda; black rhino persecuted for 150 years; dugong "around Bahrain, Libya and the UAE" as printed; tiger in parts of India, Thailand, Cambodia, Vietnam, China, Russia, Malaysia, Indonesia; right whale in two parts of the North Atlantic). Adjective-order rule key: a general adjective comes first and a colour adjective comes second (full order taught in WB B: opinion, size, shape, age, colour, nationality, material).

**Lesson 2: Almost Extinct** (CB p.35; WB pp.32–34; Tracks 24–25; G&F p.102). The four fact files lesson. A key: 1 C mountain gorilla · 2 B giant panda · 3 D tiger · 4 D tiger. WB A table key (what must be done): Right whale — cut pollution · Giant panda — breed in zoos, protect habitat · Mountain gorilla — stop hunting, breed in zoos, protect habitat · Tiger — stop hunting, breed in zoos, protect habitat. WB D key (look / looks / look like): My cat looks like a tiger · Fuad looks very tired today · Your brother looks like a businessman · That book looks interesting · I look like my father · Those elephants look ill. **Example descriptions kept in the notes** (The Right Whale — "can grow up to 60 feet long… live in small groups, called 'pods'… up to 100 years old"; Tiger — stripes, swimming, running). First conditional key includes the WB F matching (1 If everybody comes, there will be 38 people at the dinner … 8 If we are late, Aziz won't wait for us). Track 24: "If you call Mariam, she will help you with the homework" (intonation). Track 25: "If the test isn't too difficult, we'll pass. / If I don't get a place on the team, I'll be very disappointed."

**Lesson 3: Recycling Mobile Phones** (CB p.36; WB pp.35–36; Track 26). Life-cycle order key: a5 · b4 · c2 · d1 · e3 · f8 · g7 · h6. Track 26 (the mobile phone's life cycle, told as a story) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** (the owner is a boy; he lends it to his sister **Noura**; her little brother **Tariq** drops it twice and Noura drops it once; after two years the battery stops working; the phone goes back to the shop, to the recycling centre, the plastic to the rubbish dump, the metal parts to be recycled and made into a new phone at the factory).

**Lesson 4: Now you too can be green!** (CB p.37; WB pp.36–38; Track 27). Poster key: A – Glass bin (blue) · B – Metal bin (yellow) · C – Paper bin (red). Track 27 (Professor Al Jaber's recycling talk, per the lesson notes) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** (rubbish dumps near towns and cities getting bigger every year, dirty and dangerous; paper from trees felled in Norway or Canada and carried thousands of miles using a lot of oil). Completion key: work · cheap · metal · bigger · destroying · thousands · throw · process · environment. WB C key (re- verbs in sentences): replay · rewrite · reuse · recharge · rebuild · reorganize · redo ("x = 4y 2 − 0.01? That can't be right! Let's redo the question from the start").

**Lesson 5: Offering Help** (CB p.38; WB pp.39–40; Tracks 28–31). Track 28 (the Serengeti volunteers offer jobs to Mr Obuya) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**. Offers key: Victoria — feed the baby lion ✗ · type a list of animals ✓ · show people where to park ✓; David — fix the broken picnic table ✓ · drive the Land Rover ✗ · clean the windows of the minibus ✓; Habib — make a new no-smoking sign ✗ · check the fence around the camp ✓ · clear up the picnic area ✓. Track 29 (listen and repeat — four offer/response pairs) printed. Track 30 (four more Obuya/Victoria conversations, checking understanding) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**; key: 1 DW · 2 EQ · 3 DW · 4 EQ. Track 31 (Obuya's five sentences A–E) printed; matching key: 1 Sentence E [DW] · 2 Sentence C [DW] · 3 Sentence A [EQ] · 4 Sentence B [DW] · 5 Sentence D [EQ]. WB F key (question tags to check): The table in the picnic area is broken, isn't it? · The animals can't get into the car park, can they? · The tourists should stay in their cars, shouldn't they? · The new no-smoking sign will be OK, won't it?

**Lesson 6: The Best of Both Worlds** (CB p.39; WB pp.41–42; Tracks 32–33). The hybrid-car lesson. B answer: the one bad thing — "They're expensive." Track 32 (the *Which car to buy?* interview with **Terry Garcia**) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**, and confirms the interviewee's full name (the interviewer is labelled WCTB = *Which car to buy?*): Los Angeles, almost four million people, sister's place in San Francisco "nearly 650 kilometres", the hybrid uses its petrol engine on the open road, "You get the best of both worlds." Advantages/disadvantages table key: Petrol engine only — good on the open road, cheaper than hybrid / creates pollution every time you use it; Electric motor only — never contributes to pollution / batteries aren't good enough for long journeys; Hybrid — doesn't pollute in the city, has petrol engine for open road / more expensive than the other cars, pollutes when petrol engine is used. Track 33 (listen and repeat): "I expect I'll buy one. / Exactly. / They will definitely make things better. / I'll certainly buy a hybrid." Suggested answers for reducing city car pollution: ban cars from city centres · encourage people to use bicycles more · make people car share · improve public transport (buses, trains, etc.). **Sample essay kept in the notes**: "How can we reduce pollution from cars in cities?" (70–100 words; hybrid cars, electric cars, and lead-free petrol "cheaper than hybrid or electric cars" as printed).

**Lesson 7: The Arabian Oryx** (CB p.40; WB pp.42–43; Track 34; G&F p.102). A answer: the oryx became extinct in the wild **in 1972** — confirming the Course Book article. WB A T/F key: 1 True · 2 False (they walk hundreds of kilometres) · 3 False (the population was very small) · 4 True · 5 True · 6 False (the last one died in 1972) · 7 False (zoos had quite a good number) · 8 True. Track 34 (eight contracted sentences to write out in full) printed with its expansion key. Track 35 (listen and check): seven contracted sentences (Tigers don't normally attack people … The football games weren't very exciting).

**Lesson 8: The Aswan Dam** (CB p.41; WB pp.44–45). Natasha Ward's "Triumph or Tragedy?" article lesson. Headline key: there are good and bad things about the dam; triumph = something successful, a good thing; tragedy = a bad or unhappy thing, something that caused serious problems. WB B table key — Advantages: the river is always the same (it doesn't flood any more); produces clean electricity for people and industry. Disadvantages: people lost their homes; farmers have to use chemical fertilizers on the land now — expensive; fewer fish because there is less food. Stress-marked words from the unit: de'velopment · 'energy · en'vironment · 'habitat · poll'ution · re'cycle · 'seawater · tech'nology. WB C key: 1 False (it runs through the town) · 2 False (it is over 40 years old) · 3 True · 4 True · 5 False (it will continue to produce electricity for many years). **Sample picture descriptions (six) and a sample place description kept in the notes** ("I am standing in the desert. It is very hot and there is no wind…"). **Round up** closes the unit.

## Unit 4 — Fun with English 1 (notes book pp.84–95)

Revision unit: skills, grammar and vocabulary from Units 1 to 3; no new overview strands beyond 21st Century Skills (describing a TV programme, discussing possibility, categorizing information, transferring information to a table, predicting a conversation). Key words page (book p.85) is the short revision list of 9: blind · episode · hero · misfortune · ranger · receptionist · sociable · sympathy · values — matching the Course Book's Unit 4 Key Words list.

**Lesson 1: Bakkar's Adventures** (CB p.46; WB pp.46–48). The "HAVE YOU HEARD OF BAKKAR?" article lesson. WB A answers: 1 During Ramadan · 2 In the United Arab Emirates, Oman and Tunisia · 3 She is a little blind girl in one of the episodes who saves her school friends from danger · 4 Along the Red Sea · 5 In 1990 · 6 *Kani wi Mani* · 7 Her uncle. WB B key: 1e · 2h · 3d · 4f · 5a · 6c · 7b · 8g. WB C possible answers (adjective order): a peaceful Lebanese garden · traditional Indian clothes · an interesting Italian film · a kind Libyan woman · a boring Scottish poet · a strange Egyptian play · delicious Tunisian food. **Sample TV description kept in the notes** ("The Two Sisters" — Safia and Fatima; the episode called "The Lost Homework"; FOX channel on satellite TV every Wednesday at seven p.m.). WB F key (completing the animation article): environmental · rise · pollution · ecology · protect · endangered · survey · communicate · ideas · shows · opinion · during.

**Lesson 2: Shall I help you?** (CB p.47; WB pp.49–50; Track 36; G&F pp.101–102). Track 36 (Zeina helps Helen, who has hurt her knee) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** ("Shall I help you walk to that seat?… If you put your arm around my shoulder, you'll be able to walk… I think I've broken my leg, I won't be able to play in the tennis competition on Saturday."). WB B key: Zeina offers to help Helen walk to a seat – 2 · Zeina helps Helen to stand up – 3 · Helen thinks her leg is broken – 5 · Zeina offers to get Helen a drink – 4 · Zeina thinks Helen is joking – 1. Sample continuations and the retelling sample are kept in the notes. WB E key (first conditionals from the story): "If you put your arm around my shoulder, you'll be able to walk. / If it is broken, I won't be able to play in the tennis competition on Saturday." WB F key given in full.

**Lesson 3: On the Phone** (CB p.48; WB p.51; Tracks 37–40). Track 37 (three phone calls) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**: Call 1 Youssef asks Suleiman to pick up his photos from the photo shop in the Centre Mall (ready today; the ticket is a long white slip of paper on the desk in Youssef's room; "It's £5. But I've already paid."). Call 2 the UGC Cinema Information line (*Alone in the Desert* Cert 15 at 6.30 and 8.45 · *Going Home* Cert 12 at 1.30 and 3.30 · *Dancing on Glass* Cert 18 at 7.15 and 9.30; tickets £6.50 for adults, £5 for children and students). Call 3 **Faisal Al Dossari** phones Mike Holland (1657 89732) about the blue Fender electric guitar advertised in *Music Monthly* (£300). Matching key: Call 1: D · Call 2: A · Call 3: B. Tracks 38, 39 and 40 are each marked as repeats of Calls 1, 2 and 3 from Track 37, with their answer keys (Call 3 key: In *Music Monthly* · It's ten months old · £300 · At 4.30 · Al Dossari).

**Lesson 4: I've just won first prize!** (CB p.49; WB pp.52–53; Track 41; G&F pp.99–101). Feelings dialogues. Track 41 printed — **agrees with the Course Book**: Omar: "You look angry." Maher: "I am angry." … "Some careless person has just crashed into my car." Omar: "That's so annoying!" The notes then give the model answers for the six practice dialogues (sad — grandfather has just died; worried — exam tomorrow; bored — surfing the net all day; tired — working in uncle's shop; happy — just won first prize in a painting competition; hot — playing tennis all morning; plus excited — won an online competition; nervous — lost the mobile phone my brother lent me). WB A key (so/such joining) and WB B/C present perfect simple and continuous keys are given in full. Book p.95 (PDF p.94) is a blank page before Unit 5.

## Unit 5 — Food and Drink (notes book pp.96–117)

Overview strands: Reading and writing — completing an essay about eating habits, choosing a headline and matching missing questions in an interview, writing an essay about one's own eating habits, personal answers about imagined situations, quiz questions/dialogues/summaries, emails of thanks and complaint. Listening and speaking — listening for and writing advice, labelling diagrams, pronunciation, food preferences, things one has to/had to do, drawing conclusions, asking for and giving advice and opinions, imagined situations, past and present wishes and regrets. Structure and language — can't/mustn't/shouldn't for prohibition; must/have to for obligation and necessity; second conditional; I wish/If only for present and future wishes and regrets; should/had better/If I were you, I'd … and imperatives for advice. Vocabulary — food and drink words, cooking utensils and ways food is cooked; container words and collocations; adjectives to describe food; health and fitness words; compound nouns. Key words page (book p.97) agrees with the Course Book's Unit 5 list.

**Lesson 1: Food Preferences** (CB p.52; WB pp.54–56; Tracks 42–43). A answers (food items in the pictures, left to right): melons, grapes, asparagus, peppers, cabbage, celery, tomatoes, apples and bananas · fish, carrots, milk, cheese, yogurt · cheeseburger and cola · rice, naan bread, poppadums. Track 42 (*Speak Out* with Rob Marsden on local radio — five teenagers on fast food) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**: Latifa (you don't have to pay very much — cheap) · Adam (it's always the same — boring) · Hanaa (great if you're in a hurry — quick) · Nasseem (eats it two or three times a week; fish and chips and kebabs — delicious) · Ross (very bad for you, too much oil and fat — unhealthy). Track 43 (listen and repeat exchanges) printed in full. D **sample answer kept** (Indian food — "I like meet cooked i a spicy sauce" as printed in the sample, with its spelling slips). WB keys include the big food table (meat/fruit/vegetables/drinks/meal), container phrases (a bottle of lemonade · a bowl of soup · a slice of bread · a tin of tomato paste · a cup of coffee · a packet of sweets · a spoonful of sugar), the essay completion key (energy · weight · meal · bowl · glass · egg · slice · oil · delicious · cup · onion · flavour · fruit · carton · course · vegetables · chilli · salt · grilled · lemonade), the essay plan (1 breakfast · 2 lunch · 3 supper, present simple), the **sample essay "My Favourite Food" kept in the notes** (about 150 words; bananas before college, burgers with friends, grilled chicken and boiled potatoes at home, yoghurt and honey), and WB F collocations (chop onions · light a fire · do the washing-up · mix butter, sugar and flour · beat eggs · grill meat · make a cake · lay the table · boil some water).

**Lesson 2: Healthy Habits** (CB p.53; WB pp.56–58; Track 44; G&F pp.102–103). Track 44 (Layla the nutritionist puts Rashid on a diet) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** ("This is ridiculous, Layla. I can't eat burgers. I mustn't eat chips. I must avoid fried food… You should drink eight glasses of water a day… you shouldn't go to bed late"; eight hours' sleep a night). Completion key: wrong · food · chips · grilled · boiled · vegetables · lemonade · glasses. Rule key: "Must has the strongest meaning. Must/mustn't is stronger than should/shouldn't"; *can* also means "be able to or know how to". WB keys given in full (can/must/mustn't sentences; school rules — 1 must · 2 mustn't · 3 can · 4 mustn't · 5 can · 6 mustn't · 7 mustn't · 8 must · 9 can · 10 must; matching 1b 2l 3d 4k 5a 6h 7g 8f 9c 10i 11j 12e).

**Lesson 3: Expressing Necessity** (CB p.54; WB pp.59–61; G&F p.103). have to lesson. A key: 1 c and g (possibly e, h) · 2 e and h (possibly c) · 3 a and d (possibly c) · 4 b and f (possibly d). B key: 1 – 4 (the flight attendant) · 2 – 1 (the weightlifter) · 3 – 2 (the basketball player) · 4 – 3 (the teacher). Rule answers: negative of have to = don't have to · negative of has to = doesn't have to · past tense of have to = had to · negative of had to = didn't have to. WB A key (the letter completions, have to/don't have to) and WB E key (mustn't or not have to: mustn't · doesn't have to · don't have to · mustn't · don't have to · didn't have to · mustn't · don't have to) given in full. WB B: Ben's job — get up early/work late, wear a uniform, clean the pots and pans, lay/clear the tables, stay after work to wait for his aunt and uncle.

**Lesson 4: You'd better wash it first** (CB p.55; WB pp.62–64; Tracks 45–47; G&F p.103). Track 45 (Mariam and her brother Khaled cooking — too much salt in the stew) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** ("You'd better add a tin of tomatoes. That'll make it less salty… You'd better not. It'll make the stew go very white."). B key: 1B 2D 3A 4C 5E 6F. Track 46 (the tasteless-soup exchange, said twice with parsley and lemon juice, then garlic) printed. Track 47 (six kitchen problem-and-advice scenes) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** (Mariam cuts her finger on the cheese grater — "You'd better wash it and put a plaster on it"; Peter crying over chopping onions; lamb kebabs and the missing matches — "You'd better use this lighter"; Majida and the washing-up gloves; the chocolate cake and the electric mixer; Nadia burns her finger on the teapot — "You'd better put it in cold water for a few minutes… You'd better not. You might burn your finger again."). Its answer key is given in full.

**Lesson 5: If I were in your shoes …** (CB pp.56–57; WB pp.64–67; G&F pp.103–104). The "If …" magazine article lesson (Camilla of Brighton; Aziz of Sabha). A key: the interviewer's questions go in the sequence 2, 3, 1, 6, 5, 7, 4. Rule key: If + past tense, would/could + infinitive. WB keys given in full, including the honesty quiz questions (If the waiter forgot to give you your bill at a café, would you tell him? · If you found 2 million Libyan dinars in the street… · If a very rich friend lent you 5,000 Libyan dinars and forgot about it…) with sample answers, and the found-wallet role-play sample dialogue (ending "Maybe we could give the money to charity").

**Lesson 6: If I Were You, I Wouldn't Worry** (CB p.58; WB pp.68–69; Track 48; G&F p.104). Track 48 (Layla asks Suha for advice about spots) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** ("If I were you, I'd wash my face three times a day… And you should stop eating sugar and sweets… I'm not sure about that. I tried it, but it didn't work."). B key: 1g 2a 3b 4e 5c 6f 7h 8d. The notes explain *I were* as a subjunctive form, "preferred to I was (It is a form of subjunctive, which is not common in the English language.)"

**Lesson 7: I wish I could fly** (CB p.59; WB pp.69–72; Tracks 49–51; G&F p.104). Track 49 (Huda's wishes) printed — **numbered Track 49 in this book**: "I can't go with you. I really wish I could. I have to do my Maths homework. If only I didn't have to do it… I wish I knew the answer to this Maths problem. I wish I didn't have to study Maths at school. If only I was good with numbers." Rule key: We use *I wish* or *If only* + the past simple tense (I wish I had a new laptop · If only I was/were better at sport · I wish I was/were good at maths · If only my parents would let me stay up late sometimes · I wish I could go horse-riding at the weekend). Track 50 (twelve wishes to match to six pictures) printed in full; picture key A: 2, 5 · B: 7, 12 · C: 4, 9 · D: 6, 8 · E: 1, 10 · F: 3, 11; WB C key (present/future): 1 Picture E – future · 2 Picture F – present · 3 Picture D – present · 4 Picture A – future · 5 Picture B – present · 6 Picture C – future. WB D key (past perfect regrets): If only we had walked instead of coming by car · I wish I hadn't left my mobile phone at home · If only I had played more carefully · If only I had worked harder for this exam · I wish I hadn't eaten so much chocolate. Track 51 (the regrets survey talk) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**: a survey of a hundred British men and women aged fifty, each allowed to name one regret — family life 30% · careers or working life 15% · health 10% · **forty-five per cent had no regrets at all**.

**Lesson 8: If only I'd listened** (CB pp.60–61; WB pp.73–77; G&F p.104). DANA'S STORY lesson. A key begins 1c 2b (full key in the notes). Rule key: To express regret, use *I wish* or *If only* + the past perfect tense (I wish I had listened to my mother · I wish I hadn't been so selfish · If only I had taken my brother with me). WB B possible answers settle the story's facts: 1 **He pulls Layla's long hair and he wants the girls to play football with him when they want to talk to each other** (why Faisal annoys the girls) · 2 Dana told her mother that there wouldn't be space in the car for Faisal, but she didn't know if this was true · 3 Dana's mother phoned her · 4 He had gone out on his bike and a car had hit him · 5 She felt extremely sorry · 6 She wished she hadn't been so selfish and had taken Faisal to the picnic · 7 *It* in line 4 refers to Layla's family; *Him* in line 29 refers to Faisal, Dana's little brother. **Sample summary kept in the notes** (Faisal was unconscious; Layla's family drove Dana to the hospital; Faisal woke up on the fourth day and came home a few days later — he was fine). The email-of-complaint exercises (Mike's complaint; WB E order key 1e 2a 3d 4c 5b; WB F formal-email key 1 b · 2 a · 3 d · 4 c) lead to WB G: **sample formal email of complaint kept in full** — Thomas Eggers, 23, Cardiff Road, Southampton S014 2HB, Thursday 18th September, 2018, to Mr Mathews: the central heating isn't working, a crack in the bathroom window reported in May, the lift out of order five or six times since the start of the year, closing "Yours sincerely". **Round up** closes the unit.

## Unit 6 — The Media (notes book pp.118–137)

Overview strands: Reading and writing — choosing the best headlines, comparing a film review with a recorded trailer, reading an interview for detail and adding questions, starting a class magazine, formal and informal language, reporting interview results, writing headlines and a newspaper article, writing for a class magazine, punctuation. Listening and speaking — identifying types of programme, listening for gist/detail and taking notes, polite and impolite ways of interrupting, intonation in Yes/No and Wh- questions, discussing TV preferences, interviewing two people about the same event and reporting findings, planning a class (magazine), drawing conclusions. Structure and language — reporting dialogue; revision of Wh- and Yes/No questions; revision of countable and non-countable nouns; adverbs/adverbial phrases expressing result and reason. Vocabulary — types of TV programme and film; media words; formal and informal expressions; language for expressing different functions. Key words page (book p.119) agrees with the Course Book's Unit 6 list.

**Lesson 1: What's on?** (CB p.66; WB p.78; Track 52). A key: a8 b2 c9 d4 e1 f7. The ten programme types are defined in the notes (animation — cartoons drawn by people or by computers; education; film/movie — often more than 90 minutes; music; news; sport; weather forecast; plus the Course Book's chat show, soap opera and lifestyle examples). Track 52 (six TV programmes) printed in full — weather forecast (temperatures around 18 or 19 degrees) · chat show (host Alan with Rose Johnson, who has just finished a film with Tom Marconi) · animation (the Cat and the fish) · news (Jeremy with Fiona Bright in Glasgow — this morning's train crash in Scotland; ambulances have taken forty-one people to hospital; no one has been killed) · lifestyle (Hotel Splendid on Phuket, Thailand — £579 with British Airways) · education (spiders and their webs; St Kitts in the Caribbean). Type key: 2 Chat show · 3 Animation · 4 News · 5 Lifestyle · 6 Education (1, the weather forecast, is the worked example).

**Lesson 2: The Language of the News** (CB p.67; WB pp.79–80; Track 53). The Manchester office-fire article lesson (by Dan Williams). B key: the best headline is "Fire destroys office building" (the first headline focuses on an unimportant detail; the third and fourth are inaccurate). Track 53 (a witness's spoken account of the same fire) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book article's facts**: the fire started on the ground floor of number 218 Queen Street at 10.10 a.m. and got up to the fourth floor; everyone was got out of number 218 and the buildings on either side; a loud bang; by 11 o'clock the fire seemed to be out but the inside of the building had been completely destroyed; ambulances took five people to hospital, but they were OK. Completion key and the informal→formal word table are given in full (got up to → reached · in → within · got here → arrived · got out of → evacuate(d) · bang → explosion · told → instructed · get → move · put → placed · cars → vehicles · stopped → prevented · going into → entering · to be out → was brought under control · inside → interior · they were OK → their condition was not serious). WB C key: 1d 2f 3e 4a 5l 6i 7c 8h 9j 10k 11g 12b. WB D rewrite key given in full.

**Lesson 3: You said you were happy** (CB p.68; WB pp.81–84; Track 54; G&F pp.104–105). Reported speech, built on the chat show with host Alan Jones and novelist William Lee (*Memories*, First Book Award). Warm-up answers: William is 22; he said he was very happy last year, on the same chat show, because he had just had a first novel published; his novel has now won the First Book Award. A key (reported speech in the conversation): "You told me last year that you didn't even expect *Memories* to be a great success" · "…you said that your next book would be better" · "…last year you said that writing was a very lonely occupation" · "You told me that you weren't sure that you'd continue." Track 54 (William's first interview, his own words) printed: "I still live at home with my parents · I lived in China until I was fifteen · I've always liked writing stories. I'm no good at anything else · I'm going to relax in Spain for a month · After that, I'll get on with my next novel · It'll probably be set in China · I can't spell, so I have to use 'spell check' a lot · I write in English, but I think in Mandarin" — with the reported-speech key (He said he had lived in China until he was fifteen … He said he wrote in English, but he thought in Mandarin). WB D introduces footballer **Younis Mahmood** (matching key a3 b2 c1 e4, sentences 5 and 6 in any order; d "He said that he had been looking for a new team since January"; f "He said that he had scored a lot of goals last year"), with the book's own note that "last year" would strictly become "the previous year" but the report is written immediately, so it is not necessary to point this out. WB F settles a new pair: **Tariq, 17, and his sister Layla, 15**, on holiday with their parents to **Sabratha** in Libya — the reported-speech key is given in full (Tariq said it had been a boring holiday, nothing to do; Layla said it had been fantastic, lots to see and do; Tariq had missed an international football match on TV, Layla had taken hundreds of photos of Ishtar Gate and the temples — as printed; Tariq had just played on his phone and hadn't spoken to anyone; Layla would definitely go to Sabratha again). **Model interview report kept in the notes** (Layla and Mahmoud on the big football match between their colleges on Friday — one goal each, but Mahmoud's college scored two goals, so they won).

**Lesson 4: Different Opinions** (CB p.69; WB p.85; Tracks 55–56). *The Spanish Captain* lesson. The poster notes name the lead actors **Joe McGregor and Emily Knightly** (as printed in the lesson notes), the director **Pedro Garcia**, the music by **David Williams**, filming on location in Morocco, Spain and England, and A Lion Films Production; quotes from The Daily News and a film programme host, James Woss ("a beautiful story", "fantastic special effects"). Track 55 (the trailer) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**, and names the captain in full: "Joe McGregor is brilliant as **Captain Diego Santos**. **Emma Knightly** is unforgettable as his lovely young bride… The year is 1588 and the two countries are at war… The king of Spain tells him to go to war. His heart tells him to stay at home… You will never forget *The Spanish Captain*." **Name slip inside this book:** the lesson notes call the actress **Emily** Knightly; the trailer script and the Workbook table call her **Emma** Knightly. Track 56 is marked "[Track 56 is the same as Track 55.]" Trailer-vs-review table key: Joe McGregor's acting — trailer "brilliant" / review "he looks good; it's difficult to believe he's a sea captain" · Emma Knightly's acting — "unforgettable" / "not bad; we only see her for 5 minutes" · the story — "beautiful" / "rather ridiculous" · the special effects — "fantastic" / "extremely good; went on for too long" · the music — "wonderful" / "wonderful; already bought the CD". Reviewer Sarah Osborne agrees with the trailer about the music and the special effects (though they went on too long and got boring); she disagrees about the story and the quality of the acting. (A bullet in the lesson notes — "Write *brilliant* and *unforgettable* on the board…" — is printed twice in a row, a duplication in this book as printed.) **Sample film review kept in the notes** (four stars out of five for a historical film about the Roman Empire with Gregory Arndale and Charlotte Eastwood; Emperor Commodus; over two hours long).

**Lesson 5: A Famous Libyan Artist** (CB pp.70–71; WB pp.86–87; Track 57). The ALI OMAR ERMES profile lesson (by Eman Ahmed). A T/F key: 1 false · 2 true · 3 true · 4 false · 5 true · 6 true · 7 false · 8 true · 9 false · 10 true · 11 true · 12 false. Sample follow-up questions with his answers are kept (biggest painting — "I once did a painting that was 3 metres high!"; first painting at about three — "It wasn't a very good painting, but I still have it, because my mother kept it"), and WB A gives Eman's nine actual questions (Do you have/Have you got any brothers or sisters? · Do your family like painting, too? · Which is your best painting? · When did you first sell a painting? · What other painters do you admire? · Where do you get your ideas? · Do you use photographs for your work? · Have you been using the Internet to sell paintings? · Which countries have you been to?). Track 57 (Aunt Salwa phones Fawzi, whom she has not seen for five years) printed: How tall are you? · Which year are you in at school? · What are you studying? · Do you still play tennis? · Did you receive a birthday present from me? · Have you learnt to drive yet? — with the reported forms key (She asked me how tall I was … She asked me if I had learnt to drive).

**Lesson 6: Headlines** (CB pp.72–73; WB pp.88–90). Headline key: A 5 BOY BITES SNAKE · B 8 Opportunity for young golf players · C 3 5,000 litres of petrol in river: Fish die · D 4 TOP PRICE FOR TOP CAMEL — matching the Course Book's four articles (Max Paton and the snake near Cape Town; the Gulf golf tour; the Thames petrol tanker at Richmond, 10,000 litres in the article against the headline's 5,000 as printed; the racing camel sold for $150,000 in the UAE, owner Omar Abdel Hamid). WB A possible headlines: "Venezuelan Road Bridge Collapses – No one killed or injured" · "Monkeys Steal Mobiles!" **Sample article kept in full**: "ANIMALS ESCAPE FROM ZOO" (two snakes escaped from San Diego Zoo; zookeeper Steven Diaz; the two pythons, called Sam and Dave; pythons move very slowly, one mile per hour). WB D gives the Max Paton paragraph correctly punctuated (Mrs Betty Paton; "I was so terrified," she said; "It was a poisonous snake, but Max had almost bitten the head off"). WB E/F/G punctuation and capital-letter keys given in full (email address fareed.seeham@hotmail.com; the Eshtar Hotel in Al Sa'adon Street; basketball in Tobruk).

**Lesson 7: At the Museum** (CB p.74; WB pp.91–92; Track 58). Track 58 (Mrs **Al-Hamza**, guide at the National Museum of Tripoli, briefing Waleed's school group) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** (don't walk away from the group; you're allowed to talk, but not loudly; you must fill in three worksheets as we go round, not at the end of the tour; eating and drinking not allowed; you can take photos, but not with flash; "I have your tickets, so you don't need to stop at the ticket office"). Tick key: sentences 1, 4, 6, 8, 9 and 10 should be ticked. The notes teach *tell* for reporting a command and *ask* for reporting a request; WB B countable/uncountable key given in full (glass U/C, times C, time U, exercise U, exercises C, paper U/C, hair C/U, coffee U).

**Lesson 8: Magazine Start Up** (CB p.75; WB p.93). The TOP CLASS magazine lesson. A key: the first article is a report about a new building for a school; the second is an interview with a student about her holiday. The committee of three (editor / sub-editor / designer) is chosen by class vote on slips of paper; the magazine title and how often it appears (once or twice per semester) are also class decisions. Articles run 100–150 words. **Sample article kept in full**: "Interview with teacher Mrs. Mahmudi" (teaching English for ten years; "I enjoy helping my students to be more confident"; favourite food to cook is lamb stew). **Round up** closes the unit.

## Unit 7 — Leisure (notes book pp.138–161)

Overview strands: Reading and writing — expressing different emotions, interviewing someone for a job, reading for specific information, completing missing information, matching information to pictures, working out vocabulary from context, completing a form with festival information, distinguishing facts and opinions in newspaper articles, completing application forms, adding details to an outline story, writing a description of a favourite TV show, designing an interview form, adding opinions to short articles, writing dialogues and a story. Listening and speaking — completing short spoken descriptions, listening to verify and correct information on a form, identifying emotions and intonation, expressing concern/sympathy/apology/explanation, making up dialogues, reading aloud following a model, role-playing a travel agent and customer, describing a game. Structure and language — passive forms; defining relative clauses; prefixes and suffixes (re-, im-, in-, un-, -less, -ful); get/have something done. Key words page (book p.139) agrees with the Course Book's Unit 7 list, including its slip as printed there ("reopen (adj)").

**Lesson 1: Staying at the Ice Hotel** (CB pp.80–81; WB pp.94–95; Track 59; G&F pp.105–106). The ICE TOURS brochure lesson. A answers: 1 It is made of ice · 2 The ice melts · 3 It is reopened in December. Track 59 (Highlights of your 3-day holiday in Lapland) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** (Day 1 cold drinks in glasses made of ice, reindeer skins and a warm sleeping bag; Day 2 reindeer sled through the cold desert of Lapland, shown how to catch a reindeer with a rope, dinner in a traditional Lapland home; Day 3 dog sled to the mountains, taught to drive sled dogs, dinner at the Ice Hotel, "in the coolest restaurant in the world"). Completion key: warm · desert · catch · traditional · mountains · drive · restaurant. **Teacher-only background note kept in the filing**: "Lapland is Finland's northernmost region. It is not heavily populated – the total population is around 180,000. Lapland borders Sweden, Norway, Russia and the Baltic Sea. It's known for its huge sub-Arctic wilderness, ski resorts and natural sights, including the midnight sun and the Northern Lights. The capital of Lapland is Rovaniemi. Lapland is the home to the Sami people. The native animal of Lapland is the reindeer." Passive-verb key for both brochure texts given in full (Text 1: was opened, is built, are used, is rebuilt, is brought, is stored, is started, are opened; Text 2: will be served, will be shown, (will be) given, will be taken, will be shown, will be served, will be driven, will be taught, will be served).

**Lesson 2: Advertisements** (CB p.82; WB pp.95–97; Tracks 60–61). get/have something done. Track 60 (the four spoken advertisements) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**: Dahab Jewellers (necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings; "Every girl in Tripoli has their ears pierced here") · Jameela the hairdresser, **55519362** ("I can make your life easier!… I also specialize in hairdressing for weddings") · THE SNAP SHOP (passport photos ready in one hour; "Last year, over a million people had passport photos taken at The Snap Shop") · AL HAZEM OPTICIANS ("When did you last have your eyes tested? Did you know you should have them tested at least once a year?"). Completion key: earrings · clean · prices · easier · professional · weddings · passport · ready · people · eyes · once · appointment. Track 61 (the Benghazi Kitchens dialogue) printed: "I had it done by Benghazi Kitchens… Sure it's **55519362**" — the same number as Jameela's, as printed in both tracks. WB A order key: the sentences are in this order: 6, 2, 3, 4, 1, 5. WB E key (have/get something done questions) given in full.

**Lesson 3: A Market Where You Can Buy Spices** (CB p.83; WB pp.98–100; G&F p.106). "The Best of Libya" lesson. A key: 1A 2C 3D 4B 5E. B answers: 1 The Souq Al Mushir is full of magnificent souvenir shops · 2 Anyone who is interested in traditional things · 3 Bab Al-Hurya market · 4 Parrots, monkeys and falcons · 5 Tourists who want an adventure. Relative-clause keys given in full, including the omission rule: "When the relative pronoun *who, that* or *which* is the object of a verb, we can leave it out. When the relative pronoun *who, that* or *which* is the subject of a verb, we can't leave it out."

**Lesson 4: Fifteen Questions** (CB pp.84–85; WB pp.100–104; Track 62). The WHO WANTS TO BE A MILLIONAIRE? article lesson; the article's text is reproduced in the notes and **agrees with the Course Book throughout** (Chris Tarrant's "Is that your final answer?" and George Kurdahi's "'Jawaab nihaa'ee?'"; invented in Britain in 1998 and taken to the United States a year later; in 1999 shown six nights a week on the American channel ABC; exported to 70 other countries including Pakistan, Indonesia and Turkey; ten contestants in the 'Fastest Finger First' round; guaranteed £1,000 and £32,000; three lifelines, each usable only once; fifteen questions to win a million; in Britain, by 2005 the top prize of a million pounds had been won four times; the Arabic version first shown in November 2000 and already two contestants had won the top prize of one million Saudi riyals; the last series of the British show was watched by 18 million viewers; no time limit). WB B answers and the passive-verb list (to be told · is being described · is called · was invented · was taken · was being shown · has been exported · is allowed · is asked · must be chosen · is won · are guaranteed · is allowed · can be used · will be taken away · will be left · have been used · must be answered · has been won · was (first) shown · was watched · is built up) are given in full, with the note that the passive "is often used to describe games, rules and technical or scientific processes." **Sample TV description kept in the notes**: "Cake Boss" (invented in 2009, more than 150 episodes, Season one viewed by 2.3 million people, shown on satellite channel TLC in more than 220 countries; favourite episode "Under the Sea" — a cake that looks like an octopus, too big for the car, so they call their friend Frankie and he brings his van; the best cake on the show is a huge tiger cake with chocolate inside). Track 62 is the reading aloud of the article's first two paragraphs.

**Lesson 5: Explaining Situations in the Past** (CB p.86; WB pp.105–107; Tracks 63–64). Track 63 (Latifa is forty-five minutes late meeting Muna — a pipe burst in the kitchen; only Grandmother and Latifa at home; the plumber "took ages"; her mobile was left at her cousin's; "About time!" — "So, am I forgiven?" — "I suppose so. But you'll have to pay for lunch." — "Sure! It's a deal!") printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book**. Informal-expression key: Hi! = Hello · I couldn't help it = There was nothing else I could do/I had to do it · He took ages = He took a very long time · I guess not = I believe you had no choice · About time! = It is time you did this (learned my phone number)/You should have learned it before now · I suppose so = Yes, I do forgive you, but reluctantly/unwillingly · Sure! = Of course · It's a deal! = I agree. Track 64 (seven short speeches to match to feeling adjectives) printed in full; key: a cheerful 7 · b embarrassed 1 · c grateful 4 · d relaxed 6 · e thrilled 3 · f tired 2 · g worried 5.

**Lesson 6: Positive and Negative Vibes** (CB p.87; WB p.107; Tracks 65–68). **Track 65 (Farah and Reema) is printed here in the SHORT form — the same words as the Course Book's lesson page**: "What's the matter? / My father's in hospital. / You must be really worried. / I am. I feel very anxious. / You poor thing! / Thank you for your kind words." **The Course Book's own Transcript prints two extra exchanges after this point (Farah offers help; Reema asks for a lift to the hospital; Farah agrees) which this book does not print — the Teacher's Book follows the lesson page, so the longer version exists only in the Course Book Transcript.** Model dialogue answers for the replacement practice are given (passed my driving test — "I'm over the moon"; full marks in the maths exam — "Well done"; car stolen — "I'm absolutely furious"; fight with my best friend — "What a shame!"). Track 66 (two ways of saying the same news — the boy's varied, excited intonation against Mr Donaldson's flat intonation) printed. Track 67 (six speakers; tick the excited ones) printed; key: speakers **1, 4 and 5** are excited. Track 68 is a repeat of Track 67.

**Lesson 7: Two Magnificent Goals** (CB p.88; WB pp.108–111). Fact vs opinion, on the two England–Spain blogs. Key: **the second article contains mostly facts**; the opinions in both articles are underlined in the notes (first article: "a very interesting and, at times, exciting game", "they can be the best in the world", "Their defence was magic", "it looked impossible for a ball to get through", "a rather lucky goal", "He is always good", "he was brilliant", "In my view, the referee was wrong to give it", "will not be forgotten for a very long time"; second article: only "In general, however, it was a good performance by both teams"). The factual report as printed in the notes agrees with the Course Book: Vicente scored from fourteen metres in the forty-first minute; Rooney scored twice (52nd and 59th minute); Rooney pushed Ruben Baraja to the ground and got a yellow card — his fifth yellow card this season. WB A settles a Workbook-only tennis report: **Vidya Patel** from India, 18, beat **Carmen Sanchez** from Venezuela, 30, at the French Open Championship to reach the quarter-finals; Patel's fastest serve was 158 kph; Sanchez won the first set 6-3, Patel won the second set 7-5 and the third set 6-2; the game lasted one hour and forty-seven minutes. WB C/D vocabulary keys given in full (missing vowels: disappointed · embarrassed · furious · painless · anxious; hairdresser · jeweller · plumber · contestant · viewer · audience · guest; snow · dawn · sunset · ice. Missing first/last letters: PERFUME · SERVE · INDECISION · FORGIVE · REFEREE · ANXIETY · WEDDING · ROPE · ROUND · CONSTRUCT · IMAGINE · SIGNATURE).

**Lesson 8: Use Blue or Black Ink** (CB p.89; WB pp.112–113). Form-filling. A key (words printed on the form): ink · post · attach · FIRST NAME · MIDDLE NAME · SURNAME · DATE OF BIRTH · TITLE · GENDER · STATUS · NATIONALITY · OCCUPATION · ADDRESS · TELEPHONE · MOBILE · E-MAIL · SIGNATURE · DATE · language. WB A key (the form completed from the information given): Fawzi · Azzedine · 22 · Male · Student · Libyan · Arabic, English, French · 2 years · swimming, waterskiing, riding · swimming instructor for three months in Paris. **Round up** closes the unit. Book p.161 (PDF p.160) is a blank page before Unit 8.

## Unit 8 — Fun with English 2 (notes book pp.162–173)

Revision unit: skills, grammar and vocabulary from Units 5 to 7. Key words page (book p.163) is the short revision list of 9: dunes · film extras · limit · professionally · publish · quad biking · snowboarding · spectacular · whitewater rafting — matching the Course Book's Unit 8 Key Words list.

**Lesson 1: Giving Details** (CB p.94; WB pp.114–116; Track 69). Relative-clause definitions. Track 69 printed in full: Layla: "What do you call someone who talks to guests on a TV chat show?" Hasan: "A presenter." Layla: "Thanks." B key: 1i 2a 3c 4d 5h 6e 7b 8f 9g. WB A sample definitions kept (a necklace is something which women wear around their necks; a jeweller is someone who sells jewellery; snow is frozen rain; an island is land that is surrounded by water; a still life is a painting that has objects in it, not people; etc.).

**Lesson 2: Future Plans** (CB p.95; WB p.117; Tracks 70–71). Track 70 (Fawzi and Justin) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book** ("If I had lots of money, I'd buy a motorcycle and drive around Europe… They're looking for 80 extras in a film. It's a comedy… I'd better put it straight onto my mobile… If I were you, I'd call them right away"). Completion key: had · motorcycle · cinema · job · comedy · fun · apply · number · were. Teacher's answers: Justin's "Very funny" is ironic — he has no money and can't even afford the cinema; "pretty" in "pretty soon" is a colloquial use meaning "very soon"; extras are people used in crowd scenes in films who don't have to be actors. Track 71 (the Ahlen Productions radio advertisement) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book's Track 71**: about 80 extras for *My Crazy Desert Life*, "a forthcoming comedy"; filming all day Tuesday the fifth of July and Thursday the fourteenth of July, seven a.m. until five p.m.; extras not shorter than 1 metre 65 and between 16 and 23 years old; **90,000 Libyan dinars per day as printed**; breakfast, lunch and refreshments provided; auditions Wednesday the twenty-fifth of May at 9 a.m. at the International Hotel, Abu Nawas Street; sixteen- and seventeen-year-olds must bring a letter giving their parents' permission; information from Yusuf Mabrok or James Mill on **635770** ("six-three-five, double seven oh"). The newspaper-advertisement completion key is given in full. **Model audition paragraph kept in the notes**: "My name is Eisha and I am 18 years old. I am 1 metre 65 tall. I have long brown hair and brown eyes, and I am quite athletic…" (80–100 words).

**Lesson 3: Travels in Arabia** (CB p.96; WB pp.118–120; Track 72). The information-gap lesson: Student A reads the famous traveller's interview on Course Book p.96, Student B the different version in Workbook p.118; the two reporters compare what "he" told each of them (the printed example in the Course Book has the traveller first travelling to the south of Arabia in 1945 and reaching Salalah; the differing values live in the two versions and are deliberately not keyed here — the students fill the gaps from their own imagination and then find the differences). Track 72 (ten short phone messages to report) printed in full with its reported-speech key (Mona went to the shop and bought some oil · James said they were all going to the cinema with Faisal · Nadia's father wanted to speak to Ahmed · Tim would be at the bus station at 10 o'clock · Sally had found the keys of the office · Kamal was waiting in front of the supermarket · Salwa was going to be at her aunt's house until five o'clock · Fawzi had got two tickets for the football match · everybody must get to school early because of the trip · Ibrahim had done all the questions in Exercise 2).

**Lesson 4: Interviews** (CB p.97; WB pp.121–122; Track 73). Track 73 (Huda tells Ameena about her Olympics-volunteer interview) printed in full — **agrees with the Course Book, confirming it word for word**: "The director asked me if I had any previous experience in sports management. And he also asked me how many foreign languages I spoke. Then his assistant asked me if I enjoyed meeting new people… At the end of the interview, the director asked me if he could get a reference from my head teacher." Completion key: interview · volunteer · job · head teacher. The interviewers' actual questions key: Have you had any previous experience in sports management? · How many languages do you speak? · Do you enjoy meeting people? · Can I get a reference from your head teacher? WB A reported-questions key given in full (They asked me what computer programs were used in my current job … if I had a clean driving licence), with "a clean driving licence" explained as a licence with no offences recorded on it. Book p.173 (PDF p.172) is a blank page before Using the Readers.

## Using the Readers (book pp.174–176)

The full rationale and classroom guidance promised by Introduction §8. **Rationale:** "Readers are included as part of the Arts syllabus in *English for Libya*. They are intended to complement the course by introducing longer, more novelistic texts where the emphasis is principally on engaging with narrative. They are included so that students following the Arts course can have a broader cultural engagement with the English language, and so that they can develop skills and character traits that are characteristic of engagement with stories – for example, reflection, speculation, critical thinking, open-mindedness and empathy." The two Readers (*Robinson Crusoe* and *Treasure Island*) are taken from the **Garnet Oracle Classics** series, "a series of graded fiction texts designed to be read for pleasure in an educational context"; short chapters interspersed with questions; ideal for classroom use, equally suitable for individual, private reading. **Pre-reading activities:** recap by jumbling four or five sentences about the previous section for students to order; summary predictions (one correct, the others incorrect, placed in different parts of the room); character quotations as discussion prompts; return to the discussion at the end to see if predictions were correct. **Ways of reading together:** reading aloud (teacher or student, whole class or small groups/pairs) · dramatic readings in small groups with character roles and one student as narrator, different groups interpreting the same scene differently · character interviews, with the actors staying in character at all times · creative writing (news articles reporting the events of the story, comic strips with speech bubbles). **Independent reading:** the stories are graded so students "should be able to read fairly fluently, without gaps in their knowledge intruding regularly into their enjoyment of the text"; the Readers foster students' "sense of themselves as independent readers and, ultimately, as individual members of a worldwide community of literature." **Using the questions:** question types — Reflect · Reflect and communicate · Critical thinking · Inferring meaning · Speculating · Literature and language · Predicting · Transferring information · Understanding the story (a short series appearing several times in each Reader: comprehension and reflection questions). The questions incorporate 21st century skills, develop study skills (summarizing, cross-referencing), and have a creative aspect (filling in parts of the plot in the unwritten past or future). Historical context: glossaries of cultural references are provided with the stories; the stories can act as prompts for historical research projects, and students can express understanding by translating a story into different media. **Activities after reading:** vocabulary check (the unusual words are explained in each Reader's Glossary) · Round-up — summarize the action in speech or writing; identify the part of the story found most interesting, with a reason; predict what will happen next.

## Answers — Robinson Crusoe (book pp.177–182)

Answers to every question in the Course Book's Robinson Crusoe Reader, chapter by chapter (1 My early voyages · 2 The shipwreck · 3 The broken ship · 4 My home · 5 The earthquake · 6 The goats · 7 Around the island · 8 I have a surprise · 9 The wild people · 11 The education of Friday · 12 The return of the canoes · 13 The English boat · 14 The captain's story · 16 The end of my story — the answer sections run in the Reader's own chapter order as printed), plus the four Understanding the Story sections and the Extra activities. The open questions mostly carry model answers ("Answers may include…") or "Students' own answers"; the fixed keys are filed here. **Author section key:** Defoe timeline — Born (1660) · Fought in a rebellion against King James II (1685) · Went to prison (1703) · Selkirk rescued from island (1708) · Defoe wrote Robinson Crusoe (1718) [as printed] · Died (1731). Defoe was inspired by the true story of Alexander Selkirk, who survived alone on a remote island for four years, between 1704 and 1708; Defoe set his story in the Atlantic, rather than the Pacific Ocean where Selkirk was stranded, in 1659, 45 years before Selkirk was stranded. **Understanding the Story 1, B key:** 1 False · 2 True · 3 True · 4 False · 5 True · 6 True · 7 False · 8 False · 9 True · 10 True. **Understanding the Story 2, C key:** 1 D · 2 H · 3 F · 4 A · 5 B · 6 C · 7 E · 8 G. **Understanding the Story 3, E key (story facts):** the men on the beach had been eating people · Robinson planted trees to hide his fort · Robinson found a new dog on the ship · the first words he taught Friday were 'Yes', 'No', 'Food' and 'Water' · the cannibals had eaten two of Friday's friends · Friday was a tall man with long, black hair · Robinson had learned Arabic, Spanish and Portuguese in his travels · Friday told him it was possible to travel to the mainland. **Understanding the Story 4 keys:** C (who said it) 1 RC · 2 MF · 3 RC · 4 RC · 5 CAP · 6 CAP · 7 TS · 8 CAP · 9 WA · 10 CAP; D: Robinson positively influenced Friday's life by teaching him more languages than he previously spoke, and by helping him save his father from the cannibals. **Extra activities keys:** A 1 1632 · 2 30 September 1659 · 3 16 April 1660 · 4 19 December 1687 · 5 Sallee · 6 Xury · 7 Brazil · 8 Poll · 9 twenty-six · 10 sixteen · 11 Jamaica · 12 Will Atkins — all agreeing with the Reader text as filed from the Course Book; B T/F with corrections (incl. "False – Crusoe owned two different dogs on the island", "False – The mutineers planned to leave the captain on the island", "False – Three mutineers stayed on the island"); C (the story in order, 14 points, incl. "Crusoe was on the island for twenty-eight years, two months and nineteen days" and "Friday went to England with Crusoe. He also took the gold and silver coins, Poll the parrot, and the goatskin hat and umbrella"); D word list (axe · oars · sand · cannibal · goats · musket · telescope · earthquake · governor · slaves · fence · wreck · footprints · cave · waves); E the completed wordsearch grid is printed.

## Answers — Treasure Island (book pp.183–189)

Answers to every question in the Course Book's Treasure Island Reader, chapter by chapter (1 The old seaman · 2 Black Dog · 3 The black spot · 4 The end of the blind man · 5 The captain's papers · 6 Bristol · 7 Captain Smollett · 8 On board the ship · 9 What I heard in the barrel · 10 Ready for war · 11 Going to the island · 12 The man of the island · 13 The doctor's story · 14 Jim at the stockade · 15 The attack · 16 My sea story · 17 Pieces of eight · 18 Captain Silver · 19 The promise · 20 Searching for the treasure · 21 Away from Treasure Island), plus the four Understanding the Story sections and the Extra Activities. Fixed keys filed here. **Author section key:** Stevenson timeline — Born (1850) · Met future wife in Switzerland (1876) · Followed her to California (1879) · Married and moved back to Britain (1880) · *Treasure Island* published (1883) · Wrote *The Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde* and *Kidnapped* (1886) · Returned to America (1887) · Wrote *The Black Arrow* and *The Master of Ballantrae* · Travelled in the South Pacific and Australia · Died in Samoa (1894); best work listed as *Treasure Island, The Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde, Kidnapped, The Black Arrow, The Master of Ballantrae*. Chapter keys agree with the Reader text as filed from the Course Book throughout (e.g. Ch.3: the cause of death of the seaman was fear; the black spot was a warning that he had a certain amount of time before he would be killed; Jim and his mother took only enough money to pay for Billy Bones' food and his room at the inn. Ch.5: 'It' is the mysterious packet that Jim and his mother took; the names, dates and numbers in the captain's papers refer to the ships that Captain Flint attacked, when they were attacked, and how much money they stole from each one). **Understanding the Story 1 keys:** B (events in order) 4 Jim's father dies · 9 Jim, Dr Livesey and the squire agree to go find the treasure · 7 Jim meets the squire and the doctor and gives the doctor the packet · 1 The old seaman arrives at the inn · 5 Pew, the blind man, arrives and gives Bill the 'black spot' · 8 They open the packet, read the book and study the map · 6 Bill dies and Jim takes the packet from his pocket · 2 The old seaman asks Jim to look out for a one-legged sailor · 3 Black Dog arrives at the inn asking for Bill; C T/F with corrections (incl. "False – The seaman threw three gold coins on the floor", "False – The key was around the old seaman's arm" [as printed], "False – The packet had a treasure map in it"); D 1 sword · 2 afraid · 3 winter · 4 hang · 5 buried · 6 stick · 7 horses · 8 cove · 9 treasure · 10 attacked; E: Mr Dance is an officer · Pew is a pirate · Flint is a pirate · Livesey is a doctor. **Understanding the Story 2 keys:** B — all ten statements False, with corrections (incl. "False – A piece of eight was a Spanish gold coin", "False – Silver lost his leg on Flint's ship", "False – There were twenty-six" [men]); D 1 LJS · 2 CS · 3 DL · 4 LJS · 5 H · 6 H · 7 CS · 8 ST · 9 CS · 10 DL. **Understanding the Story 3 keys:** A 1 E · 2 G · 3 A · 4 H · 5 B · 6 C · 7 D · 8 F; B (events in order) 4 Jim met Ben Gunn · 7 Captain Smollett put a British flag on the roof · 1 Two rowing boats pulled the ship to the harbour · 5 The doctor took the small boat and found the stockade · 8 Silver arrived with a white flag · 9 The pirates attacked the stockade · 2 Jim jumped out of the boat and started running · 6 The small boat went under the water because there were too many people in it · 10 The pirates ran away · 3 Silver threw his crutch at Jack's back; C 1 Captain Smollett · 2 Long John Silver · 3 Benn Gunn [as printed] · 4 Long John Silver · 5 Squire Trelawney · 6 Jim Hawkins · 7 Long John Silver · 8 Long John Silver · 9 Captain Smollett · 10 Long John Silver. **Understanding the Story 4 keys:** B 1 True · 2 False – The *Hispaniola* pulled the small boat along all night · 3 False – Hands gave Jim instructions · 4 False – Hands screamed and fell from the mast into the sea · 5 True · 6 True · 7 False – The treasure hole was empty · 8 False – The stockade was a place of insects and sickness · 9 False – Silver escaped in Mexico · 10 False – They left some of Captain Flint's treasure behind. **Extra Activities keys:** A (the sorting table) Pirates who were on Captain Flint's ship: Blind Pew, Mr Hands, Black Dog, Tom Morgan, Long John Silver, Billy Bones, Ben Gunn · Those left alive at the end of the story: Captain Smollett, Squire Trelawney, Mr Dance, Jim Hawkins, Tom Morgan, Long John Silver, Dr Livesey, Ben Gunn, Abraham Gray · The good men on the ship: Captain Smollett, Alan, Squire Trelawney, Jim Hawkins, Mr Arrow, Hunter, Dr Livesey, Jack, Redruth · Those who did not leave England: Blind Pew, Black Dog, Billy Bones, Mrs Hawkins; B 1 1757 · 2 *The Walrus* · 3 January · 4 Kitt's Hole Cove · 5 The Spyglass Inn · 6 blue · 7 20 · 8 700,000 pounds of gold · 9 Mexico; C (15 story statements) and D (anchor · crutch · chest · masts · wounded · shoulder · flag · cove · hanged · logs · goats · hop · attacked · cabin · caves) given in full; E the completed wordsearch grid is printed. Book pp.190–191 (PDF pp.189–191) are blank.

## Back cover (PDF p.192)

"Components of English for Libya, Secondary 2" — **Course Book · Workbook · Teacher's Book · Audio** — the same four components as the Course Book's back cover; **no Test booklet named on this cover either** (the Test booklet exists only as a named component inside both Teacher's Books' Introductions). Component descriptions printed (the Teacher's Book: "contains an introduction with detailed practical advice and procedures for using the course effectively… Step-by-step procedures for individual lessons provide a solid framework for lesson preparation. Wherever possible, model answers are provided."). "For more resources, visit: www.englishforlibya.com"; printed by International Press, Lebanon; code **21220201**.

## Differences and slips found in this book

1. **Folio 13 is missing from this copy** — the page carrying the end of Introduction §7.2, all of §7.3 Reading, and the start of §7.4 Writing is absent (folio 12 ends mid-list; folio 14 opens mid-list). Recorded, not filled in.
2. **Track 13 vs Track 14 name slip (Unit 2):** Nisrin's photo is of her brother **Ali** in the Track 13 script and of her brother **Adel** in the Track 14 script on the facing page.
3. **Track 65 (Unit 7):** this book prints the short lesson-page form of the Farah/Reema dialogue, ending "Thank you for your kind words." The Course Book's Transcript prints two extra exchanges (Farah offers help; Reema asks for a lift to the hospital; Farah agrees) that this book does not have — so the longer version exists only in the Course Book Transcript.
4. **Emily vs Emma Knightly (Unit 6):** the Lesson 4 notes name the *Spanish Captain* actress **Emily** Knightly; the Track 55 trailer script and the Workbook comparison table in the same notes call her **Emma** Knightly. The trailer also gives the captain's full name, **Captain Diego Santos**, where the poster and review print only Diego.
5. One bullet in the Unit 6 Lesson 4 notes ("Write *brilliant* and *unforgettable* on the board…") is printed twice in a row.
6. The Unit 2 Lesson 7 Workbook note (on WB A Q5) says students may deduce "Ammar might have lost his mobile", where the dialogue's deduction concerns David's phone; as printed.
7. Track numbering checked against the Course Book all through: **every script in this book agrees with the Course Book's printed Transcript except Track 65 above** — no name splits of the G8/G10 kind (no Rashid/Waleed, no Wijdan/Nadia, no Mona/Sarah split) anywhere in Secondary 2 Literary. Audio runs to Track 73 in both books. Marked repeats agree in both books: T7=T6 · T22=T21 · T38/39/40 = the calls from T37 · T56=T55 · T68=T67.
